We did not manage to improve the accuracy by choosing different values. In fact, we made it worse – the first model had a 96% accuracy, this one 95%. Use some different values to play around with this and see the effects on the accuracy.
In the next chapter, we will look at image classification with a Convnet.

Chapter 14
Using TensorFlow for
ConvNet Image Classification
ConvNets are otherwise known as convolutional neural networks or CNNS and are typically used for image classification tasks. We’re going to learn how to build one and then use it, with TensorFlow, on the MNIST dataset.
Architecture of a ConvNet
Many Facebook users will likely remember that, in the early days, they had to manually tag people in images they uploaded to the social network. Now it’s all done automatically using a convnet. These are pretty easy to understand – an image is processed as the input, and later given a label.
Typically, an image is passed as an input; it goes through a number of steps, and then the network can make a prediction about the image.
Each image is made up of arrays of pixels, having a width and a height. If the image is grayscale, it has only one channel, while color images have three – red, green, and blue – stacked on top of one another. The pixels are each given a value from 0 to 255; the lower the value, the lighter it is, and vice versa.
The Convolutional Operation
The convolutional layer is the most important part of a model, and its aim is to reduce the image size for faster weight computations and to make the generalization better.
During this part of the process, the network will retain the  important features in the image, cutting out unwanted noise, i.e., features that are meaningless and can skew the results.
Instead, convnets extract only the pixels that are relevant by using a mathematical technique known as convolution. This lets networks learn features that increase in complexity at every layer; each matrix is divided into smaller parts so the essential bits can be learned in each part.
ConvNet Components
ConvNets have four stages or components:
  • Convolution
  • Non Linearity - ReLu
  • Pooling/Sub-Sampling
  • Classification - Fully Connected Layer
Convolution
The convolution layer has one purpose – to locally extract the image features. This means the network learns relevant patterns in the image so it can recognize them anywhere in the image
Convolution is a type of multiplication done element-wise. It is easy enough to understand – a section of the image is scanned by the computer, usually a 3x3 dimension, which is then multiplied to a filter. The output is called a feature map, and this gets repeated until the entire image has been scanned. Once this is done, the image size is smaller.
So, how does it work?
In this stage, a filter is applied to a small pixel array from the image. This filter moves about the image in a 3x3 or 5x5 shape, computing the convolution. You will notice that the width and height of the output may not be the same as the input, and this is down to the border effect.
Let’s say that you have a feature map of 5x5 and a filter of 3x3. In the center is a single window that can screen a grid size of 3x3. The feature map that is output from this will be two tiles smaller, a dimension of 3x3.
If you want the same input and output dimensions, padding needs to be added. This means that you add the correct number of columns and rows on every side of your matrix, ensuring the convolution fits all of the input tiles.
When the network is defined, there are three parameters that control the convolved features:
  • Depth :  defines how many filters should be applied in the convolution
  • Stride : defines the jump number between two sliced. If you have a stride of 1, the windows move one pixel; the higher the stride, the smaller the feature map
  • Zero-Padding : padding adds rows and columns on each side of the map, making the input and the output dimensions the same.
Non Linearity – ReLu
When the convolution process is finished, an activation function is used on the output to provide non-linearity. ReLu is the typical activation function, and all negative value pixels are replaced with zero.
Max Pooling
Pooling is designed to reduce the input image dimensionality, reducing the operation’s complexity. By reducing dimensionality, there are fewer weights that need computing; thus, overfitting is prevented.
In pooling, the stride and size must be defined. The standard method is to use the feature map’s maximum value. The result is that the feature map size is significantly reduced.
Fully Connected Layers
The final stage is where the artificial neural network is built. All the neurons from each layer are connected to the next layer, and the input image number is classified using a SoftMax activation function.
Training a  CNN Using TensorFlow
Now it’s time to build a CNN using TensorFlow; we are using the MNIST dataset.
Step One – Uploading the Dataset
You can download the MNIST dataset from http://yann.lecun.com/exdb/mnist/.  Store it in your Downloads folder, and then we will use fetch_mldata(‘MNIST original’) to upload it.
Next, use train_test_split to divide the dataset into training and test sets. This is followed by using MinMaxScalar to scale the features.
import numpy as np
import tensorflow as tf
from sklearn.datasets import fetch_mldata
#Change USERNAME to your computer username
## Windows USER
mnist = fetch_mldata('C:\\Users\\USERNAME\\Downloads\\MNIST original')
## Mac User
mnist = fetch_mldata('/Users/USERNAME/Downloads/MNIST original')
print(mnist.data.shape)
print(mnist.target.shape)
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(mnist.data, mnist.target, test_size=0.2, random_state=42)
y_train  = y_train.astype(int)
y_test  = y_test.astype(int)
batch_size =len(X_train)
print(X_train.shape, y_train.shape,y_test.shape )
## rescale
from sklearn.preprocessing import MinMaxScaler
scaler = MinMaxScaler()
# Train
X_train_scaled = scaler.fit_transform(X_train.astype(np.float64))
# test
X_test_scaled = scaler.fit_transform(X_test.astype(np.float64))
feature_columns = [tf.feature_column.numeric_column('x', shape=X_train_scaled.shape[1:])]
X_train_scaled.shape[1:]
 
Defining the CNN
Constructing a CNN requires:
  • Convolutional Layer – n filters are applied to the feature map and, once the convolution is done a ReLu activation function is applied to provide the network with non-linearity
  • Pooling Layer – the feature map needs to be downsampled; this reduces the feature map dimensionality, improves speed, and stops overfitting. Max pooling is the typical one, dividing maps into 2x2 subregions and retaining the max values
  • Fully Connected Layers – neurons are connected to the next layer, and the label is classified per the features that result from the convolutional process; the pooling layer reduces those results.
CNN Architecture
  • Convolutional Layer : 14 filters of 5x5 are applied, extracting subregions of 5x5, with ReLu
  • Pooling Layer : a 2x2 filter is used with a stride of 2 to do the max pooling
  • Convolutional Layer : 36 filters of 5x5 are applied with ReLu
  • Pooling Layer : as above, filter of2x2 and stride of 2
  • 1,764 neurons: with a 0.4 dropout rate
  • Dense Layer: one neuron for each of the target digit classes 0 to 9 = 10 neurons
CNN's require three critical modules:
  • conv2d() . To build a 2D layer, passing as arguments  how many filters, the size of the filter kernel, the padding, and the chosen activation function.
  • max_pooling2d(). To build a 2D layer with the ma-pooling algorithm.
  • dense(). To build the dense layer containing units and hidden layers.
Now we are going to build our CNN, so let’s delve into each building block before we pull it all together with the function.
Step Two – The Input Layer
def cnn_model_fn(features, labels, mode):
    input_layer = tf.reshape(tensor = features["x"],shape =[-1, 28, 28, 1])
We need a tensor defined with the data shape and we use the tf.reshape module to do that. The initial argument is the data features, defined in the function argument.
The MNIST dataset contains monochronic images, size 28x28. Inside the shape argument, the batch size is set to -1, allowing it to take the features shape.
Step Three – The Convolutional Layer
# first Convolutional Layer
  conv1 = tf.layers.conv2d(
      inputs=input_layer,
      filters=14,
      kernel_size=[5, 5],
      padding="same",
      activation=tf.nn.ReLu)
There are 14 filters in the first layer with a 5x5 kernel size. Using the same padding ensures that the input and output tensors are the same height and width; TensorFlow does this by adding zeros to both the columns and rows. Then the ReLu function is used, resulting in an output size of [28, 28, 14].
Step Four – The Pooling Layer
Once the convolution layer is finished, the pooling layer comes in. The computation reduces the data dimensionality, using the max_pooling2d module with a 2x2 size and stride of 2. The previous layer is the input, and the size of the output should be [batch_size, 14, 14, 14].
# first Pooling Layer
pool1 = tf.layers.max_pooling2d(inputs=conv1, pool_size=[2, 2], strides=2)
Step Five – Second Round – Convolution and Pooling
There are 32 filters on the next convolution layer and the size of the output is [batch_size, 14, 14, 32]. The pooling layer is the same size as the last one and the output size is [batch_size, 14, 14, 18].
conv2 = tf.layers.conv2d(
      inputs=pool1,
      filters=36,
      kernel_size=[5, 5],
      padding="same",
      activation=tf.nn.ReLu)
pool2 = tf.layers.max_pooling2d(inputs=conv2, pool_size=[2, 2], strides=2)
Step Six – The Dense Layer
Next, the fully connected or dense layer is defined. We need the feature map to be flattened before it can be connected to the dense layer, and this is done using the reshape module, size 7*7*36.
There are 1,764 neurons in the dense layer, and a ReLu function is added too. The dropout is set as 0.3, with 30% of the weights set as 0. Dropout only happens during training. In the cnn_mode_fn model, you can find an argument mode that lets you declare whether training or evaluation is required.
pool2_flat = tf.reshape(pool2, [-1, 7 * 7 * 36])
dense = tf.layers.dense(inputs=pool2_flat, units=7 * 7 * 36, activation=tf.nn.ReLu)
dropout = tf.layers.dropout(
      inputs=dense, rate=0.3, training=mode == tf.estimator.ModeKeys.TRAIN)
Step Seven – The Logit Layer
Lastly, the logit layer is defined with the model prediction, and the shape of the output is equal to the size of the batch and to 10, the number of images.
# Logits Layer
logits = tf.layers.dense(inputs=dropout, units=10)      
A dictionary can be created with the classes and the class probability. The tf-argmax() module returns the logit layer’s highest value and the class probabilities are returned by the SoftMax function.
predictions = {                          
        # Generate predictions                          
    "classes": tf.argmax(input=logits, axis=1),                         
    "probabilities": tf.nn.softmax(logits, name="softmax_tensor")  }                
The dictionary prediction should be returned only when the mode has been set as prediction and the following code will display them:  
if mode == tf.estimator.ModeKeys.PREDICT:
    return tf.estimator.EstimatorSpec(mode=mode, predictions=predictions)
 
Next, the model loss must be computed. We already know that we can use the cross-entropy loss function, and the following code will calculate the loss:
# Calculate Loss (for both TRAIN and EVAL modes)
loss = tf.losses.sparse_softmax_cross_entropy(labels=labels, logits=logits)
Lastly, the model must be optimized to find the best weight values. The gradient descent optimizer is useful for that, with a 0.1 learning rate, to try to minimize the loss.
optimizer = tf.train.GradientDescentOptimizer(learning_rate=0.001)
train_op = optimizer.minimize(
        loss=loss,
        global_step=tf.train.get_global_step())
The CNN is finished, but we want the performance metrics displayed during evaluation. This is the accuracy of the model, and there is a module in TensorFlow called accuracy that can help us – it contains a pair of arguments, labels, and the predicted values.
eval_metric_ops = {
      "accuracy": tf.metrics.accuracy(labels=labels, predictions=predictions["classes"])}
return tf.estimator.EstimatorSpec(mode=mode, loss=loss, eval_metric_ops=eval_metric_ops)
 
That is the CNN finished, and we can now pull it all together in a function to do the training and evaluation.
def cnn_model_fn(features, labels, mode):
  """Model function for CNN."""
  # Input Layer
  input_layer = tf.reshape(features["x"], [-1, 28, 28, 1])
# Convolutional Layer
  conv1 = tf.layers.conv2d(
      inputs=input_layer,
      filters=32,
      kernel_size=[5, 5],
       padding="same",
      activation=tf.nn.ReLu)
# Pooling Layer
  pool1 = tf.layers.max_pooling2d(inputs=conv1, pool_size=[2, 2], strides=2)
# Convolutional Layer #2 and Pooling Layer
  conv2 = tf.layers.conv2d(
      inputs=pool1,
      filters=36,
      kernel_size=[5, 5],
      padding="same",
      activation=tf.nn.ReLu)
  pool2 = tf.layers.max_pooling2d(inputs=conv2, pool_size=[2, 2], strides=2)
# Dense Layer
  pool2_flat = tf.reshape(pool2, [-1, 7 * 7 * 36])
  dense = tf.layers.dense(inputs=pool2_flat, units=7 * 7 * 36, activation=tf.nn.ReLu)
  dropout = tf.layers.dropout(
      inputs=dense, rate=0.4, training=mode == tf.estimator.ModeKeys.TRAIN)
# Logits Layer
  logits = tf.layers.dense(inputs=dropout, units=10)
predictions = {
      # Generate predictions (for PREDICT and EVAL mode)
      "classes": tf.argmax(input=logits, axis=1),
      "probabilities": tf.nn.softmax(logits, name="softmax_tensor")
  }
if mode == tf.estimator.ModeKeys.PREDICT:
    return tf.estimator.EstimatorSpec(mode=mode, predictions=predictions)
# Calculate Loss
  loss = tf.losses.sparse_softmax_cross_entropy(labels=labels, logits=logits)
# Configure the Training Op (for TRAIN mode)
  if mode == tf.estimator.ModeKeys.TRAIN:
    optimizer = tf.train.GradientDescentOptimizer(learning_rate=0.001)
    train_op = optimizer.minimize(
        loss=loss,
        global_step=tf.train.get_global_step())
    return tf.estimator.EstimatorSpec(mode=mode, loss=loss, train_op=train_op)
# Add evaluation metrics Evaluation mode
  eval_metric_ops = {
      "accuracy": tf.metrics.accuracy(
          labels=labels, predictions=predictions["classes"])}
  return tf.estimator.EstimatorSpec(
      mode=mode, loss=loss, eval_metric_ops=eval_metric_ops)
  
Next, we use the CNN model to define the estimator:
# Create the Estimator
mnist_classifier = tf.estimator.Estimator(
    model_fn=cnn_model_fn, model_dir="train/mnist_convnet_model")
CNNs can take multiple iterations to train them so we need a Logging hook; this stores the SoftMax layer values after each set of 50 iterations.
# Set up logging for predictions
tensors_to_log = {"probabilities": "softmax_tensor"}
logging_hook = tf.train.LoggingTensorHook(tensors=tensors_to_log, every_n_iter=50)
The model can now be estimated. The batch size is set to 100, and the data is shuffled. The training steps are set to 16.000; now you need some patience as this may take a while:
# Train the model
train_input_fn = tf.estimator.inputs.numpy_input_fn(
    x={"x": X_train_scaled},
    y=y_train,
    batch_size=100,
    num_epochs=None,
    shuffle=True)
mnist_classifier.train(
    input_fn=train_input_fn,
    steps=16000,
    hooks=[logging_hook])
Once the training is completed, we can evaluate the model and print out the results:
# Evaluate model and print results
eval_input_fn = tf.estimator.inputs.numpy_input_fn(
    x={"x": X_test_scaled},
    y=y_test,
    num_epochs=1,
    shuffle=False)
eval_results = mnist_classifier.evaluate(input_fn=eval_input_fn)
print(eval_results)

This CNN architecture will provide a 97% accuracy. Changing the architecture, playing around with batch size, and iteration number may improve accuracy.
In our final chapter, we will take a look at autoencoders with TensorFlow.

Chapter 15
Autoencoders with TensorFlow
Autoencoders are the best tools for recreating inputs. The machine will take an input – an image, for example – and it will produce something closely related. This is classed as an unlabeled input, which means the network makes use of unsupervised learning. To be more precise, the network encodes the input, focusing only on the feature that is the most critical. This is why autoencoders are great for dimensionality reduction.
Typically, autoencoders are defined with:
  • Input
  • Internal representation
  • Output
The layers are in two primary blocks, looking much like a standard neural network. There is a small difference in that the layer where the output is, and the input must be equal. The first block is called the encoder, and the original input is passed into it. The input is then compressed or reduced by the internal representation, and, in the second block, the input is reconstructed in a phase known as decoding.
The loss function is minimized, so the weights are updated, but if the output (reconstructed) is not the same as the input, the model will be penalized. Let’s say that we have a size 50x50 image – it has 250 pixels. We also have a neural network that has a single hidden layer containing 100 neurons. The feature map, where the learning is done, is smaller than the input image by two times, meaning the network must now reconstruct those 250 pixels with just a single neuron vector that equals 100.
Building a Stacked Autoencoder Using TensorFlow
We are going to build a stacked autoencoder. You will find it is similar in architecture to a standard neural network – the input is pushed into a hidden layer so its size can be reduced, before going for reconstruction. We want an output that is as near as possible to the input, and the model needs to learn how to do this while working with a lower dimension.
We are going to use the CIFAR-0 dataset. It has 60,000 images, each 32x32, and the dataset has already been divided for us, with 50,000 images in the training dataset and 10,000 for testing. There can be a maximum of ten classes:
  • Airplane
  • Automobile
  • Bird
  • Cat
  • Deer
  • Dog
  • Frog
  • Horse
  • Ship
  • Truck
The images may be downloaded from https://www.cs.toronto.edu/~kriz/cifar.html . Unzip the images. You will find a folder called for-10-batches-py; this contains five batches, each with 10,000 randomly ordered images.
Preprocessing the Images
Step One – Importing the Data
The official website tells us that we can use the following code for uploading the data. The code will go to a dictionary with the label and the data – this code is actually a function:
import numpy as np
import tensorflow as tf
import pickle
def unpickle(file):
    import pickle
    with open(file, 'rb') as fo:
        dict = pickle.load(fo, encoding='latin1')
    return dict
Step Two – Converting the Data
To keep things simple, we will convert the data to a black and white (grayscale) format, so it has just one dimension (the color images have three).
def grayscale(im):
    return im.reshape(im.shape[0], 3, 32, 32).mean(1).reshape(im.shape[0], -1)
Step Three – Appending the Batches
We have now created our functions and loaded the dataset. Now, a loop is required to append all the data into memory. The unzip file containing the data is called data_batch> and is numbered one through five – these files can be looped over to append them.
Once this is done, the color's data is converted to grayscale. The data shape is 50000 and 1024; flattening reduces the 32x32 pixels to 2014.
# Load data into memory
data, labels = [], []
## Loop over the b
for i in range(1, 6):
    filename = './cifar-10-batches-py/data_batch_' + str(i)
    open_data = unpickle(filename)
    if len(data) > 0:
        data = np.vstack((data, open_data['data']))
        labels = np.hstack((labels, open_data['labels']))
    else:
        data = open_data['data']
        labels = open_data['labels']
data = grayscale(data)
x = np.matrix(data)
y = np.array(labels)
print(x.shape)
(50000, 1024)
Step Four – Constructing the Training Set
We want our training to be fast and easy, so we are only going to use the bird images. These are the third class, and there are 5,000 images in each class. The data shape can be printed to confirm that the class has 5,000 images and 1,024 columns:
bird_i = np.where(y == 3)[0]
bird_x = x[bird_i]
print(np.shape(bird_x))
(5000, 1024)
Step Five – Constructing the Image Visualizer
Lastly, we can plot those images by constructing the function that is required for the reconstructed image to be printed.
The easiest way to do this is to use a Matplotlib library object called imshow. The data shape must be converted; it is 1,024, and it needs to be 32*32, which is an image shape:
# To plot nice figures
%matplotlib inline
import matplotlib
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
def plot_image(image, shape=[32, 32], cmap = "Greys_r"):
    plt.imshow(image.reshape(shape), cmap=cmap,interpolation="nearest")
    plt.axis("off")  
There are three arguments in the function:
  • The input, which is the image
  • The shape, which is a list and the image dimension
  • Cmap, where we choose the color map, gray by default.
Try plotting the first dataset image:
plot_image(bird_x[1], shape=[32, 32], cmap = "Greys_r")        
Step Six – Setting the Dataset Estimator
Our dataset is now ready, so we can start using TensorFlow. Before the model is built, we need to feed our network using the TensorFlow dataset estimator. This is done sing from_tensor_sliced, repeat, and batch. Here’s the full code:
dataset = tf.data.Dataset.from_tensor_slices(x).repeat().batch(batch_size)          
The placeholder, x, has this shape:
  • [None, n_inputs] – this is set as None because we have an equal size of image feed and batch size
Next, we need an iterator; if we don’t have this code, nine of the data can go into the pipeline:
iter = dataset.make_initializable_iterator() # create the iterator features = iter.get_next()            
Our pipeline is now ready, so let’s see if the initial image is as it was before. The batch size is set as 1 because only one image is being fed to the network. The data dimension can be seen using print(sess.run(features).shape) and it equals (1, 1024). This means that one image with a size of 1024 is fed. If we had the batch size set to 2, then there would be two images fed to the pipeline. It is important that you leave the batch as size 1; otherwise, an error will be thrown – the plot_image() function can take only one image at any one time.
## Parameters
n_inputs = 32 * 32
BATCH_SIZE = 1
batch_size = tf.placeholder(tf.int64)
# using placeholder
x = tf.placeholder(tf.float32, shape=[None,n_inputs])
## Dataset
dataset = tf.data.Dataset.from_tensor_slices(x).repeat().batch(batch_size)
iter = dataset.make_initializable_iterator() # create the iterator
features = iter.get_next()
## Print image
with tf.Session() as sess:
    # feed the placeholder with data
    sess.run(iter.initializer, feed_dict={x: bird_x,
                                         batch_size: BATCH_SIZE})
    print(sess.run(features).shape)
    plot_image(sess.run(features), shape=[32, 32], cmap = "Greys_r")
(1, 1024)
Building the Network
We now come to building our network, and we will be training a stacked autoencoder. There will be one input layer in the network with 1024 points – the image shape of 32*32.
In the encoder block, there is a single hidden layer at the top, containing 300 neurons. There is also a layer in the middle, containing 150 neurons, and the decoder block and encoder are symmetric. The central and hidden layer values can be changed.
Constructing an autoencoder is not much different from constructing any deep learning model. We learned earlier how a pipeline is created so the model can be fed – that means we don’t need to create the dataset again. We will build an autoencoder using four layers.
We will be using a technique called the Xavier initialization; this allows us to set the weights as equal to the input and output variance. Lastly, ReLu is used, and an L2 regularizer ensures the loss function is regularized.
Step One – Parameter Definition
This is where we define how many neurons each layer holds, what the learning rate is, and the regularizer’s hyperparameter. Before we do that, the function is partially imported. This is a better way of defining the dense layer parameters.
The following code is the definition of the autoencoder architecture values. There are two layers – 300 neurons and 150 neurons, respectively – and n_hidden_1 and n_hidden_2 are where the values are stored.
The learning rate must be defined, with the value stored in learning_rate; we also define the L2 hyperparameter, storing the value in l2_reg
from functools import partial
## Encoder
n_hidden_1 = 300
n_hidden_2 = 150  # codings
## Decoder
n_hidden_3 = n_hidden_1
n_outputs = n_inputs
learning_rate = 0.01
l2_reg = 0.0001
We use the xavier_initializer object to call Xavier initialization from the contrib estimator. We can also use l2_regularizer to add the regularizer into the same estimator
## Define Xavier initialization
xav_init =  tf.contrib.layers.xavier_initializer()
## Define L2 regularizer
l2_regularizer = tf.contrib.layers.l2_regularizer(l2_reg)
Step Two – Defining the Layers
We have now set all the dese layer parameters. Everything can be packed up into the dense_layer variable using the partial. dense_layer object; this uses ELU activation, L2 regularization, and the Xavier initialization.
## Create dense layer
dense_layer = partial(tf.layers.dense,
                         activation=tf.nn.elu,
                         kernel_initializer=xav_init,
                         kernel_regularizer=l2_regularizer)
Step Three – Defining the Architecture
You can see from the architecture that there are three layers stacked by the network, with an output layer. In the next code, the right layers are connected:
  • Layer one – the dot product is computed between the matrices features for the input and the matrices that have 300 neurons/weights.
  • ELU Activation – once the computation has been done, the output is given to the ELU
Each layer output is given to the next layer as an input, which is why it is used for computing hidden_2, etc. Each layer has the same matrices multiplication because the same activation function is used. There is no activation function applied in the final outputs layer because this is the reconstruction of the input.
## Make mat mul
hidden_1 = dense_layer(features, n_hidden_1)
hidden_2 = dense_layer(hidden_1, n_hidden_2)
hidden_3 = dense_layer(hidden_2, n_hidden_3)
outputs = dense_layer(hidden_3, n_outputs, activation=None)
Step Four – Defining the Optimization
Finally, we need our optimizer constructed. The MSE loss function is used – remember; we use the difference between the real label and the predicted output for the MSE computation. Our model is trying to reconstruct the input, so the feature is the label. As such, we want the mean sum of the squared difference between the input and the predicted output. Using TensorFlow, the loss function is coded like this
loss = tf.reduce_mean(tf.square(outputs - features))      
Next, the loss function must be optimized, with the gradients computed using the Adam optimizer – the objective is loss minimization     
## Optimize
loss = tf.reduce_mean(tf.square(outputs - features))
optimizer = tf.train.AdamOptimizer(learning_rate)
train  = optimizer.minimize(loss)
Before the model can be trained, we need to do one more thing – set the batch size to 150. That way, 150 images are sent through the pipeline on every iteration, and those iterations must be manually computed.
BATCH_SIZE = 150
### Number of batches :  length dataset / batch size
n_batches = bird_x.shape[0] // BATCH_SIZE
print(n_batches)
33
Step Five – Running our Model
Finally, the model is trained with 100 epochs. You already know how a model is trained in TensorFlow; there is a small difference here in that the data is piped before the training is run – this enables faster training. We want the loss printed at the end of 10 epochs so we can see if our model is learning; depending on your computer hardware, training can take up to five minutes
## Set params
n_epochs = 100
## Call Saver to save model and use it again during evaluation
saver = tf.train.Saver()
with tf.Session() as sess:
    sess.run(tf.global_variables_initializer())
    # initialize iterator with train data
    sess.run(iter.initializer, feed_dict={x: bird_x,
                                          batch_size: BATCH_SIZE})
    print('Training...')
    print(sess.run(features).shape)
    for epoch in range(n_epochs):       
        for iteration in range(n_batches):
            sess.run(train)
        if epoch % 10 == 0:
            loss_train = loss.eval()   # not shown
            print("\r{}".format(epoch), "Train MSE:", loss_train)
        #saver.save(sess, "./my_model_all_layers.ckpt")
    save_path = saver.save(sess, "./model.ckpt")    
    print("Model saved in path: %s" % save_path)

Step Six – Evaluating the Model
Our model is trained, now we can evaluate it. To do this, test sert needs to be imported from the /cifar-10-batches-py/ file
test_data = unpickle('./cifar-10-batches-py/test_batch')
test_x = grayscale(test_data['data'])
#test_labels = np.array(test_data['labels'])
On Windows, the code is:
test_data = unpickle(r"E:\cifar-10-batches-py\test_batch")
Try printing the image, number 13:
plot_image(test_x[13], shape=[32, 32], cmap = "Greys_r")        
Evaluating the model requires that we use this image’s pixel value to check if the same image can be reconstructed after 1024 pixels are shrunk. We need a function defined for model evaluation on different images, and the function has two arguments:
  • df – this imports the test data
  • image_number – defines which image is to be imported
We divide the function into three:
  • getting the image reshaped to the right dimension – 1024
  • the unseen image is fed to the model, and the image is encoded and decoded
  • the constructed and real image is printed
def reconstruct_image(df, image_number = 1):
     ## Part 1: Reshape image to the right dimension i.e 1, 1024
    x_test = df[image_number]
    x_test_1 = x_test.reshape((1, 32*32))
    
## Part 2: Feed model with unseen image, encode and decode the image
    with tf.Session() as sess:     
        sess.run(tf.global_variables_initializer())
        sess.run(iter.initializer, feed_dict={x: x_test_1,
                                      batch_size: 1})
    ## Part 3:  Print real and reconstructed image
      # Restore variables from disk.
        saver.restore(sess, "./model.ckpt")  
        print("Model restored.")
      # Reconstruct image
        outputs_val = outputs.eval()
        print(outputs_val.shape)
        fig = plt.figure()
      # Plot real
        ax1 = fig.add_subplot(121)
        plot_image(x_test_1, shape=[32, 32], cmap = "Greys_r")
      # Plot estimated
        ax2 = fig.add_subplot(122)
        plot_image(outputs_val, shape=[32, 32], cmap = "Greys_r")
        plt.tight_layout()
        fig = plt.gcf()
The evaluation function has been defined so now we can see the reconstructed version of image 13
reconstruct_image(df =test_x, image_number = 13)        
INFO:tensorflow:Restoring parameters from ./model.ckpt
Model restored.
(1, 1024)
