Machine Learning
Simple and Effective Tips and Tricks to Learn Machine Learning with Scikit-Learn, Keras and Tensorflow
Benjamin Smith

Introduction
There’s no denying that machine learning is the upcoming talk of the century in mankind’s journey with computers. Conventionally, computers were seen as instruments that need to be told specifically what to do. The notion of training the computer and making it learn certain patterns that allow it to make decisions on its own would be categorized as science-fiction in the previous decade. Although the struggle to create intelligent and self-functioning computers that can understand and distinguish data was already underway in the dawn of the 2nd millennium, the efforts were not as effective. This was due to the lack of computational resources and the infancy of the very field itself. Looking back, innovations in machine-learning were mostly coincidences. In a few cases, bright minds paved the way for machine-learning to prosper by pouring their genius intellect into exploring the secrets that lied in the mathematical aspect of computers.
Now, the influence of machine-learning has become undeniable. We are seeing self-driving cars implementing the concept of machine learning in unison with artificial intelligence. We are building intelligent systems that can scavenge through insurmountable data and produce results that would otherwise be impossible. We are seeing machine-learning being implemented as a core feature in all smart appliances and voice assistants. This is only the beginning of a new age. Being one of the people that pioneered this revolution in human life is something that only comes once in every lifetime. As such, the popularity of learning machine learning and being adept at it has become a very sought after goal and this book will honor this goal.

Chapter 1
Introduction to Machine Learning
Machine Learning, what was thought to be an innovation worth a hundred years of progress a few years ago, has already been achieved by mankind in the contemporary era. Optical Character Recognition (OCR) has been one of the many useful products of this innovation has provided us. The first of its uses, quite common now, appeared back in the 90s: the spam filter. Other numerous examples of ML which are working right now in every smartphone and any computer are:
  • Voice searches
  • Recommendation mechanisms
This subject stands to explore the uniqueness of this innovation. It has made us question the very basics of learning. Does saving a file in your computer with philosophical contents make it wonder about the truths of life? Will saving a copy of the Critique of pure reason make it explore the valleys of moral ethics? And the most crucial question of all, does the data saved into the computer make it a more learned entity than before? In this chapter, the primary theme will be exploring the wonderful world of Machine Learning and the intricacies involved in incorporating machine learning in the program design. Furthermore we will look at the advantages of using machine learning. We will look at some important areas and parts that are important to understand the workings of ML. For instance, the different categories of machine learning software based on how they learn i.e. unsupervised or supervised, instance-based vs. model-based, etc. Following this brief introduction, we will observe the model of a machine learning software and observe how it functions while also paying attention to the main impediments of the process, and look at the solutions of solving such problems.  We will learn how to judge and evaluate a model and the methods to fine-tune a Machine Learning system. Besides, we will also go through the important techniques and practices that essentially allow us to, not only assess and appraise a machine learning model, but we will also learn the way to improve the model effectively.
To work on Machine Learning or by using it, a data scientist should know the technical terms and jargon of the model and the terms used in ML, and this is what we will focus on in the first chapter. It will be kind of a comprehensive overview, but it will stand as a foundation stone for building on in the entire chapters after this.
What Is Machine Learning?
Programming computers so that they can learn from data that is fed to them is commonly accepted as Machine Learning. In the year 1959, Arthur Michel defined machine learning as “The field of study that gives computers the ability to learn without being explicitly programmed” (Arthur Michel, 1959).
One of the most simple and arguably widely used programs that incorporate machine learning is a ‘Spam Filter.’ This program checks the incoming emails in the user’s inbox and automatically filters out the ones that look like spam. Let’s understand this working in a more detailed manner. Machine learning programs have special algorithms that are taught to identify patterns through training. For this reason, we need to make the algorithm of the spam filter program become familiar with patterns of a typical spam mail. To do this, we will have to train the algorithm by giving it data (that corresponds to the emails that have been marked as spammed by users) and highlighting the characteristics of a standard spam mail. The data that we are providing to the program through which it will learn a specific pattern is known as the ‘training set.’ In this case, the examples of spam mail we are using to train the program will be collectively referred to as the training set. A common phrase you will come across through the upcoming chapters of this book is ‘Training Instance”. This phrase refers to a single ‘entity’ within the training set, for instance, in the data of spam email examples, each ‘example’ is also known as a ‘training instance.’
So, the main goal of this program’s algorithm is to identify those emails that are most likely spam. This will be done by leveraging the pattern the algorithm learned during training. Also, the algorithm will consider the characteristics defined by the user as well. To improve the performance of the program, we can optionally tune some hyperparameters (but that’s an advanced task). To evaluate the performance of the program, we can do so by taking a look at the performance measures (such as accuracy, precision, recall, etc.). However, we need to carefully pick out the appropriate performance measured depending on the nature of the task. We will talk in more detail about the accuracy, precision, and recall in the 3rd chapter. For now, since we are dealing with a classification task (classifying emails as ‘spam’ or ‘not spam’), the performance measure we should be using is ‘accuracy.’ From here, we can gather how well the model performance with regards to the accuracy of its predictions, in response, we will have the ability to either tune the algorithm and hyperparameters if the accuracy is unsatisfactory.
The lesson to learn from here is that downloading data onto the computer doesn’t necessarily mean that it will change the overall capabilities of the machine itself. Simply storing a philosophical book on a computer will give it the ability to debate with the user on such topics. Hence, storing data has no relation to the functionalities of a computer. We need first to design a program that can leverage this stored data and produce a useful output.
In other words, if you just download a copy of the ‘Critique of pure reason,’ the machine indeed holds more data than before. However, the computer will not be blessed with the ability to debate the contradictions of categorical imperatives out of nowhere. Thus, it will make it store more data but not make it smarter than it was before.
Reasons to Use Machine Learning
Let’s observe the programming approach traditionally taken to create a simple bot incorporating machine learning so that it can filter through spam mail:
  1. First, we need to have an idea of the structural arrangement of a standard spam email (in most cases, spam emails share many similarities). For example, a common trait among spam emails is that [there are certain distinct words and phrases that pop up in the email’s subject line. One of the most common examples of such phrases is ‘absolutely free, no credit card, limited-time offer, etc. Other than these linguistic cues, once you go through a bunch of spam emails by yourself, you will come to notice a distinct pattern of words being used in the body of the email. In this way, you can set up additional parameters that will consider these external factors as well, improving the accuracy of the program.
  2. One of the most important elements of the spam filter is the algorithm being used. This algorithm is responsible for learning and familiarizing itself with the patterns of spam. Once the algorithm notices this pattern in an email, it will simply flag it as spam automatically.
  3. Finally, we need to take the spam bot program out for a test drive. If the performance is lackluster and the spam detection functionality is not efficient enough, then we go back to the first two steps and improve it, not everybody can make a program, even if it’s simple. It incorporates machine learning extremely well so it’s okay to accept that program is lacking and work on improving it until it’s up to your standards.
A spam filter learns which words and phrases are good clues for identifying the characteristic of ‘spam’ by detecting recurring patterns of words that are only observed in those emails that have been labeled as ‘spam.’ The patterns of words and phrases observed in those emails not labeled as ‘spam’ are not considered by the program. Due to the usage of ML, the program tends to become relatively brief and easier to maintain (as compared to traditional programs) and generally far more accurate because of its self-learning processes and independent nature. When using a traditional program (let’s say a standard bot) that uses a standard detection algorithm, if the spammers start using words such as “night” instead of “9it”, then we would be forced to update the detection algorithms accordingly. This is because such traditional programs are based on a set of rules. So, if spammers notice that the target is using a traditional spam-bot program, then they can simply take advantage of the loopholes in the program’s structure and work their way around the detection algorithms. As such, you will be forced to keep updating the rules of your program every time the spammer figures out another loophole. However, if the same spam filter program were to use machine-learning, then there would be no hassle in tackling the annoying spam attacks that are updated frequently. Such a program that uses machine learning algorithms would automatically familiarize itself with the patterns and commonly used phrases and keep itself updated. All we would have to do is update the model’s training by providing it with new batches of data in which emails have been labeled as ‘spam,’ and it would learn the new patterns by itself. This approach is way more reliable and more convenient.
When we are faced with a problem that is non-traditional or has no formulated algorithm, we turn towards the Machine Learning algorithms for solutions. For example, consider speech recognition as an example. You start by writing a program that can differentiate between the sounds of one and two. The word “two” begins with a high-pitch sound (“T”), so you can code an algorithm that calculates the intensity and pitch of the observed sounds and make use of the calculated measurements to distinguish between ‘ones’ and ‘twos.’ However, this program cannot hold a candle against complex data which includes phrases from different languages and thousands of words, and every word spoken in an environment that has considerable background noise. What we do in such a case is to simply scavenge for examples of the words we are looking for in different environments so we can feed them to the algorithm for it to learn by itself and recognize the corresponding speech.
Just as how we can see the progress bar of how much data we have downloaded as compared to the total size of the data we are downloading, we can also check the machine-learning algorithms and see how much they have learned so far. However, some algorithms are inherently complex and too advanced to the point where it becomes really tricky even to carry out such an inspection. For instance, once our program responsible for filtering spam emails has been trained on enough data, we can check out the list of words. Along with that, we check specific clusters of words that it has detected and accumulated over time to see which words are the best detectors of spam emails. In this manner, we, humans, can learn from ML. This inspection will reveal unsuspected correlations or undetected new trends which can help us form better performance measures for future algorithms. One such example that uses this technique is data mining, which incorporates ML algorithms to dig into enormous sets of data to detect the patterns mentioned above. 
Examples of Machine Learning
Following is a list that demonstrates the use of machine learning in specialized programs:
  • Images of products on a production line being analyzed to classify them are called image classification, and this task is performed by using convolutional neural networks (CNNs).
  • Semantic segmentation is used to detect tumors in the brain, to do this each pixel in the image is differentiated as our goal is to detect the exact location of the tumor along with its size, this task is performed using CNNs as well.
  • Natural language processing (NLP) is used to classify news articles, more precisely known as text classification, this task is tackled by using CNNs, recurrent neural networks (RNNs) and Transformers.
  • Discussion forums are often subject to abusive content and inappropriate content. These comments are automatically picked out, and this is text classification by using the same NLP tools.
  • Very lengthy documents take up a lot of time when they are required to be sorted through. Natural Language Processing provides a tool which is known as ‘text summarization’ that automates this arduous task.
  • A personal chatbot is created by incorporating different tools such as natural language understanding (NLU) and question-answering modules. Typically it answers all the queries in a short period when the actual authorities are not around.
  • Big Corporations and Organizations can predict or gather a general idea of how well the company will perform in the coming term by using a machine learning regression model. Such a model specializes in generating predictions about values. Moreover, the model can be made unique considering the corporation or organization developing it by utilizing any of the regression types (for example, linear, polynomial, or even SVM regression).
  • Speech recognition is used to make the applications react to voice commands. In order for the applications to work, audio samples are processed, and since such a type of data features lengthy and complex sequences, RNNs, CNNs, or Transformers are used to process them.
  • Anomaly detection is used to alert the user upon any credit card fraudulence.
  • Clients are separated into segments based on their purchases so that we can formulate a separate marketing strategy for each category of clients. This segmentation on the basis of different client requirements is called ‘clustering.’
  • Data visualization is used in presenting a complex, high-dimensional dataset with clarity by an insightful diagram; it often involves dimensionality reduction techniques.
  • A program can be developed that incorporates machine learning to consider the purchase history of a client and recommend products accordingly. To create a program that can perform such a task, we will need to procure the data of the client’s purchase history along with the purchase data of similar clients and use it to train the artificial neural network. Once the model has been adequately trained, it will be capable of predicting the purchase the client will most likely make when they visit the shop the next time.
This list could go on, but the main point here is to demonstrate how broad the scope of machine learning is in the real world.
Different Types of Machine Learning
There are various types of machine learning available nowadays. The most popular types of machine learning along with their characteristics have been listed below:
  • If a machine learning model is being overlooked by an external user (i.e., the tuning of hyperparameters, cycling through different activation functions to choose the best one, etc.) and the development and training of the model on labeled data is guided by the user as well. Such a model is categorized under a supervised learning system .
  • If a machine learning model is not supervised by an external user and the data on which the model is trained on is unlabeled. Such a machine learning model is categorized as an unsupervised learning system . Similarly, if the model is trained on a dataset that is partially labeled, then it is referred to as a semi-supervised learning system . A machine learning system that is conditioned to certain responses and then learns how to analyze its surrounding environment to determine the best course of action is categorized as a reinforced learning system .
  • A machine learning system that can only be trained by feeding it complete batches of data is known as a batch learning system . On the other hand, a machine learning system that can be trained by using data instances (mini-batches) in sequences is known as an online learning system .
You can apply these criteria in any way possible, and there are no steadfast rules about exclusively applying them.
Supervised and Unsupervised Learning
Systems that incorporate machine learning can be classified into different categories based on several factors. One such factor is the type of data (labeled or unlabeled) on which the model is being trained. Another factor is if the model’s training is being closely observed and managed by a user or not. In this section, we will discuss the major categories of machine learning based on the aforementioned factors:
Supervised Learning
Just as the name suggests, in supervised machine learning, we create a training set from a dataset we choose and train the model using it. The data within the training set is labeled. Labeled data means that within a particular dataset, all of the classes or data instances are marked as to what they actually represent. For instance, in the MNIST dataset (which we will soon discuss), the entire data comprises instances of handwritten digits. If each of the data instances (handwritten digit) is labeled with the number they represent (a handwritten digit ‘3’ is explicitly labeled as ‘3’), then we say that the dataset is labeled. Supervised learning is most commonly seen in tasks that require the model to classify objects (also known as classification tasks). To keep things simple, let’s consider the example of the spam filtering program we discussed in the preceding sections. We can say that such a program incorporates the use of supervised learning. Not only do we train the model using labeled data (emails within the dataset have been categorized and marked as ‘spam’ and ‘not spam’) but the nature of the problem is essentially a ‘classification task’ as well (classifying emails as spam or not spam).
Another task for which we use supervised learning is ‘Linear Regression.’ Unlike ‘Classification’ in which the model generates predictions regarding the correct class for a data instance, in ‘Linear Regression’ the model is required to generate predictions of values. In linear regression, we basically give the model a list that contains features about something and then train the model to predict a value according to the aforementioned features. For example, we can provide the model with a list of features such as total mileage, fuel capacity, engine type, and then ask the model to predict the value of the car based on these features. However, the process will still include training the model by using a training set that consists of both predictors (the list of features) and labels (the prices of the cars). We will talk about Linear regression more in the upcoming chapter.
Whenever we talk in the context of Machine Learning, it is important to know the difference between an attribute and a feature. An attribute basically refers to the type assigned to a data (for instance, ‘mileage’ is data whose type is different from ‘engine type’ or even ‘fuel capacity’). In contrast, when any value accompanies an attribute, then it is referred to as a ‘feature’ (for instance, if we say that ‘fuel capacity’ = 30L, then it is a feature).
Algorithms are one of the most important components within a machine learning system. Each task requires a specific type of algorithm to be used for optimal performance, hence knowing about the different algorithms used in models is also important.
Following is a list of some of the most commonly used algorithms used in supervised learning :
•        k-Nearest Neighbors
•        Linear Regression
•        Logistic Regression
•        Support Vector Machines (SVMs)
•        Decision Trees and Random Forests
•        Neural networks
Unsupervised Learning
Unsupervised learning is basically the opposite of supervised learning. Unlike its counterpart, in unsupervised learning, the data on which the model is being trained is unlabeled. This means that the data instances are not marked as to which class do each of them belongs to. Moreover, the model is not under the supervision of a user (hence the name, unsupervised learning). The following is a list of the most commonly used algorithms in unsupervised learning models:
• Clustering
​ — K-Means
​ — Hierarchical Cluster Analysis (HCA)
• Anomaly detection and novelty detection
​ — One-class SVM
​ — Isolation Forest
• Visualization and dimensionality reduction
​ — Principal Component Analysis (PCA)
​ — Locally Linear Embedding (LLE)
​ — t-Distributed Stochastic Neighbor Embedding (t-SNE)
• Association rule learning
​ — Apriori
​ — Eclat
Let’s understand unsupervised learning with the help of an example. Let’s consider that we are running a blog that is quite popular, so much so that it generates consistent traffic of visitors. Since user traffic is always good for blogs, we can improve the reachability of our blog by identifying what our visitors search for before visiting this website. To do this, we will have to use some sort of algorithm that can identify the visitors and then classify them into categories based on how similar each visitor’s search query is. Such a task generally comes under the domain of unsupervised learning. Notice that if we were to create a model and train it on this anonymously collected data, it wouldn’t have labels and we won’t necessarily be supervising it either. Let’s say we do create such a model. To perform the required classification, we would need to use a ‘Clustering algorithm.’ When we implement this algorithm, we will not need to tell the algorithm that a certain visitor (data instance) belongs to this particular group (data class). The algorithm will learn the similarities in the visitor’s search queries and then categorize them accordingly all by itself. For instance, let’s say that through some clever trick, the algorithm concludes that about 70% of the visitors that come to the blog are women while the rest are men. Based on the time these visitors access the blog (recorded in the website’s traffic history), the algorithm concludes that the majority of the viewers tend to visit the blog around evening during the weekdays while the rest tend to access the sci-fi portion of the blog only on weekends. Depending on the clustering algorithm used, the model can even incorporate this detail into the classification as well. For example, by using the HCA (Hierarchical Cluster Analysis) algorithm, the model will be capable of dividing the categories into sub-categories as well (if there’s enough information to allow it). In this way, we can identify the topics we need to discuss which will attract a large portion of our audience and boost the user traffic.
Before we move on to the next topic, let’s briefly discuss Visualization and Dimensionality reduction algorithms.
Visualization Algorithm
Just as the name suggests, a visualization algorithm is generally concerned with graphically representing complex data in either two-dimensional plots or three-dimensional plots. For example, the Principle Component Analysis (PCA) algorithm is commonly used in unsupervised learning to represent unlabeled and complex data visually. Moreover, visualization algorithms prioritize the preservation of the data structure when it is visualized. The reason behind this is to allow for a much easier analysis of the visualized data and most importantly, enable the user to look for anomalies in the data patterns.
Dimensionality Reduction Algorithms
Just as how the PCA algorithm simplifies complex data, similarly, the main purpose of dimensionality reduction algorithms is to reduce the complexity of input data while prioritizing the preservation of the inherent information (keeping information loss to a   minimum). Generally, the way dimensionality algorithms simplify data is by merging the features that are somewhat related to each other together. In this way, the number of features is reduced. This means, the number of dimensions in the data is reduced.
Let’s take a look at a simple example that we discussed in the section of ‘Linear Regression’ as well. Let’s consider the following list of features according to which we need to predict the price of the car; these features will be:
  • Fuel Capacity
  • Mileage
  • Period of use
  • Date of Manufacturing
  • The model’s popularity, etc.
From these five features, we can see that a car’s mileage and the period of time for which it has been used prior to being sold are similar features. So if we apply a dimensionality reduction algorithm here, the ‘mileage’ and ‘period of use’ features will be merged into a single feature, hence reducing the original number of features. This process of feature merging is commonly referred to as ‘Feature extraction.’
Here's a cool tip. Consider that you created a model for classification or a linear regression task. Hence, it’s a given that you will be using supervised learning algorithms. However, if you apply the dimensionality reduction algorithm on the training set before you train the supervised learning algorithms, then you will improve upon the following aspects:
  • The training speed will be improved.
  • The memory and storage requirements of the dataset will be reduced.
  • The overall performance of the model will also be improved.
Semi-Supervised Learning
Consider that you downloaded a dataset from any repository of your choice so that you can train your machine learning model. Unlike popular datasets (like the MNIST dataset), not many are completely labeled, especially ones that are particularly large. Even if you scavenge through multiple repositories, you will only find a dataset that is partially labeled at best. Moreover, labeling data by yourself takes up too much time which you could spend tuning the model. Even if you hire a company or an organization to label it, the cost would be very high as well. In such cases where we are working with a partially labeled dataset, we can use specific algorithms. Such algorithms handle the labeled part of such datasets. This approach is known as semi-supervised learning .
We can even see semi-supervised machine learning being applied in online services as well. Take Google Photos for instance. If we upload a family album on their server through the cloud, this means that the server is essentially being fed unseen and unlabeled data. Through machine learning, the server can identify that a particular person in a photo is showing up in other photos of the album as well. If we label this data instance (i.e., specify the name of the person) then the server can automatically label the rest of the photos in which this person appears as well.
Reinforcement Learning
Unlike the different types of machine learnings we have discussed so far, reinforcement learning is radically different. The system using reinforcement learning is known as the ‘Agent’ and the specialty of such a system is that it is capable of computing an appropriate action by observing its surroundings. If the agent chooses the correct response, then it is given a ‘reward.’ This reward can be easily programmed or it can be improvised depending on the choice of the developer. The distinctive feature of reinforcement learning is that the system tries to identify the most optimal strategy within a confined space. This means that the machine is given a specific environment with specific stimuli; the system carefully observes it’s surrounding and then develops an optimal strategy according to which it makes decisions. The strategy formulated by the machine is known as its ‘policy.’
The best examples which showcase the full potential of reinforcement learning are robots and AI bots. Majority of the robots that have been designed incorporate reinforcement learning in order to learn how to manipulate their limbs to enable them to walk. Similarly, AlphaGo, which is an AI bot designed to play the board game ‘Go.’ The bot incorporated reinforcement learning and was trained by allowing it to analyze large amounts of professional ‘Go’ matches. Once it was done with analyzing, it played the game with itself and in the end, it beat the board game’s world champion in an official match based on the policy it formulated on its own.
Online and Batch Learning
Batch Learning
A model using batch learning does not allow data to be fed into the system in sequences or in the form of instances. The model is trained by using the entire dataset, i.e., the entire batch of data is fed to the system at once, hence the name ‘batch learning.’ Since the model is dealing with an entire dataset (for comparison, MNIST being a standard dataset consists of about 70 thousand images), the obvious consequence is that we cut back on the training speed. Since the training speed is slower than usual, batch learning is usually incorporated when want to train the model locally (offline). One drawback of batch learning is that the model will only be able to perform tasks for which it has been specifically trained for (i.e., it will only be able to perform actions that it already knows). It does not adapt or learn anymore unless it is trained again using the entire dataset; this means that if you have new data instances, then they will first have to be included in the original dataset and trained from scratch once again. Hence, batch learning is also referred to as ‘offline learning.’ However, the process of training, evaluation, and testing can be automated. So after the initialization of the model, you can simply automate these processes to be performed automatically and all you will need to do is simply load the dataset onto the model.
Online Learning
Online learning is simply the complete opposite of batch learning (also known as offline learning). In online learning, the model is provided with training data in sequences. This means that the model can be trained with separate data instances or a bunch of data instances (which are known as mini-batches). Since the quantity of training data is exponentially smaller as compared to batch learning, it is a given that the training speed of the model will be fast and produce less strain on the computational resources as well. In this way, the system can easily adapt whenever new data instances arrive.
Online learning becomes a plausible option when we are dealing with data that has a continuous flow (for instance, stock market data has an uninterrupted flow). Our tasks require the model to adapt to the changes without too much delay. Moreover, if we are using a system that lacks behind others in terms of raw power, then the online learning system is a perfect choice since it is not that resource-hungry. Another good feature of online learning is that once it has been trained on new instances of data, the user can then delete these data instances from the system, hence saving storage space as well.
In online learning, some algorithms are capable of handling large datasets as well. So if we have a dataset that does not fit our system’s main memory, then we can use online learning to train our model on this dataset. Training a model without using the system’s main memory is known as ‘out-of-core learning.’ The way online learning algorithms are capable of doing this is by simply loading mini-batches from the dataset and then training the model on this data for one epoch. Then the algorithm proceeds to load the next mini-batch of data and train the model. This process is repeated until the model has been trained on the entire dataset.
Hindering Factors For Machine Learning
When creating and developing a machine learning model, the most important contribution of the user is providing the model with the optimal algorithm as well as adequate data to train the model. However, the creation and development of a machine learning model is essentially a process of trial and error. We simply cannot get everything right when developing a new machine learning model from the ground up on our first try. There’s always something that needs to be changed, tuned, or even re-designed. In this section, we will discuss two of the major factors that hinder the progress of a machine learning model:
  • Bad data
  • Bad Algorithms
Here’s a list of some examples demonstrating the encounter of ‘bad data’ when dealing with machine-learning models.
Insufficient Quantity Of Data
In machine learning, nothing is as simple as showing it a picture of your favorite cat and then expecting the program to recognize a cat whenever it sees one. We are essentially teaching a machine that perceives the world as one-dimensional binary digits to recognize three-dimensional objects. For the computer to perform such tasks, it needs to be trained with a large amount of data. Since this is an important requirement in machine-learning, insufficient quantities of data are an example of ‘bad data’).
Non-Representative Training Data
For the machine learning model to generalize the patterns and other things it learned from the training data on unseen data (that the model has never come across), the model must be trained with data that somewhat represents the unseen data. We cannot train a model to recognize handwritten digits and then expect it to predict the price of houses in the upcoming years. If we want the model to recognize handwritten digits, then we must train with data that actually represents handwritten digits.
To further elaborate, if we want to create a model that predicts the general happiness of the population for each country, then we need to train it on data that represents the country’s status for which we expect the predictions. If the model is trained only on data that shows declining happiness and prosperity in 3rd world countries, then it won’t perform as we want to when we test it on data that requires it to predict the prosperity and happiness of developed countries. The training data does not represent the case we are testing the model on, and it is missing a lot of countries. Unless we include countries from every category (rich, moderately rich, and poor countries), the model will not generalize well because the training data is not truly representative of what we want it to work with.
Poor Quality Data
Just as how the name suggests, datasets that are lacking in quality are a prime example of ‘bad data’ that hinders the progress, development, and most importantly, the performance of the machine learning model. By poor quality, we basically mean a dataset that has:
  • Common errors
  • Outlying data instances (those instances that do not belong in any class within the dataset)
  • Low clarity (commonly referred to as ‘noise’).
  • Incomplete or Missing features
These are some of the issues that can be commonly observed in poorly compiled datasets. The leading reason through which data has the aforementioned faults is the use of inaccurate measurements when recording the data. Using data that has poor quality will make it nearly impossible for the model to learn the proper patterns. In simpler terms, using a dataset that has poor quality will only lead to the model showcasing poor performance in the result as well.
This is also one of the main reasons why data scientists allocate a huge portion of their time improving the quality of the data and refining it; otherwise the rest of their efforts will be laid to waste.
So, if you ever decide to create a machine learning model and have to look for a good dataset, you should always check it thoroughly to see if there aren’t any problems (especially the ones mentioned above). Let’s say that you notice that the dataset you have chosen has some outlying data instances. You can eliminate these instances themselves or if possible, you can fix these instances as well (whichever seems feasible at the time).
Similarly, if you notice that there are data instances that are not accompanied by all of the instances they should have (for example a bunch of data instances in which the client has not mentioned their age), then you have several options. Firstly, you can choose to neglect the missing attribute (the specific feature) entirely. Secondly, you can choose to neglect these particular data instances and eliminate them from the dataset. Thirdly, you can specify the missing information by yourself (in this case, you would calculate the average age of the customer list and guess it). Finally, we can create two separate models, where one is trained with these erroneous instances and one where these incomplete instances have been omitted to see if there’s any noticeable difference in performance.
Irrelevant Data
It doesn’t require much thought to figure out that if we use data that is completely irrelevant to the task the model is supposed to perform, then the model will absolutely fail. If we train the model on the wrong dataset, then the model will be incapable of doing anything when faced with unseen data. It will not be able to see any relevance to the patterns it learned on the training set to the structure and type of data being presented in the testing phase or even in real-life applications. Hence, it is important to make sure that even if the dataset we are using is relevant to the model’s assigned task, it does not have any insignificant or unnecessary features as well.
Overfitting The Training Data
To understand the concept of overfitting, we can relate it to ‘overgeneralizing.’ In overgeneralizing, we take an isolated observation and make a general assumption. For example, let’s consider a theoretical scenario where a person visits another city and since it’s his first time there, he asks a pedestrian for directions. Instead of responding to his request for guidance, the pedestrian scoffs and ignores him. After such an encounter, the person assumes that everybody in this city is as rude as this stranger. This is known as overgeneralization and the concept of ‘overfitting’ means the same for computers. If the user is not vigilant and experienced, the model can overfit on the training data. This means that the model will learn the patterns from the training set pretty well but when it comes to the testing set, the model will assume that the same pattern must hold true for the unseen data as well. In other words, the model fails to generalize well on unseen data even though its performance is satisfactory on the training data.
Underfitting The Data
From the name, it is obvious that underfitting is a phenomenon observed in machine learning that is the complete opposite of overfitting. When a machine learning model underfits on training data, this means that the structure of the data is just too plain for the model even to start detecting and learning any patterns. In other words, data on which the model is trained with is too unrealistic as compared to the problems that occur in the real world, in this way, the predictions generated by the model will always be inaccurate. For example, if we take the example of the happiness of the populations for different countries, the model trained on such data has a higher risk of underfitting. This is because the observations in the data do not accurately represent real life. Simply living in a rich country won’t necessarily make you happy. To reduce underfitting, we can do so by:
  • Using a model that is comparatively more capable and boasts a wider range of premises.
  • Using better and more refined features with the selected algorithm (this process is known as ‘feature engineering’).
  • Using lesser constraints with the model, i.e., scaling down the use of regularizers and using lesser hyperparameters.

Chapter 2
Perform Regression
Tasks Using Scikit-Learn
In this chapter, we will be focusing our discussion towards creating a machine learning project to perform a standard regression task by utilizing the Scikit-Learn library. It is important to note that the nature of the task is similar to what a data scientist would focus on. Hence, to keep the discussion as realistic as possible, we will approach the project with the mindset of a data scientist as well. Don’t worry; we will only explore only those aspects of the projects that refer to data science, as discussing anything more would be out of the context of this book.
In this chapter, our main focus will be to obtain a dataset that is up to the standards of the project, then analyzing the data in order to gain insights that will guide us through tuning and improving the performance of the model itself. Once we have explored the data and have obtained a satisfactory understanding of it, we will proceed to create a machine learning model and use appropriate algorithms to train it. Once the model has been trained, we will go through the standard practices to evaluate and test the model. With this, we will have a good idea of the areas in which the model is still lacking. In this way, we can tune and refine the model to bring it as close to perfection as possible.
Gathering the Data
As discussed in the previous chapter, using unrealistic data will only lead to unrealistic predictions and such results are practically useless in the real-world application of machine learning models. So, theoretical datasets are out of the question. However, procuring datasets is not as difficult as one may think. The machine learning community has very useful repositories that feature datasets of good quality allowing for users to create projects by themselves or even help people practice so that they can improve their model-creating and developing skills.
Here’s a list of some of the most common repositories that host datasets. If you’re looking for a specific dataset for your machine learning project, chances are you will find what you’re looking for on one of these domains:
  • Reputable open data repositories : UC Irvine Machine Learning Repository, Kaggle Datasets, and Amazon’s AWS datasets.
  • Meta Portals which redirect to other open data repositories : Data Portals, OpenDataMonitor, and Quandl.
  • Websites that can direct you to various open data repositories : Wikipedia’s list of Machine Learning datasets, Quora, and The datasets subreddit.
Since the major focus of this topic is linear regression, hence we will need to get a dataset that is relevant to this task. Otherwise, there will be no point creating a linear regression model while using a classification dataset. One of the most popular choices for Linear Regression tasks (where the model predicts values) which are also completely labeled is the California Housing Dataset. This dataset can be obtained from the StatLib repository. Although this dataset consists of the housing prices in California from the 90s, it has several qualities that make it more than suitable for training a linear regression model. For this project, we will ignore the date for which the data was recorded since it will not impact the performance of the model itself.
Choosing a Regression Task
There is a variety of regressions tasks to choose from. For instance, you can choose to create a model to predict the pricing trends in the stock market. However, we will be choosing the California Housing price prediction task as the data can be easily acquired and it is error-free along with being completely labeled. The data covers the pricing for every block in the state of California (at that time) along with few extra metrics as well (for instance, average income and population).
In the California Housing dataset, we can see that houses have been grouped into ‘blocks.’ This in accordance with the standard defined by the Census Bureau of the United States where each block (a geographical unit) consists of a population of anywhere between six hundred to three thousand civilians. To keep things simple and easy to understand, we will refer to ‘blocks’ as ‘districts’ as this is a more contextually accurate term to use.
The goal of our machine learning model will be to predict the average price of housing for a particular district.
Choosing the Appropriate Performance Measure
One of the most important steps in creating a model is to select the most optimal performance measure for the task at hand. We will explore performance measures with more details and depth in the upcoming chapters. In this section, we will only discuss the characteristics of the popular performance measures used in supervised learning, these are:
  • Root Mean Square Error (also known as RMSE)
  • Mean Absolute Error (also known as MAE)
Root Mean Square Error
Once we have chosen the task we want the model to perform, (in this case it would be linear regression on the California Housing Dataset) the next plan of action is to pick a performance measure that suits our task.
RMSE is a popular choice for models where we want to calculate the effectiveness of the model when it deals with regression problems. RMSE is important when creating linear regression models. If we do not implement any performance measure, then we won’t be able to have an idea of the overall errors the model makes when generating predictions. This will consequently snatch away the ability to pre-emptively tune model before we proceed any further as we will only know about the model’s lackluster performance when we actually test it and see that the predictions are somewhat off. RMSE calculates and displays the average error being made in the model’s predictions and depending on the size and significance of the error; it allocates them with corresponding weights. So an error with larger weight immediately signals the user that this error is pretty significant. Similarly, an error with a small weight will immediately let the user know that the error is not that significant.
Although many regression tasks prefer the implementation of the Root Mean Square Error function as the performance measure, there are always cases where you need to make an exception, simply because some regression tasks might just prefer a different function entirely.
Mean Absolute Error
When you encounter such exceptional cases (such as districts within the data that are ‘outliers’) where using the RMSE function is not a viable option, the Mean Absolute Error function becomes the next best choice.
Let’s try to understand the purpose of RMSE and MAE functions within a model. To keep it simple and easy to comprehend, the core concept of both RMSE and MAE functions is that they are a type of independent navigator. To elaborate, these functions calculate the direction of two particular vectors, the prediction vector, and the target value vector. Just as the name suggests, the prediction vector defines the pathway of the model’s predictions and the target vector defines the destination of the corresponding predictions. By calculating their directions, the functions can tell us whether the model’s predictions are correct or incorrect based on the fact if the prediction vector converges with the target vector.
The Mean Absolute Error is particularly useful for handling outlier data instances. For now, we will not dive any further into the performance measure functions as entire sections have been designated for this discussion only in the last two chapters.

Download the Data
Typically, when you work with linear regression datasets, the data is either in the form of tables, documents, or even files. Similarly, you will find that this data is stored in a database or any other form of data storage. This is because data scientists use software that can access the relational databases and process the data conveniently. However, to access the database, one would need to enter the appropriate credentials in order to authorize the access.
In this project, things are not that complicated. To access and load the data onto our system for use, we simply need to download the required file (which will be compressed) on to our machine. The file we will need for this project is the ‘housing.tgz ’ compressed file. Once the file is downloaded, we need to decompress it. To do that, we can use any decompression software tool of our choice. Once the file has been decompressed, we will end up with a CSV file named ‘housing.csv.’ CSV files consist of data where the values are separated from each other through commas.
We can also create a short python script that automates the process of downloading the dataset, decompressing, and extracting it. The following lines of code demonstrate how to do that:
import os
import tarfile
import urllib
DOWNLOAD_ROOT = "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ageron/handson-ml2/master/" HOUSING_PATH = os.path.join("datasets", "housing"
) HOUSING_URL = DOWNLOAD_ROOT + "datasets/housing/housing.tgz"
def fetch_housing_data(housing_url=HOUSING_URL,     housing_path=HOUSING_PATH): os.makedirs(housing_path, exist_ok=True)
tgz_path = os.path.join(housing_path, "housing.tgz")
urllib.request.urlretrieve(housing_url, tgz_path)
housing_tgz = tarfile.open(tgz_path)
housing_tgz.extractall(path=housing_path)
housing_tgz.close()
By using this script, we make the process of downloading the dataset more convenient and easy. Also, whenever we fetch the data (by using the fetch_housing_data() argument) a separate file directory will be generated within our workspace directory and the compressed file will be downloaded and put in this directory automatically. Once the file has been downloaded, the script will decompress and extract the housing.tgz file on its own as well, saving us the trouble of doing so.
Now that we have the necessary data for the machine learning model, all that’s left to do is load it onto the system’s memory using the ‘Pandas’ tool we installed earlier. Just as how we used a script to download, decompress, and extract the compressed data file, we can also use a script to load the data using the Pandas tool as well:
import pandas as pd
def load_housing_data(housing_path=HOUSING_PATH):
csv_path = os.path.join(housing_path, "housing.csv")
return pd.read_csv(csv_path)
Quickly Analyzing the Data
Before we go any further, it’s always a good idea to quickly glance over the structure of the data and understand how it’s organized. This practice will prove to be incredibly useful in other machine learning projects as there are times when we need to specify the data structure to the model and resize it according to the layers we are using.
By using the head() method from DataFrame, we can display the data that is stored in tables within the California Housing dataset. The following figure demonstrates this:
In the figure shown above, we have only displayed the first five rows in the California Housing dataset. Within this table, each row (o to 4) represents a separate district (blocks). Moreover, by looking at the arrangement and features of the table, we can see that there are a total of ten attributes (the data’s features). These attributes are the following:
  • California Housing Dataset Attributes: longitude, latitude, housing_median_age, total_rooms, total_bedrooms, population, households, median_income, median_house-value, ocean_proximity .
There are times when we do not want to observe data in tabular form. Instead, we prefer data to be shown in descriptive form. For such cases, we can use the info() method . This method returns a detailed description of the data which includes a summary of the features as well. For instance, when we use the info() method on the California Housing dataset, we will be greeted with a summary (how many rows and columns there are in the dataset, what’s the data type of each attribute, etc.) as shown in the figure below:
By looking at the summarized description, we can instantly comprehend that the entire dataset has a total of 20,640 instances. Although the number of instances is lower as compared to the other standard machine learning datasets, this is still more than enough for our project. Now here’s where analyzing the data before using it on the machine learning model becomes useful. Carefully look at the non-null values shown in the info() method’s description of the data. We can see that the fifth attribute (total_bedroom) has a total of 204333 non-null values. While on the surface, this may not seem as something significant, but if you look around, you’ll see that while the total_bedroom attribute has 20433 non-null values, the other attributes have 20640 non-null values. If we subtract the two numbers, then we get a remainder of 207. This means the total_bedroom attribute is missing from 207 districts in the dataset; the other attributes are included in every district. Now that we know there’s a discrepancy and what it is, we can make adjustments but we will do that later.
Another place where things seem to be amiss is the data types of the attributes. The first nine attributes are all floating-point types while only the last attribute (ocean_proximity) is an object type. Since this attribute is an object, this means it can be used as an object in the project as well. However, the dataset is in the form of Comma Separated Values, and this tells us that it should have been a textual attribute. If we use the head() method to display the tabular form of the California Housing dataset, we will see that the 10th attribute (ocean_proximity) has values that reiterate. If we consider this aspect, this would mean that the attribute is a categorical type. If we go through this assumption, then we can use the value_counts() method to compute the total number of existing categories along with the number of districts that are linked to these aforementioned categories:
>>> housing["ocean_proximity"].value_counts()
We now have a good understanding of the dataset and now, we can proceed to the next step which will be to divide the dataset into a training set and a testing set.
Creating a Testing Set Using Random Sampling
Generally, we do not have ample resources to gather a separate dataset just to test the capabilities of a machine learning model. This is because data needs to be cleaned, refined, and error-free, and searching for such a dataset to test our model can often be like looking for a needle in a haystack. For the sake of convenience (and mostly because we don’t have a choice), we separate a portion from the dataset we are training the model on and treat it as an independent dataset. In this way, we now have a dataset that will be used primarily for training the model and another dataset which the model has never seen to test how well the model has learned and how well it performs in real-world tasks.
But there are sometimes human errors (albeit it might be unintentional). Let’s discuss this aspect briefly before we move on to the next topic. By quickly analyzing the data in the California Housing set, we came to understand the structure of the dataset more intricately. This allowed us to implement better algorithms, performance measures, and activation functions, etc. In the same way,  looking through the testing set will also give us similar insight. If we know the data structure of the testing set, then we can purposefully design and choose the most optimal settings for the model according to the testing set. This creates a bias where we are giving more importance to the data we are training the model on rather than preparing it to tackle real-world problems. The end result of this bias would be that the model performs spectacularly on the training and testing set, but its performance lacks significantly when it tries to solve real-world problems. Thus showing us that the efforts we made to make a good linear regression machine learning model did not reciprocate as we would expect. This bias is generally referred to as ‘data snooping bias ’ and it would be recommended that if you know that you will end up using a portion from the original dataset as the training set, then you shouldn’t go through the entire dataset.
Now let’s move on to the part where we actually separate a portion from the California Housing set to make a testing set. Generally, the process of picking out data instances from the set is random, but you can also choose to only use the last portion of the dataset as your testing set as well. However, in this case, the testing set will be created by choosing random instances from the dataset and the size of the relative size of the testing set will be 20% of the original dataset.
The following lines of code demonstrate how to create a testing set. First, we will have to specify the random shuffling of the original dataset and then create the testing set while leveraging the shuffling we defined:
import numpy as np
def split_train_test(data, test_ratio):
shuffled_indices = np.random.permutation(len(data))
test_set_size = int(len(data) * test_ratio)
test_indices = shuffled_indices[:test_set_size]
train_indices = shuffled_indices[test_set_size:]
return data.iloc[train_indices], data.iloc[test_indices]
Now to create the testing set:
13 >>> train_set, test_set = split_train_test(housing, 0.2)
>>> len(train_set)
16512
>>> len(test_set)
4128
Although this approach towards creating a testing set indeed does the job, however, it’s not necessarily the best solution. There are other ways of doing it as well which we will see when we discuss deep neural networks in this book. For now, let’s focus on why this approach is not devoid of any flaws. The thing is, even though a testing set has been created using these lines of code, we cannot omit them from the program once we have saved the testing set. So, whenever the program is executed, a testing set that is completely distinct from the previous one will be created. This defeats the entire purpose of testing sets; at some point, the model will be familiar with the entire dataset, except for the separate portion. When the next testing set is generated, the instances being used there are likely the instances that the model has already gone through. So here’s the gist of the problem we are currently facing. We cannot omit this particular method of creating a testing set, but we also want testing set to be generated for our use as well. The only logical solution is to find a way in which the testing set that is randomly generated by this function is always the same. This would mean that no matter how many times we execute the program, we won’t need to worry about the model being falsely tested.
Here is the aforementioned solution to the testing set problem:
  • If you’re working with a dataset where each instance is accompanied by a marker that acts as its identifier, then we can leverage this aspect. Luckily, the California Housing set has instances that are marked. As such, we can use these identifiers to judge whether this instance should be included in the testing set or not. This can be done by specifying a threshold. Each identifier has a hash value and this makes for a good threshold. We can set up the threshold in a way that if the hash value of an instance’s identifier is less than 20% of the maximum hash value, then it is allowed to be picked for the testing set. Otherwise, this specific instance is not considered suitable to be used in the testing set.
  • There are cases where not every dataset has instances accompanied by identifiers. In such a scenario, an easy solution is to simply generate the testing set when the model is executed for the first time and then save it. Afterward, you can omit the function that generates the testing set along with the portion of the dataset which was used in the testing set. So, when you start up the model again, you can load up this testing set only then and everything will go smoothly.
To implement the first solution with the identifiers as the deciding factor for each instance’s involvement in the testing set, use the following lines of code:
from zlib import crc32
def test_set_check(identifier, test_ratio):
return crc32(np.int64(identifier)) & 0xffffffff < test_ratio * 2**32
def split_train_test_by_id(data, test_ratio, id_column):
ids = data[id_column]
in_test_set = ids.apply(lambda id_: test_set_check(id_, test_ratio))
return data.loc[~in_test_set], data.loc[in_test_set]
Since we are dealing with data that is originally in tabular form, this means that the index (rows and column) of each data instance is unique. The data instances in row 2 cannot be the same instance in row 3 and vice versa and same is the case for columns. Generally, we would prefer to use columns as the identifiers for each data instance but unfortunately, in the California Housing set, the columns are not unique but the rows are. So we will be using the rows as the unique identifiers for each data instance:
housing_with_id = housing.reset_index() # adds an `index` column
train_set, test_set = split_train_test_by_id(housing_with_id, 0.2, "index")
Since we are using rows as the identifiers for data instances, there is a possible scenario that might risk disrupting the very solution itself. Let’s say that we want to add new data to the original dataset. If we do so carelessly, we might mess up the arrangement of the rows (corresponding to each data instance) which would, in turn, mess up the whole identifier set up as well. Thus, we need to make sure that even if new data does get added, it is placed at the end of the table only leaving the rest of the index arrangement untouched.
If you cannot control the placement of new data, then we simply need to find another element that can serve as a unique identifier for the data instances. Using the attributes of the dataset is a good option as well. However, it is important to remember that if you are using an attribute as a unique identifier, then the attribute itself must be stable. This means that when the data is updated or new data is added, the attributes of the existing instances should not be affected and must not clash with the attributes of the new data instances. In this case, if we were to use attributes as the unique identifiers, then the most suitable option would be the geographical attributes (longitudes and latitudes) for very obvious reasons. The use of attributes as unique identifiers has been demonstrated in the following lines of code:
15 housing_with_id["id"] = housing["longitude"] * 1000 + housing["latitude"]
train_set, test_set = split_train_test_by_id(housing_with_id, 0.2, "id")
Preparing the Data
Now that we have split the data into two sets, we are now ready to prepare it so that the algorithms can use it. By saying we need to prepare the data, we actually mean that we need to transform it so that it becomes usable for the algorithms. This is because algorithms need to be fed data in a specific format and type. We cannot use the original structure of the data. There are two ways to prepare the data, we can either do it manually or we can use functions to automate the process. You might think that if you have ample time, doing it manually would be preferable but on the contrary, using functions has better advantages. Some of these perks have been listed below:
  1. In some cases, there are times when you need to perform the data transformations again (for instance, when adding new data or loading an updated dataset). If you used a function to do the data transformation, then doing it again will be very easy. However, if you did it manually, then a simple task will become quite arduous and time-consuming.
  2. Each algorithm requires data to be transformed differently before it can use it. So, for different tasks, different datasets, and different algorithms, you will steadily build up a collection of transformation functions. At that point, not only will you be familiar with the functions but also, you can reuse them as well.
  3. Functions can be used to transform the data as it is being streamed to the memory, in turn, maximizing the efficiency of your model.
  4. Using functions allow for easy and quick data transformations. This enables the user to try out different data transformations with different algorithms to see what suits your model the best.
For now, we will create a copy of the training set and omit the labels and predictors within it. If we do not do this, then the function will also transform the predictors and labels as well. This can be done by using the copy() and drop() methods as shown in the following lines of code:
housing = strat_train_set.drop("median_house_value", axis=1)
housing_labels = strat_train_set["median_house_value"].copy()
Data Cleaning
Generally, it is recommended to understand and learn about the algorithms first before trying to do a machine learning project on your own. This is because algorithms play a major role in how the model learns patterns. Since algorithms are in the driving seat of the model’s journey towards learning, using data that is incomplete or noisy will badly affect the patterns the algorithm learns as well. This is why many of the algorithms used in machine learning (with a few exceptions) are designed so that they do not function if the data being fed to them has incomplete attributes (features).
At the beginning of this project, while analyzing the dataset we came to know that 207 districts were missing the fifth attribute (total_bedroom) and the attribute itself is short of some values as well. So before we go any further, we must patch up this problem. There are three ways we can do that:
  • We can simply ignore those districts which are missing the attributes and omit them from the dataset altogether. This can be done by using the dropna() method of DataFrame as shown below:
housing.dropna(subset=["total_bedrooms"])
  • We can omit the attribute from every data instance so we don’t have to deal with it. This can be done by using the drop() method of DataFrame as shown below:
housing.drop("total_bedrooms", axis=1)
  • We can assign a value of our own choice to the missing values in the attribute. Although this will be based on the user’s intuition, so the performance of the model might get affected based on which value you choose. This can be done by using the fillna() method of DataFrame as shown below:
median = housing["total_bedrooms"].median()
housing["total_bedrooms"].fillna(median, inplace=True)
Choosing option three is more logical as the impact of either omitting those districts or omitting the attribute from the entire dataset can have a huge impact on the model’s performance as well. So if you choose the third method as well, then it is recommended that you use the median value (calculating the average value of all the other values of the same attribute) to replace the missing values in the attribute. Once you have calculated the median value, you should save it since you will also need to use it to replace the missing values in the testing set later on as well.
Here’s a handy dandy tip regarding Scikit-Learn when you encounter missing values. The Scikit-Learn library features a special class which is known as ‘SimpleImputer .’ By using this class, we can easily calculate the median value in a particular attribute and then use it to fill in the missing values. The reason why using this class from Scikit-Learn is preferred is because it automates the calculation of medians and the replacement of values in a simple manner. Using the SimpleImputer class is pretty simple. Here’s a chronological explanation of how to implement the SimpleImputer class from Scikit-Learn:
from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer
imputer = SimpleImputer(strategy=" median")
Median is the calculation of the average of several values (that are of the same type). Since this calculation is done on numbers, it makes no sense for us to include an attribute that is not a floating-point type or any numerical data type. From analyzing the data, we know that the last attribute (ocean_proximity) is not a floating-point type like the rest of the attributes, hence we need to drop it before we can use the SimpleImputer. To do this, we will simply create a copy of the dataset which does not have the ocean_proximity attribute:
housing_num = housing.drop("ocean_proximity", axis=1)
We will now use the fit() method to make sure that the training set allows for the imputer instance to fit. To do this, we use the following statement:
imputer.fit(housing_num)
At this point, the SimpleImputer class from the Scikit-Library should be done with its job, i.e., the median value of all nine attributes in the dataset (remember that we omitted the 10th attribute ocean_proximity) has been calculated and is stored within the class’s corresponding variable. But we shouldn’t be relaxed right now. Right now, we know that in the current version of the California Housing dataset we are using, the 5th attribute has missing values. However, there’s no guarantee that when the model is executed, the newly generated values won’t have any missing values as well. So, it’s better not to take chances. That’s why all the attributes (except for the 10th attribute) will be used in the SimpleImputer class and not just the 5th attribute (total_bedrooms) :
>>> imputer.statistics_ array
([ -118.51 , 34.26 , 29. , 2119.5 , 433. , 1164. , 408. , 3.5409])
>>> housing_num.median().values array
([ -118.51 , 34.26 , 29. , 2119.5 , 433. , 1164. , 408. , 3.5409])
The imputer is now familiar with the medians of every attribute. We can implement this imputer to convert the training set and fill in any values that might be missing:
X = imputer.transform(housing_num)
The output is a standard NumPy array that features attributes that have been transformed by the imputer. Converting array back to its original form so that it can be used in Pandas is not that difficult as well. All you have to do is use the following statement:
housing_tr = pd.DataFrame(X, columns=housing_num.columns, index=housing_num.index)
Creating, Training and Evaluating the Model
The process of training a machine learning model is not that difficult. On the contrary, if you have understood the concepts which have been elaborated in the previous sections of this book, then you will find that the training process comparatively simple. To train the model, we must first create the model itself. To do this, we will first import the LinearRegression library which will contain all of the algorithms, tools, and activation functions necessary to train the model. Moreover, we also load the dataset we have prepared along with the corresponding labels. This has been demonstrated below:
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
lin_reg = LinearRegression() lin_reg.fit(housing_prepared, housing_labels)
We have now successfully created a machine learning model specifically for performing Linear Regression tasks. We will now proceed to train the model on the testing dataset:
>>> some_data = housing.iloc[:5]
>>> some_labels = housing_labels.iloc[:5]
>>> some_data_prepared = full_pipeline.transform(some_data)
>>> print("Predictions:", lin_reg.predict(some_data_prepared))
Predictions: [ 210644.6045 317768.8069 210956.4333 59218.9888 189747.5584]
>>> print("Labels:", list(some_labels)) Labels: [286600.0, 340600.0, 196900.0, 46300.0, 254500.0]
The model works properly but the predictions are not exactly accurate. For instance, the first prediction generated by the model after training is inaccurate by a measure of 40% (meaning the value predicted deviates from the target value by a margin of 40%). The Linear Regression Model’s RMSE can be assessed on the complete training set with the help of Scikit-Learn’s mean_squared_error() function:
>>> from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error
>>> housing_predictions = lin_reg.predict(housing_prepared)
>>> lin_mse = mean_squared_error(housing_labels, housing_predictions)
>>> lin_rmse = np.sqrt(lin_mse)
>>> lin_rmse
68628.19819848922
The result clearly is unsatisfactory and it proves as an example of a model underfitting the training data. The obtained result suggests that either the information provided by the features is not sufficient, or that the model is not very strong. Underfitting can be fixed by:
  • Choosing a powerful model
  • Inputting the training algorithm with better features
  • Reducing constraints on the model.
The last option can be eliminated here because the model is non-regularized. Before trying out the addition of more features like the log of population, choose to work with a more complex model such as the DecisionTreeRegressor. This strong model is capable of searching for more complex nonlinear relationships in the data. The following lines of code demonstrate this.
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeRegressor
tree_reg = DecisionTreeRegressor()
tree_reg.fit(housing_prepared, housing_labels)
After finishing the training of the model, evaluate it on the training set as shown below:
>>> housing_predictions = tree_reg.predict(housing_prepared)
>>> tree_mse = mean_squared_error(housing_labels, housing_predictions)
>>> tree_rmse = np.sqrt(tree_mse)
>>> tree_rmse 0.0
The code doesn’t seem to display any error which may lead to the conclusion that the model is absolutely perfect. But the more likely assumption would be that the model has overfitted to the data in a terrible way leading to the given result. To make sure of this, do not interfere with the test set until the model is prepared to be launched with confidence. One part of the training set is used to train the model while the other part is used for validation of the model.
Using the Testing Dataset to Evaluate the Model
Before testing the model, we would usually tune the hyperparameters to improve the overall performance of the model; this is done using techniques such as cross-validation etc. However, these topics have been discussed in detail in the upcoming chapters. For now, we will focus on evaluating and testing the model.
So, the next step for this project will include evaluating the final model on the test set. The process is simple and involves acquiring predictors and the labels from the test set, executing the full_pipeline.transform() method to transform the data and evaluating the final model on the test set. The entire process has been demonstrated below:
final_model = grid_search.best_estimator_
X_test = strat_test_set.drop("median_house_value", axis=1)
y_test = strat_test_set["median_house_value"].copy
() X_test_prepared = full_pipeline.transform(X_test)
final_predictions = final_model.predict(X_test_prepared)
final_mse = mean_squared_error(y_test, final_predictions)
final_rmse = np.sqrt(final_mse) # => evaluates to 47,730.2
Although the performance of the model is not that spectacular, once you have understood the concept of hyperparameter tuning, which has been explained in detail in upcoming chapters, you will be capable of boosting your model’s performance by a sizable margin. This will serve as a good exercise, i.e., improving a model you have already created when you learn something new.
However, before you get excited about the potential benefits of hyperparameter tuning, you must understand one thing. If hyperparameters are excessively tuned, the working of the model turns out to be somewhat worse than described by cross-validation. This is because the system is set to work better with validation data instead of unknown datasets. If such a problem occurs, it is recommended not to recalibrate the hyperparameters to get values that look good on the test set but cannot generally work with new data.

Chapter 3
Creating a Classification Model
with Scikit-Learn
In the first chapter, we learned that in a supervised machine learning model, the most common task performed is either a regression task (the model generates predictions for values) or a classification task (the model generates predictions for classes). Similarly, in the previous chapter, we explored how to build a machine learning model and train it for a regression task. In this chapter, we will now put our focus towards building a model to predict classes.
The MNIST Dataset
For any task, the machine learning model needs data to be trained with the appropriate data so it can become familiar with the job that’s being assigned to it. Since we are creating a model that can perform classification for the first time (in this book anyway), we will be using a dataset that is the most popular for such an adventure, the MNIST dataset.
The MNIST dataset can be seen as the ‘hello world!’ equivalent in machine learning. Beginners becoming familiar with the field of machine learning are often guided through the basics with important tips and tricks using datasets like these.
The MNIST dataset is a collection of images depicting handwritten digits. The set contains a total of 70,000 images (small in resolution and size). The reason why this dataset is being used as the forefront for first machine learning projects because the goal of the model is simple and the dataset is fairly large and is completely labeled. Labeled data means that every element of the dataset has been marked with appropriate classes and such. For the MNIST dataset, each image has been labeled with a number that corresponds to the digit being portrayed by the image. All 70,000 images are labeled under a total of 10 classes. Each of the images belongs to either one of these classes (class digit 1, class digit 2, and class digit 3, etc.).
Since the MNIST dataset is the best option for learning the building and training of machine learning models to perform classification, many people also use this dataset to try out new algorithms and new techniques seeing how the model performs. Even if we don’t talk about this dataset in this book, you are bound to come across MNIST sooner or later in your journey of exploring machine learning.
Downloading the MNIST Dataset
We will be using Scikit-Learn to create the machine learning model and train it. Downloading and fetching the MNIST dataset for the model is pretty straightforward with Scikit-Learn’s helper functions. These functions allow the users to download and fetch pretty much any popular dataset in the community (and as we discussed before, MNIST is one such dataset). The following lines of code demonstrate the use of Scikit-Learn’s fetch() function to download the MNIST dataset:
>>> from sklearn.datasets import fetch_openml
>>> mnist = fetch_openml('mnist_784', version=1)
>>> mnist.keys()
