However, before we proceed, we need to be careful of the initialization of our output layer. In this case, the output layer was initialized randomly, so there are high chances that the result displayed by the model will contain errors. In turn, this might lead to the spawning of big error gradients that will eventually destroy the weights we have reused.
A simple and effective way to tackle this problem is just to freeze the layers we have reused for a duration of few epochs during training. This will give the newly created layers of the model some time to learn weights. To freeze the layers, we just need to set the attribute of trainable to a value of 0, i.e., False, and then proceed to compile the model. The entire process has been shown below:
for layer in model_B_on_A.layers[:-1]:
layer.trainable = False
model_B_on_A.compile(loss="binary_crossentropy", optimizer="sgd",
metrics=["accuracy"])
When freezing and unfreezing the layers, always remember to compile the model afterward every time.
Now we let the model train for a couple of epochs and then unfreeze the layers by switching their trainable attribute to ‘True.’ After we unfreeze the layers, we must compile the model then let it continue its training. In this way, it can tune the layers which have been transferred from Model X to perform the task (binary classification) of the new Model, i.e., Model Y.
In addition, to mitigate the chances of failure or errors, we should always lower the rate of learning whenever we unfreeze models. If the layers are being trained at a normal pace, this might cause a discrepancy in the learning rates of the reused layers and the new layers, so it’s best to lower the learning rate so that we can avoid wounding the reused weights.
history = model_B_on_A.fit(X_train_B, y_train_B, epochs=4,
validation_data=(X_valid_B, y_valid_B))
for layer in model_B_on_A.layers[:-1]:
layer.trainable = True
optimizer = keras.optimizers.SGD(lr=1e-4)                # the default lr is 1e-2
model_B_on_A.compile(loss="binary_crossentropy", optimizer=optimizer,
metrics=["accuracy"])
history = model_B_on_A.fit(X_train_B, y_train_B, epochs=16,
validation_data=(X_valid_B, y_valid_B))
Let’s evaluate how the model performs after using the transfer learning technique.
>>> model_B_on_A.evaluate(X_test_B, y_test_B)
[0.06887910133600235, 0.9925]
From the above evaluation result, we can see that the overall accuracy of the model on the testing set data is a whopping 99.25%. If we consider the accuracy of the model before the use of transfer learning, then we have successfully reduced the rate of error from 2.8% to about 0.7% which is a very big improvement.
However, it is advised that the transfer learning technique be only used for large and deep neural networks because small neural networks can only learn a handful of very specific patterns. Hence, the layers used from such models would be practically useless for other small neural networks.
Dealing with the Problem of Slow Training
It’s no surprise that building a deep neural network with many connections and weights will also exponentially increase the time required to train it fully. Let’s say that you have successfully built a deep neural network by overcoming the gradient problem. Next, you resolved the lack of adequate training data by using transfer learning, but the next problem you’ll face is very slow training speeds.
You might not have noticed it but there are four techniques discussed in the previous sections that also have an effect of increasing the training speed of deep neural networks. To be specific, these techniques are:
  • Employing a good initialization strategy
  • Choosing and using the most suitable activation function
  • Batch Normalization
  • Transfer Learning
While these four techniques are primarily used to solve some other problems plaguing deep neural networks, they also positively affect the speed at which the network trains itself.
In this section, we will be discussing a method that is concerned with primarily boosting the overall training speed of the deep neural network. This method involves using an optimizer that is faster and more efficient than the standard Gradient Descent optimizer we are used to seeing. The identity and properties of an optimizer depend on the algorithm it uses. In this section, we will discuss four of the most popular and most effective algorithms (except for AdaGrad) for optimizers:
  1. Momentum Optimization
  2. Nesterov Accelerated Gradient
  3. AdaGrad
  4. RMSProp
Momentum Optimization
The core concept of momentum optimization was first introduced in the year 1964, but let’s leave the boring history stuff for now and get straight into how it works.
By the name itself, we can get a rough idea that the fundamental working of this algorithm has something to do with momentum. Let us look at an analogy to understand momentum optimization. Let’s say a bowling ball is rolling down a sloped surface that is smooth. During the time the ball rolls, the initial momentum will be considerably slower. Still, as it keeps on rolling down the sloped surface, the ball will gain momentum at a considerably fast pace until it is bound by the terminal velocity which is due to any type of resistance (rolling friction and air resistance). Momentum optimization works in the same way; it starts slow but gains speed quickly until it reaches the maximum limit to where it can speed up the training process.
In comparison, the way Gradient Descent works is by taking short steps at regular intervals down the same smooth sloped surface. This means that momentum optimization will be the first to complete training the entire network as opposed to gradient descent. Hence, momentum optimization will work considerably faster than Gradient Descent for deep neural networks.
Now let’s move on to the mathematical aspect of these two algorithms and understand how they work.
First off, Gradient Descent is an algorithm that primarily works by performing updates on the weights (denoted by θ) and so does momentum optimization. But in Gradient Descent, the update is calculated in a series of arithmetical operations. Firstly, the algorithm multiplies the weights ∇ θJ(θ) with the defined learning rate (denoted by ‘η’) and then directly subtracts it with the cost function’s gradient ‘J(θ).’ The resulting equation is as follows:
θ ← θ – η∇ θJ(θ)
However, the major flaw of this algorithm is that it does not consider the values of the preceding gradients. This is exactly the reason as to why using Gradient Descent results in an exponentially slow training process.
On to momentum optimization which, unlike Gradient Descent, gives due importance to the preceding gradients (which would be ignored by Gradient Descent). To elaborate, on every iteration, it takes the ‘momentum vector (which is denoted by m )’ and subtracts the local gradient from it. Then it updates the weights by simply adding the new value of the momentum vector to them. In simpler terms, the gradients are accelerating (not to be confused with speed). The resulting mathematical equation is as follows:
  1. Subtracting the local gradient from momentum:
m ←βm − η∇ θJ(θ)
  1. Updating the weights by adding the resulting momentum to it:
θ← θ + m
However, if we let things be, the momentum will eventually become larger and uncontrollable. Thus, the algorithm also employs a kind of mechanism to stimulate friction to keep the momentum in check by using a hyperparameter ‘β’. This hyperparameter is referred to as ‘momentum ’ and it can be set to any value between 0 (which indicates maximum friction) and 1 (which indicates no friction). Generally, the value of this hyperparameter is set to 0.9.
So, the bigger the momentum, the larger the weight update. This can also be seen as ‘terminal velocity’ referred to in the analogy.
Keras makes it very easy and simple for users to implement momentum optimization into deep neural networks. The following line demonstrates how it can be implemented:
optimizer = keras.optimizers.SGD(lr=0.001, momentum=0.9)
Momentum optimization does have a drawback but it’s not that dire which might require careful consideration before use. When we use momentum optimization, we are also using a hyperparameter along with it. This means that we now have an additional hyperparameter that needs to be tuned, but it has been observed that by keeping it at 0.9, the optimizer works pretty well.
Nesterov Accelerated Gradient
Notice that momentum and acceleration are concepts that are closely related to each other. The same is the case with the Nesterov Accelerated Gradient; it is very similar to momentum optimization but has some key changes which make it perform even better. To be more precise, the Nesterov Accelerated Gradient method is an optimizer which is basically a variant of momentum optimization.
The Nesterov Gradient Method (some might even refer to this as Nesterov momentum optimization) works by calculating the cost function’s gradient and performs the same arithmetic operations as momentum optimization. But the difference here is that instead of calculating the cost function’s gradient at its local position (this is referred to as θ), it considers the position ahead in the same direction as that of the momentum. Mathematically, this new position of the cost function’s gradient can be expressed as θ + βm. Here’s the mathematical expression:
  1. Subtracting the gradient at a position in the direction of momentum:
m  ← mβ - η∇ θJ(θ)
  1. Adding the resulting momentum to the weights:
θ ← θ + m
This change might seem insignificant or just underwhelming but in reality, the difference in training speed is monumental as compared to momentum optimization. The reason behind this is that at any given moment, the momentum vector will be directly pointing towards the optimum point. This makes the gradient updates more accurate as compared to momentum optimization and in essence, less time consuming for the gradients to come back in the right direction. The following figure demonstrates how the Nesterov momentum optimization updates the gradients as compared to the updates done by standard momentum optimization.
On the surface, this might look like a small change with no big impact. But the thing is, these small changes in the direction of the gradient updates towards the optimum eventually add up and then affect the training speed monumentally. This is why Nesterov momentum optimization is faster and more efficient than standard momentum optimization.
The implementation of the Nesterov Accelerated Gradient method is the same as momentum optimization in Keras, all you need to do is just add a small statement, i.e., nesterov=True when you define the SGD optimizer and you’re done. The following line of code demonstrates the implementation of the Nesterov Accelerated Gradient:
optimizer = keras.optimizers.SGD(lr=0.001, momentum=0.9, nesterov=True)
AdaGrad
To understand the AdaGrad optimizer, we must first understand which deficiency of Gradient Descent does it rectify or improve. The elongated bowl problem is a popular analogy used to describe the shortcomings of Gradient Descent in a manner that is easy to understand so let’s use this. Instead of explaining the whole scenario, we will only stick to the important parts.
The elongated bowl problem explains that Gradient Descent will aim to find the steepest slope and quickly go down. However, this slope does not direct the gradient updates in the right direction (the optimum), but once it reaches the end of the steepness, it then moves slowly with small steps towards the bottom of the valley.
AdaGrad uses an algorithm that can change the direction of Gradient Descent towards the optimum a bit earlier. It does this by simply taking the gradient vectors and then downscale them according to the dimensions that are the steepest. The AdaGrad optimizer also has a hyperparameter which is the learning rate.
The mathematical explanation of AdaGrad is too quirky and complex for this discussion so we will leave it out. However, if you’re interested in how it works, then you can always look it up on the internet and you’ll find more than enough information.
One thing you must remember when using AdaGrad is that the learning rate of the neural network using this optimizer seems to diminish over time (i.e., decay). This learning rate decay is harsher for steep dimensions as compared to other dimensions that have a relatively gentler slope. Moreover, AdaGrad is known for its risk of slowing down considerably after only a short amount of time before even reaching the global optimum. But this doesn’t make it particularly bad. For instance, the hyperparameter used by the AdaGrad optimizer doesn’t need much tuning.
However, the overall trend in the impactfulness of optimizers dictates that AdaGrad does more harm than good. It can be very useful for shallow neural networks or small neural networks performing Linear Regression tasks, but it is not recommended to be used to train deep neural networks.
RMSProp
In AdaGrad, we learned that the algorithm is unsuitable for use in large and deep neural networks because of how quickly it starts to slow down never seems to converge to the optimum. RMSProp is a better and usable variant of the AdaGrad optimizer as it eliminates the flaws AdaGrad has.
The way RMSProp tackles the flaw of slowing too quickly and never converging to the optimum is by considering only those gradients from the most recent iterations (unlike AdaGrad which considered all of the gradients from the beginning of the network’s training). The algorithm accomplishes this by employing exponential decay. The decay rate is a hyperparameter for the RMSProp optimizer but it does not need much tuning. Although you can set it to any value between 0 and 1, the default value (0.9) has been seen to work quite well, and often, you will not need to tune the hyperparameter even if it’s at the default value.
Implementing an RMSProp optimizer using Keras is very simple and easy. The correct way has been demonstrated below:
optimizer = keras.optimizers.RMSprop(lr=0.001, rho=0.9)
In the line of code shown above, the argument at the end (rho=0.9) refers to the hyperparameter of exponential decay. Apart from tasks that are basic and simple, the RMSProp optimizer is observed to perform way better than AdaGrad for complex tasks and deep neural networks.
Dealing with the Risk of Overfitting of Deep Neural Networks
We have now come down to the last major problem faced in using deep neural networks, the risk of overfitting the model when the network has so many parameters.
In standard deep neural networks, you will find that most of them generally have hundreds of thousands of parameters and some may even feature more than a million parameters. Although this gives the model an insane amount of capacity for performing very complex tasks and processes highly complex datasets, the problem of overfitting still looms around. In other words, the flexibility that arises with using so many parameters comes at a price, a higher chance for the network to overfit to the dataset. One of the most commonly used methods for tackling this problem is ‘Regularization .’
In the previous chapter, we discussed some techniques and methods that can be used as regularizers too. Even the technique of ‘Batch Normalization’ is somewhat for a good regularizer (although the main purpose of creating this method was to handle the gradient problems). However, we will now shift our focus to techniques that are dedicated to regularization and are currently popular for their functionality. These regularization techniques are as follows:
  • l1 and l2 regularization
  • Dropout regularization
  • Max-norm regularization
l 1 and l 2 Regularization
This type of regularization is mainly used to constrain the connection weights of the network in which it is being used (l2 regularization) or to create a sparse model with most of the weights set to 0 (l1 regularization). You can also use both of these regularizations together as well. For demonstration purposes, let’s implement the l2 regularization on some connection weights of a dense layer in Keras while keeping the ‘regularization factor’ to a value of 0.01:
layer = keras.layers.Dense(100, activation="elu",
kernel_initializer="he_normal",
kernel_regularizer=keras.regularizers.l2(0.01))
In the last line, we can see that the regularization method has been specified by using the kernel_regularizer argument. The argument is followed by the l2() function detailing the use of l2 regularization. When we train the model, the function will return the l­2 regularizer on every training epoch. This way, the regularizer will be able to calculate the regularization loss. Once it has the value of the regularization loss, it then adds it to the concluding loss.
To use l1 regularization, we simply need to replace the last argument with ‘keras.regularizers.l1() ’. This will return the l1 regularizer. If you want to use both l1 ­ and l2 regularization, we can do so by using the following argument ‘keras.regularizers.l1_l2() ’. However, you must specify the regularization factors for both regularizers since we are using two regularizations.
Usually, when applying regularizations to layers, the trend leans towards applying this regularizer to every layer in the model’s neural network. But this trend is not for regularizers only, applying the same initialization strategy and the same activation function throughout the hidden layers is what you will end up doing in a deep neural network. But to do this, the approach a beginner or somebody with not much experience working with deep neural networks will simply repeat the arguments for the regularizer, activation function, and initialization strategy for every layer in the network. But this approach is not practical. Instead, it makes the resulting code more prone to error and unnecessarily lengthy. To avoid repeating the arguments over and over, we can either just use loops to refactor the code itself, or use the Python function ‘functools.partial() ’ as demonstrated below:
from functools import partial
RegularizedDense = partial(keras.layers.Dense,
activation="elu",
kernel_initializer="he_normal",
kernel_regularizer=keras.regularizers.l2(0.01))
model = keras.models.Sequential([
keras.layers.Flatten(input_shape=[28, 28]),
RegularizedDense(300),
RegularizedDense(100),
RegularizedDense(10, activation="softmax",
kernel_initializer="glorot_uniform")
])
Dropout
This is arguably the most popular and widely used regularizer specifically for deep neural networks due to how successful it is when implemented in a model. To get an idea of its practicality and its usefulness, consider this. If we use the dropout regularizer in a neural network that has been built by professionals with state-of-the-art architecture and resources, we can easily expect a 1% to 2% increase in the model’s accuracy. At first glance, many people might consider this boost in accuracy as insignificant, but it’s quite the contrary. If the best deep neural network model features an accuracy of 96%, then a 2% boost would be really huge (this also means that the error rate of the model has dropped by 50%, i.e., from 4% error to 2% error).
You might think that since the regularizer is good, there must be a very complex working it has to follow to get such results. But to the contrary, the dropout regularizer actually works in a pretty simple manner.
When the dropout regularizer is implemented in a neural network, all of the neurons (input neurons), except for the output neurons, have a chance to be completely ignored during training. In other words, there’s a chance that any neuron can be dropped from the training process. The probability variable p specifies this chance. However, dropping a neuron does not mean that it is eliminated from the model. When a neuron is dropped, it will not be included in the current training epoch, but when the next epoch starts, the previously ignored neuron will be trained as well, and in its place, some other neuron will be dropped. This process of dropping during training keeps happening until the training ends.
The probability p according to which each neuron has a chance of being dropped is actually the hyperparameter of the dropout regularizer itself. This hyperparameter is referred to as the ‘dropout rate .’ Generally, the hyperparameter is set to a value between 10% to 50% for the best results. However, it has been observed that if the regularizer is used in a recurrent neural network, the best value for the hyperparameter is between 20% to 30%. Similarly, if the regularizer is used in a convolutional neural network, it’s best to set the hyperparameter to a value between 40% to 50%.  The following figure demonstrates the process of dropping neurons:
It is a common question as to how does such a destructive and unconventional approach boosts the accuracy of a neural network. Let’s explore an analogy (although it might not be fully practical, the concept is well-delivered). Consider a company that follows the same routine happening in a neural network using the dropout regularizer. Each day, every employee has to toss a coin to decide if they are supposed to show up in the office or take the day off. Theoretically, such a company would have to adapt it’s working infrastructure accordingly. Every employee would have to be efficient in every expertise required in the organization. This means that there will be no longer a person who is designated solely for a critical task; every employee would be required to be capable of performing the same task as well. Moreover, employees would also have to extend their scope of cooperation to not just a handful of people but everyone in the organization. In such a way, even if one person who has expertise in the company’s critical tasks quits, the company wouldn’t be affected in any major way. On the contrary, the company would become quite resilient. Although this analogy is not very practical in real life, the theoretical results of such an approach are very much true for neural networks.
To be more precise, in a neural network using the dropout regularizer, each neuron has no choice but to learn patterns as much as possible as it cannot rely on the neighboring neurons since they can be dropped at any point. Moreover, excessive reliance on a handful of neurons to perform major tasks is also eliminated. So, even if there is a slight change in the inputs, the neurons will not be affected as much (they would become less sensitive). In conclusion, the resulting neural network is stern and robust that can generalize fairly well.
We have kept the concept simple enough but there is one detail that cannot be simplified. Let’s say that we keep the hyperparameter p at 50%. This means that in the training phase, a neuron will end up being connected to double the input neurons as opposed to the validation phase or even generating predictions. This creates a disparity in what the connection weights would be during training and what they would be during actual testing. If we leave it be, then during testing, every neuron in the network will receive an input signal that is two times as big compared to what the neural network was trained with. This will lead to the poor performance of the model. To compensate this disparity, we have to take each connection weight of the neurons in the network and multiply it by a factor of 0.5.
The dropout regularizer can be implemented in the neural network during its training phase by using Keras. For implementing the regularization technique, we will need to add a layer by using the ‘keras.layers.Dropout ’ statement. When the training process starts, random inputs will be dropped (i.e., set to 0) by the regularizer and the remaining inputs will be divided according to the probability set. Once the training is over, the job of the regularizer is also over. It simply gives the input to the succeeding layer. The following lines of code demonstrate the implementation of the dropout regularization just before each of the dense layers. The rate of the dropout has also been specified using the ‘rate= ’ argument (0.2).
model = keras.models.Sequential([
keras.layers.Flatten(input_shape=[28, 28]) ,
keras.layers.Dropout(rate=0.2),
keras.layers.Dense(300, activation="elu", kernel_initializer="he_normal"),
keras.layers.Dropout(rate=0.2),
keras.layers.Dense(100, activation="elu", kernel_initializer="he_normal"),
keras.layers.Dropout(rate=0.2),
keras.layers.Dense(10, activation="softmax")
])
The dropout rate can be adjusted according to the overfitting observed on the training set. If the model overfits on the training set, then you can increase the dropout rate until its overfitting is eliminated. Similarly, if you set the dropout rate high from the start, then the neural network will most likely underfit on the training set. You will need to adjust the dropout rate by decreasing it until the neural network does not underfit. You will need to tune the dropout rate until you find the right value for your network.
A cool trick is to note the size of your layers and then adjust the dropout rate for each layer separately according to their sizes. For instance, for large layers, you can increase the dropout rate and for layers that are small in size, you can decrease the dropout rate. In deep learning models that have been made with state-of-the-art resources and architectural skills, the dropout rate is primarily used only after the final hidden layer in the neural network. So if you think that a full dropout results in adverse effects for your model’s neural network, then you can try this trick as well.
Max-Norm Regularization
Max-Norm is another regularization technique that is fairly popular for deep neural networks.
The way the Max-Norm regularization technique works is by intercepting the input connections for each neuron in the neural network and constraining their weights (w ) accordingly.
Like the rest of the regularization techniques, to implement the Max-Norm regularization technique using Keras, we need to use the ‘kernel_constraint ’ argument of the hidden layers;  we want to implement it to max_norm() . After specifying an appropriate value to max_norm() , the implementation process will be completed. The following lines of code demonstrate the implementation of Max-Norm regularization along with the specification of a value for max_norm() :
keras.layers.Dense(100, activation="elu", kernel_initializer="he_normal",
kernel_constraint=keras.constraints.max_norm(1.))

We started by discussing the very basics of machine-learning and detailing some cool tips and tricks improving the quality of learning as compared to a standard guide book. Every chapter was structured and designed in a way that would not only ease the readability experience for the reader but also address topics that are of the utmost importance in the modern-day and age of machine learning. In the first half, we discussed techniques, projects, and demonstrations by primarily using Scikit-Learn. While in the later parts of the book, we shifted towards using Keras and TensorFlow for large and deep neural networks that are capable of handling very complex tasks. Throughout this transition between API’s several important tips and tricks have also been given to improve the user’s understanding of the concepts and also increasing the effectiveness when practicing what they learned. The book has pertained to providing only the best content with explanations that do not rely much on jargon but easy to understand analogies, especially in the last two chapters. Moreover, the way the chapters and the contents have been strategically placed, we effectively ensured that the reader would not get lost or overwhelmed by certain topics. The priority was to explain the background concepts earlier and then move on to the foreground discussing difficult concepts.
