Introduction
How often do you sit there and wonder just what your computer is capable of? Are there any limits to what it can do? These days, computers do far more than we give them credit for, far more than just solving a few mathematical equations. In the last 50 years or so, computer programming has advanced to a level where it is now one of the top tools to help us save time and automate tasks, but that leads to another question – just how much can be automated? And how do we do it?
Could a computer look at an image and identify, say, two people walking together along a beach? Does software have the ability to make accurate medical decisions, like trained medical professionals do? And can software make stock market predictions better than any human can? Some time ago, we would have been laughed at for asking questions like this, but now, given the advancements we’ve seen, these questions can easily be answered positively, and each of those questions, an implementation if you like, has one strategy in common.
In recent times, we have seen plenty of theoretical advances. Add these to the latest technologies, and it’s easy to see why it is easy for anyone with a decent computer system to try to solve these hard problems. Well, okay, not anyone can do it but, at the end of the day, that’s what you want to do, and that’s why you are reading my book. Right?
These days, programmers don’t need to know all the tiny details of a problem to solve it. Think about the conversion of text into speech. Traditionally, we would need to understand how the human vocal cords were structured, biologically speaking, and we would need to use code that was not generalized, was domain-specific, and had-designed if we wanted to understand what was being said. Now, we can just write a piece of code that looks at multiple examples and works out the solution to the problem, provided it had sufficient examples and time.
Another example would be identifying the sentiment in a Tweet or a piece of text as negative or positive. Perhaps you want a more granular look at the text, looking for specifics that indicate what the writer loves, hates, or likes, whether they are sad, angry, or happy. We would have scanned the text in the past, trying to locate specific words or punctuation that would indicate the sentiment. Now we use algorithms.
Algorithms learn from the data they are given in much the same way as humans learn from their experiences.  We learn by reading books, seeing things, studying, talking to others, and from looking at the internet, among many other things. But how can a computer learn to think the same way a human does? While that cannot be definitively answered, the world’s best researchers have come up with lots of different intelligent programs.  In amongst all these programs, one thing has come to light – recurring patterns – and these have led to what we now call machine learning, a standardized field used for all sorts of problems.
And that is what we are going to discuss here. The book is split into four distinct sections:
  1. An introduction to machine learning, where you will learn what machine learning is, how it works, and the different approaches. We’ll talk about programming languages and also discuss deep learning and how it differs from machine learning.
  2. TensorFlow, where you will learn everything you need to learn about the popular machine learning package. You will learn what it is, its architecture, the TensorFlow components, and finish with a couple of practical examples showing how it works.
  3. Keras, where you will learn what Keras is, what backends are, and how it compares to TensorFlow. You will also learn how to install it, learn how it relates to deep learning, and see a couple of practical examples.
  4. Scikit-learn, where you will learn what it is about, how it works, installing it, and two projects for you to follow.
The idea is to give you complete examples of how all three work and, throughout, you will also start to see who they work together. There are full installation instructions and full projects for you to follow along with so if you ready to start your machine learning journey, let’s dive in.

Part One
An Introduction to Machine Learning

What is Machine Learning?
Most of us have heard of machine learning, but the vast majority still do not know what it is. I’m sure you do as you wouldn’t have picked up this book otherwise, but as a recap, here goes.
Machine learning is an Artificial Intelligence subfield. Its primary goal is to learn to understand how data is structured and then get that data into a model that humans can understand and use. That’s it, really. Except there is far more to it than that, of course.
Machine learning is a part of the computer science domain, but it has many differences from the traditional approaches. Traditional computing algorithms are made up of explicitly programmed instructions that computers use to solve problems or calculations. Machine learning differs because the algorithms are used to let the computer train on a set of data before using statistical analysis to get output values within a given range. This helps computers to use sample data to build models so that the decision-making process can be automated. That process is based entirely on data input.
Anybody who uses technology today, no matter what it is, has used machine learning. Take facial recognition, for example. By using this, Facebook and other social media platforms make it possible to tag people in photos. OCR (optical character recognition) is used to convert text images into movable text. Recommendation engines make recommendations on purchases (Amazon, eBay, etc.), on movies to watch (Amazon, Netflix, etc.), and on music (Spotify). And then we have the self-drive vehicles and even your email inbox spam filter. All of these are powered by machine learning.
This section will look at the more common methods of machine learning and common approaches. We’ll also take a quick look at the best programming languages to use. Lastly, we’ll look at deep learning and how it differs from machine learning.
This will not be a long section because I want to move on to the real focus of this book – using TensorFlow, Scikit-Learn, and Keras for machine and deep learning.
Machine Learning Methods
Machine learning tasks are classified quite broadly. The classification categories depend on the method of learning or how the learning feedback is passed to the system that needs it. Although two are used more than the third, there are three main categories – they are supervised learning, unsupervised learning, and reinforcement learning.
Supervised Learning
With supervised learning, the computer is given a set of input data. Each input has a label that states the desired output.  The idea behind supervised learning is that the model learns from the supplied data to provide predictions on unseen data. Learning data patterns on one set, it can compare the actual output with what it was taught to spot errors so that the model can be modified as needed. As such, supervised learning is all about finding patterns and using them to predict labels on unlabeled data.
Here's an example. A supervised learning algorithm is given images containing sharks and oceans. The shark images are labeled "fish," and the ocean images are labeled "water." When the supervised learning algorithm is trained on this data, it should then be able to identify unlabeled images of sharks and oceans correctly.
One of the commonest use cases for supervised learning is predicting a future event's probability using historical data. For example, it could use historical data from the stock market to predict future fluctuations or, one of the more popular uses, for filtering out spam emails. Or the machine can be fed labeled images of dogs to learn how to classify unlabeled images.
Unsupervised Learning
Unsupervised learning uses unlabeled data, which means the algorithm has to find the common patterns in the input data by itself. There is far more unlabeled data in the world than there is labeled, so the unsupervised learning methods have a much higher value than the supervised methods.
The primary function of unsupervised learning could be as simple as finding hidden patterns in data, or it could be more complex, like learning features. This allows the computer to find the representations needed for classifying the raw data.
Typically, unsupervised learning is used for transactional data. Let's say that you have a large dataset containing information about customers and their purchases. Humans are unlikely to be able to make any sense out of the data in terms of similar attributes but, when you feed the data into an unsupervised learning algorithm, it can find those attributes, such as women who purchase one item are likely to purchase some other item. In that way, businesses can draw up targeted ad campaigns to increase customer purchases.
Unsupervised learning methods are not given answers like supervised methods are. That means they can examine much more complex datasets containing apparently unrelated data and organize it meaningfully.  We often use unsupervised learning methods to detect anomalies, such as identifying fraudulent card purchases. Or recommender systems that provide suggestions on what a customer should consider buying. Going back to the example of dogs' images, an unsupervised method could take unlabeled images and classify similar images together.
Reinforcement Learning
The final learning method is reinforcement learning, which isn't used anywhere near as much as supervised or unsupervised learning. Reinforcement learning is about taking actions to ensure maximum rewards in each given situation and is used to find the best path for machines or software to take in that situation. Like unsupervised learning,  reinforcement learning is not provided with the answers upfront. Instead, the model has to learn what to do; bad decisions penalize the machine while good ones reward it, and that is how the model learns – from experience.
Machine Learning Approaches
Images from https://morioh.com/
The field of machine learning has a close relationship to computational statistics, making a background in statistics useful for those who want to understand how to use the algorithms.
If you are not familiar with statistics, perhaps your starting point should be to understand the difference between regression and correlation, as they are two of the most common machine learning techniques used for investigating relationships between variables.
At its most basic, regression looks at relationships between independent and dependent variables. Regression statistics are used to predict dependent variables when the independent ones are known, meaning regression is useful for prediction tasks. Correlation measures the association between variables that are not labeled as dependent or independent.
Machine learning approaches are continuously evolving, and new ones are being developed all the time. We’ll look at a couple of the more popular ones in use today.
K-Nearest Neighbor
K-nearest neighbor, otherwise known as KNN, is used in both regression and classification problems for pattern recognition. The K is a positive integer, usually quite small, and, in regression and classification, the input is the K number of nearest training examples in the given space.
We’ll look at classification as this is the most common use, with an output of class membership. A new object is assigned to the most common class among the nearest neighbors. So, for k = 1, the object will be assigned to the nearest neighbor class.  If it were k = 3, the object would be assigned to the three nearest classes.
The diagram below shows two types of objects – blue diamonds and orange stars. Both belong in separate classes, labeled “diamond” and “star.”
When we add another object, say a green heart, the algorithm needs to work out what class it belongs to – in other words, it has to classify it.
When k = 3 is chosen, the algorithm locates the three neighbors closes to the green heart and chooses which class it belongs to. In the diagram below, you can see that there are two stars and one diamond nearest to the heart. That means the heart is classified to the star class.
KNN is one of the most basic of all the algorithms and is classed as a “lazy learner.” This is because it will not generalize past the training data unless the system is given a specific query.
Decision Tree Learning
Decision trees are used to provide visual representations of decisions and to show the decision-making process.  They are used for predictive purposes in data mining and machine learning, mapping data observations and information about the data's target value.
Decision tree learning is used to create models for predicting a target’s value based on the input variable values.  In predictive models, the attributes determined via data observation are represented by the tree branches and the conclusions by the tree leaves.
When the source data is tasked with learning the tree, it is divided into multiple subsets based on attribute value tests. The tests are recursively repeated on each subset and, once a node subset has a value equivalent to the target value, the process is done.
Here’s an example of some of the conditions that determine whether someone should go fishing, including barometric pressure and weather conditions.
This is a simplified tree where we classify examples by sorting them through the decision tree to the right node. The classification associated with that node or leaf is then returned – in our case, it’s just a Yes or No. The tree is used to classify the daily conditions in terms of whether they are right for fishing.
A more complex tree would have more features in the dataset than this one, but it is still pretty straightforward to determine the relationships.  Decision trees require that a number of determinations must be made, such as the features needed, the conditions required for splitting the data, and understanding when the tree has reached the end and provides a clear answer.
Programming Languages
There are several computer programming languages suitable for machine learning, and when you choose one to specialize in, you should consider the current job adverts and what they list as required skills.  Indeed.com is a popular place for machine learning job ads, and the data indicates that the most popular language is Python, followed by Java, R, and then C++.
Python is undoubtedly the most popular, and much of that is down to the recent development of different deep learning frameworks, including Keras, TensorFlow, and so on. Python’s syntax is already easy to learn and read, and it can also be used for scripting as well as programming. That makes it one of the more powerful languages and the most straightforward for working directly with and pre-processing data.  Scikit-learn is one of the popular machine learning libraries and is built on Python packages such as Matplotlib, SciPy, and NumPy.
Java tends to be more used in enterprise programming, typically by developers for front-end desktop applications who are also working on enterprise-level machine learning. It doesn’t tend to be the primary language chosen by beginners to programming or for those looking to learn about machine learning, but those with a Java background do use it in machine learning applications. In terms of industry applications of machine learning, Java is used for network security more than Python is, including fraud detection and cyber-attacks.
Some of the popular Java machine learning libraries are an open-source deep learning library called Deplearning4j, a machine learning language toolkit called MALLET for use with text applications such as NLP (natural language processing,) classifying documents, clustering and topic modeling, and Weka, which is a collection of data mining algorithms.
R is another open-source language typically used in statistical computing. It has become more popular over time and tends to be used more by academics. It isn’t usually used for industrial environments, but recent advances in data science have lifted its popularity. Some of its more popular machine learning packages include caret (Classification and Regression Training,) for predictive models, e1071, which contains statistical and probability theory functions, and randomForest, used for regression and classification.
Lastly, C++ is typically used in AI and ML for robotic or gaming applications. It is favored by electronics engineers and developers of embedded computing hardware for machine learning because they have a high level of proficiency in the language. Some of the top C++ machine learning libraries are mlpack, Dlib, which offers lots of algorithms, and Shark, which is modular and open-source.

Deep Learning
Deep learning tries to imitate the way the human brain works to process information, such as light stimuli into vision and sound stimuli into hearing. The architecture is based on the neural networks in the human brain and they are made up of layers in an ANN (Artificial Neural Network) that consists of GPUs and hardware.
Deep learning makes use of nonlinear processing units for the extraction or transformation of the data features. The output from one layer goes to the next layer as its input, and so on until the final layer. Deep learning algorithms can be supervised for classification or unsupervised for pattern recognition and analysis.
Out of all the algorithms used and developed for machine learning, deep learning ones take in more data and have even beaten humans in some tasks. That is why deep learning is now the go-to approach with the most potential in artificial intelligence.
Deep learning has also produced significant advances in speech recognition and computer vision, with one of the most popular examples being IBM Watson.
Deep Learning Vs. Machine Learning
One of the easiest ways of understanding the difference between the two is this  - deep learning IS machine learning. To be more specific, it is a machine learning evolution, using neural networks we can program so that machines can make more accurate decisions without human intervention.
The most basic definition of machine learning is:
"Algorithms that parse data, learn from that data, and then apply what they've learned to make informed decisions."
On-demand music or video streaming services are easy machine learning examples. The services use algorithms to recommend movies and music to users based on their listening or viewing activity. They also associate yours with other users' preferences, too, marching you with those who have similar tastes. We often hear this technique called AI, and it is used in lots of services that provided automatic recommendations – Amazon, eBay, Netflix, Spotify, and more.
Machine learning is the power behind many automated tasks across many industries, including finance companies, data security, healthcare, and more. The algorithms are being constantly programmed to learn to simulate human thinking – virtual personal assistants if you like.
Machine learning also involves a ton of complex coding and mathematics. When it comes down to it, these power mechanical functions in the same way that a car, a flashlight, or a computer screen does. When you hear someone say that something is "capable of machine learning," they mean that it can take the data provided to it, perform a function, and gradually get better at it.
However, things get really interesting in terms of machine learning when we bring deep learning into the equation, especially deep neural networks.
How Deep Learning and Machine Learning Differ
Practically speaking, like machine learning is a subset of AI, so deep learning is a subset of machine learning. In technical terms, it IS machine learning, and it works in much the same way. That's why people tend to use the two terms interchangeably, but there are differences in their capabilities.
A basic machine learning model can progressively improve its function, and that means it still needs guidance. When an AI algorithm returns a wrong prediction, a data engineer must make the required adjustments.  Deep learning algorithms can work alone to see if predictions are right or not, using their own neural networks.
Let's use a flashlight as an example. We could program it to turn itself on whenever it hears the word "dark" given as a command. In time, it could learn to switch on whenever it hears the word in any phrase. That's machine learning. In deep learning, the flashlight would work out that it could turn itself on when it hears something like "the light switch has stopped working" or "I can't see." In short, deep learning models learn through their own computing methods, making it look as though it has a brain.
How Deep Learning Works
Deep learning models are continually analyzing logically structured data in the same way that a human draws conclusions about things. This is done via a structure of layered algorithms known as an artificial neural network. The ANN design is based on the human brain's neural network, and it allows for a learning process that is way more capable than your average machine learning algorithm.
Making sure a deep learning model doesn't make the wrong predictions is not easy, as it requires a great deal of training to get the process right. But when it is right deep, when it does do what it was intended for, deep learning is seen as a marvel, one that many see as AIs true backbone.
One of the best examples of deep learning is AlphaGo. It's a Google-created program with a neural network, trained to play Go, an abstract board game known for needing intuition and intellect.  The program was pitted against professional human players and learned how to play at a high level, higher than anything ever seen in AI before. It also learned without being told when to make a move – most machine learning models would need to be told when to do it.  When AlphaGo beat several professional Go masters, it caused something of a stir. Not only had a machine learned the complex aspects and techniques of the game, but it had also become one of the best players in the world.
As a recap, the differences are:
  • Machine learning algorithms are used for parsing data. They learn from it and make decisions based on their learning.
  • Deep learning algorithms are structured in layers, creating an ANN or artificial neural network that learns on its own and can make intelligent decisions.
  • Deep learning is a machine learning subset and, while they both fall under AI, deep learning is the power behind the human-like AI.
Here we reviewed what machine learning and deep learning are, common approaches, programming languages and more. You need to keep in mind that machine learning is a continuous innovation and the methods, approaches and algorithms are continually changing.
Let’s move on to looking at TensorFlow in depth.

Part Two
An Introduction to TensorFlow

What is TensorFlow ?
Images from https://guru99.com
TensorFlow is the most famous and most popular library worldwide for deep learning. Developed by Google, it is used in all Google products to improve translation, searches, recommendations, image captioning and more. Take the Google search engine, for example. Powered by AI, it returns speedy results and also suggests search terms. I.e. if you type in “data” it will predict that you want to look for “data science.”
Google incorporates machine learning because they have humongous databases that they can use to provide their users with the best possible experience.
Three primary groups of people use machine learning:
  • Researchers
  • Data scientists
  • Programmers.
Each group can share data toolsets, collaborating to improve their efficiency.
It’s fair to say that Google has the biggest computer in the world, jam-packed with data, so they built TensorFlow to scale. Developed by the Google Brain Team, it is a library designed to make machine learning faster, along with deep neural network research.
It runs on multiple GPUs or CPUs, works on mobile systems, and has a number of wrappers in different languages, such as Python, Java and C++.
TensorFlow History
In the last couple of years, deep learning has massively outperformed any other machine learning algorithm that works on huge amounts of data. Google saw this and discovered that it could use this to make improvements to its services, including the Google search engine, Google Photo and Gmail.
To facilitate this, they built the TensorFlow framework, allowing developers and researchers to collaborate on an artificial intelligence model. It was late in 2015 before it was first made public, but it was 2017 before the first properly stable version was released. TensorFlow is an open-source library distributed under an Apache Open Source License, which means anyone can use it, redistribute it and modify it, all free.
TensorFlow Architecture
There are three parts to the TensorFlow architecture:
  • Data pre-processing
  • Model building
  • Training and estimating the model
The reason it was called TensorFlow is that it can take a tensor as its input. A tensor is a multi-dimensional array and operational flowcharts, otherwise known as graphs, can be constructed to perform on the tensor. The input is fed in at one end, flows through the multiple operations and then exits as an output at the other end. In short, the tensor flows through the system, hence the name.
Where Can TensorFlow Run?
We can classify the TensorFlow software and hardware requirements into two phases. The first is the development phase, where the model is trained and this is typically on a laptop or computer. The second phase is the interference or run phase where, once it has been trained, TensorFlow can run on different platforms, including:
  • Desktops or laptops on macOS, Windows or Linux
  • Cloud as a Web Service
  • Mobile devices
TensorFlow can be trained on more than one machine, and then the trained model can be run on a completely different machine, including GPUs and CPUs. The GPU was originally designed to be used with video games, but in 2010, Stanford researchers discovered that it was also suitable for algebra and matrix operations. That meant the GPU was incredibly fast and idea for these types of calculation, especially as deep learning has a strong reliance on matrix multiplication.  TensorFlow is written in C++, which makes it incredibly fast for matrix computations and, although C++ is used for the implementation, other languages, typically Python, can access and control it.
The last main feature is the TensorBoard, which enables the user to graphically visualize TensorFlow at work.
TensorFlow Components
The two primary components of TensorFlow are:
Tensor
The tensor is the main framework of TensorFlow, and every computation in it will involve these tensors. So, what is a tensor? It is a matric or a vector with n-dimensions representing all data types. All the tensor’s values have the same data type with a shape that is known or at least partly known, and that data shape is array or matrix’s dimensionality.
Tensors can originate from two places – the input data or as the result of a computation. All TensorFlow operations take place inside a graph, which is a computation set where all operations are successive. The operations are connected and are known as op nodes.
The graph outlines the ops and the node connections, but the values are not displayed. The tensor is the edge of the nodes and is a way of populating the operation with all the data.
Graphs
TensorFlow also uses a graph framework, and this is where the sequence of the computations carried out during training are gathered together and described. The graph is highly portable, which means the computations can be preserved for immediate use or kept for later.
Tensors consist of nodes and edges. The node is where the mathematical operation is and where we get the output from the endpoints. The edges explain the input and the output relationships between each node.
Why TensorFlow is So Popular
TensorFlow is popular because it is accessible by anyone and everyone. It has multiple APIs built to scale ANNs, CNNs and other deep learning architectures. Plus, because TensorFlow is based on graphs, the developer can use TensorBoard to see how the neural networks are constructed and this also helps with debugging.
Top TensorFlow Algorithms
TensorFlow has APIs built-in for the following algorithms:
  • Boosted tree classification: tf.estimator.BoostedTreesClassifier
  • Booster tree regression: tf.estimator.BoostedTreesRegressor
  • Classification:tf.estimator.LinearClassifier
  • Deep learning classification: tf.estimator.DNNClassifier
  • Deep learning wipe and deep: tf.estimator.DNNLinearCombinedClassifier
Linear regression: tf.estimator.LinearRegressor

Simple Example of TensorFlow
import numpy as np
import tensorflow as tf
It really couldn’t be simpler. In those two simple code lines, we imported TensorFlow as tf. In Python, you will note that we commonly shorten the library names and the only advantage to that is to avoid having to type the full library name when we want to use it. For example, TensorFlow is imported as tf and when we want a TensorFlow function, we can simply call tf. The same goes for NumPy, another popular Python machine learning library.
Let’s see another simple example that shows you how TensorFlow works. We want to multiply two numbers and we need a computational graph to do that. In the example, X_1 will be multiplied with X_2. TensorFlow will connect the operation by creating a node and we’ve called it “multiply.” Once the graph has been determined, the computational engines in TensorFlow will multiply those numbers for us.
The last thing we need to do is get a TensorFlow session running a computational graph with those values and print the multiplication result.
We need to define our input nodes. Whenever a node is created in TensorFlow, we much choose the node type to create. X1 and X2 are going to be placeholders and, whenever a new calculation is made, the placeholders assign new values. Our placeholders are going to be created as TF dot placeholder nodes.
Step One – Defining the Variable
When the placeholder node is created, we need to pass the data type in. As we are using numbers, we use the floating-point type, which you can see as tf.float32. The node also needs to be named. When we examine a graphical representation of the model, those are the names we see.  So we named ours X_1 and X_2 by passing parameters with the name values into each one. 
X_1 = tf.placeholder(tf.float32, name = "X_1")
X_2 = tf.placeholder(tf.float32, name = "X_2")
Step Two – Defining the Computation
Next, we define the node responsible for doing the multiplication, and that is done in TensorFlow with a tf.multiply node. X_1 and X_2 are passed to the multiplication node, which tells TensorFlow that the nodes need to be linked in the graph. In short, we are requesting that it gets the values from the two specified nodes and then multiply them. We also need to name the multiplication node; we called ours “multiply.” All of this defines the computational graph.
multiply = tf.multiply(X_1, X_2, name = "multiply")
Step Three – Executing the Operation
For graph operations to be executed, a session must be created, and this is done by using tf.session. Once the session has been created, we can call session – this tells it we want the operations run on the graph, using the run command.
When we run the addition operation, it will see that the X_1 and X_2 node values are required, which means we need to give it the values using the feed.dict command, passing the X_1 value as 1,2,3 and the X_2 value as 4,5,6. Finally, the results are printed. The multiplication operations are 1x4, 2x5 and 3x6 so we should see an output of 4, 10, 18.
Here’s the whole code from start to finish:
multiply = tf.multiply(X_1, X_2, name = "multiply")
with tf.Session() as session:
result = session.run(multiply, feed_dict={X_1:[1,2,3], X_2: [4,5,6]})
print(result)
[ 4. 10. 18.]
Loading the Data into TensorFlow
Before you can even think about training any machine learning algorithm, the data needs to be loaded and there are two ways to do this:
  1. Load into Memory is the simplest and most common method where all the data is fed as one array into memory. This is done by writing a short code in Python which is not related to TensorFlow.
  2. Use a TensorFlow Pipeline – In TensorFlow, there is an API for helping load data, carry out the operation and feed the algorithm, all rolled into one. This is a good method to use when working with huge datasets, such as image datasets, which are often too large to go into memory. The pipeline will do the memory management for you.
So, which one should you use?
  1. Loading the data in memory – this is the best method to use if your dataset is small, less than 10 GB in size, as the data easily fits into memory. One of the best libraries to use is Pandas as this helps you import the CSV files.
  2. Using the TensorFlow pipeline – if your dataset is huge, use this method. For example, if your computer has 16 GB of memory and you are trying to import 50 GB of data, it won’t work; all you succeed in doing is crashing your computer.
For the latter method you would need to build a pipeline and use it to load small batches of the data at a time. Each batch goes into the pipeline and is gotten ready for training on. This is a great solution because you can use something called parallel computing, which means TensorFlow trains the model across several CPUs. This helps in training very powerful neural nets.
Now we’ll look at how to build a pipeline.
Building a TensorFlow Pipeline
In our earlier example, we added the X_1 and X_2 values manually. Now we need to see how a pipeline is built to load the data into TensorFlow.
Step One – Creating the data
First, we can use NumPy to generate a pair of random values:
import numpy as np
x_input = np.random.sample((1,2))
print(x_input)
[[0.8835775 0.23766977]]
Step Two – Creating the Placeholder
As in our first example, the placeholder is created and named X. The Tensor shape must be explicitly specified so we load an array containing just two values and the shape is written as shape=[1,2].
# use placeholder
x = tf.placeholder(tf.float32, shape=[1,2], name = 'X')
Step Three – Defining the Dataset Method
Next, the dataset needs to be defined, and this is where the placeholder value will be populated. To do this, we use the following method:
dataset = tf.data.Dataset.from_tensor_slices(x)
Step Four – Creating the Pipeline
Now the pipeline needs to be initialized. This is where the data flows, so we need an iterator. We use make_initializable_iterator and give it the name of “iterator.” This iterator is called to feed in the data batches, one at a time, so we use get_next. This is just a simple example, using one data batch containing two values.
iterator = dataset.make_initializable_iterator()
get_next = iterator.get_next()
Step Five – Executing the Operation
This is much like our first example. A session is initiated and the operation iterator is run. We give feed_dict the NumPy-generated values, which then populate the placeholder. Lastly, we print the result using get_next:
with tf.Session() as sess:
# feed placeholder with data
sess.run(iterator.initializer, feed_dict={ x: x_input })
print(sess.run(get_next)) # output [ 0.52374458  0.71968478]
[0.8835775  0.23766978]
That was just a simple example of how TensorFlow works. Things will get a little more complicated, but first, we need to take time out to show you how to install TensorFlow on your Windows or Mac computer.

Installing TensorFlow
The easiest way to install TensorFlow is to use a Python distribution called Anaconda. Once we have installed it, we will look at how to use it with Jupyter, a popular notebook viewer.
First, TensorFlow will work across multiple GPUs and CPUs which means we can distribute the computations across multiple devices, thus speeding up the training. By using parallelization, you eliminate the need to wait days or weeks for the algorithm results.
There are two Windows version of TensorFlow:
  • CPU Support Only – this is the only version you can use if your computer does not have a NVIDIA GPU.
  • GPU Support – if you do have a NVIDIA GPU and you need the capacity for strong computation, this version is much faster.
We will be using the first, the CPU support version, as it is more than sufficient for the purposes of this tutorial. Also, there is no GPU Support version for the Mac.
Here goes – read through this first, before you start following it, as some is theory and some is practical instruction.
Install Anaconda
We’re going to look first at how to install Anaconda on Windows and macOS.
Windows
  1. Go to https://www.anaconda.com/download/#windows and download the Anaconda installer and save it to your desktop
  2. Find and double-click on the installer to launch it – do NOT do this from your favorites folder as it will cause permission errors
  3. Click on Next on the launcher window
  4. Read and click I Agree on the terms and conditions
  5. Unless you are installing for all system users, choose to install for Just Me on the next window and choose where it will be installed
  6. Click on Next
  7. On the next window, you have two boxes you can potentially check – the first asks if you want Anaconda added to your PATH environment variable. We don’t recommend this as it tends to interfere with some other software on your computer, so don’t check this box. Instead, you should use Anaconda by using the Start Menu prompt or Anaconda Navigator.
  8. The second check box asks if you want Anaconda registered as the default Python. Check this box unless you are intending to run more than one version of Python or Anaconda.
  9. Now click on Install. If you want to see which packages are being installed, click on Show Details, or just leave it to install by itself
  10. Click on Next and you get the option of installing PyCharm; for now just click Next on this window
  11. Once Anaconda is installed, you get a dialog box thanking you for installing it.  Click on Finish.
Now you need to verify your installation and there are two ways to do this – using Anaconda Navigator or conda.
Navigator
This is a GUI (graphical user interface) that gets installed alongside Anaconda. Go to your Start menu and type in Anaconda Navigator.  If it opens, Anaconda was successfully installed. If it doesn’t, the installation was not successful. In that case, temporarily disable your antivirus software and run through the steps above again. Don’t forget to re-enable it again afterward.
Conda
This one is for those of you who like to use the CLI (command-line interface). Click on your Start menu and type in Anaconda Prompt. From here, you have a choice of methods to check the installation. At the command prompt:
  • Type in conda list.  If the installation was successful, you will see the installed packages listed along with their version numbers; OR
  • Type python at the prompt. The Python shell should run if Anaconda is installed, and you should see information about the version. Enter quit() to close the shell; OR
  • Type anaconda-navigator at the prompt to open Anaconda; it will only open if the installation was successful.
Mac
You have a choice of two methods to install Anaconda on Mac – the graphical installer or command line. If you are not sure which one to use, go for the graphical installer.
Graphical Install
  1. Go to https://www.anaconda.com/downloads#macos and download the correct installer for the Python version you want
  2. Double-click the file icon and start the installation by clicking on Continue
  3. Click through the screens for Introduction, Read Me and License, agreeing to each one
  4. Click Install on the next window – this will install Anaconda into the ~/opt directory, which is the recommended one
  5. A Destination Select screen opens, click on Install for Me Only
  6. Click on Continue  and then click Continue on the PyCharm screen
  7. You will be greeted with the Thank You For Installing screen when Anaconda is installed.
Command-Line Install
This is for those who prefer using the terminal window
  1. Open your browser and go to https://www.anaconda.com/downloads#macos
  2. Download the command-line version
  3. Open your terminal and type in the following command, replacing filename with your own path and filename:
shasum -a 256 /path/filename
  1. Now decide whether you are installing Python 3.7 or 2.7. For 3.7, type in the following command:
bash ~/Downloads/Anaconda3-2020.02-MacOSX-x86_64.sh
and for 2.7, type in this command:
bash ~/Downloads/Anaconda2-2019.10-MacOSX-x86_64.sh
Make sure, in both cases, that you replace Downloads with your own path and Anaconda3-2020.02-MacOSX-x86_64.sh with the name of the file that was downloaded.
  1. You will now see an installer prompt asking you to look at the license terms – click Enter, scroll down to the bottom and then click on Yes
  2. Now the installer requests that you confirm the location by pressing the enter key. You have two choices – agree to the default, in which case you will see PREFIX=/home/<user>/anaconda<2 or 3>. If you want to change the default, press CTRL+C and input the new directory. The former is recommended.
  3. The installer will now ask you if you want to run conda init to initialize Anaconda3 – click on Yes
  4. Wait for the installation to finish and you will see the “thank you for installing” window.
  5. Close the installer and open the terminal window so the installation can come into effect.
Now decide if you want each shell session to have an activated base environment. If you do, run the following command at the prompt:
conda config --set auto_activate_base False or True
If you prefer conda to run from anywhere, without the base activated, type in this:
conda init
conda config --set auto_activate_base False
Lastly, the installation must be verified, using Anaconda Navigator or conda.
Navigator
  • Click on Launchpad and pick Anaconda Navigator; OR
    press CMD+Space to launch Spotlight Search; type in Navigator and the program opens
Conda
  • Press CMD+Space to launch Spotlight Search and type Navigator
Now use one of the following to verify the installation:
  • Type in conda list.  If the installation was successful, you will see the installed packages listed along with their version numbers; OR
  • Type python at the prompt. The Python shell should run if Anaconda is installed, and you should see information about the version. Enter quit() to close the shell; OR
  • Type anaconda-navigator at the prompt to open Anaconda; it will only open if the installation as successful.
Install TensorFlow
Step One – Locate Anaconda
Windows
The first step is to find the Anaconda path. We need a conda environment where all the required libraries will reside. Open the Anaconda prompt and type in:
C:\>where anaconda
We want to know the folder name where we installed Anaconda because that path is where the new environment will be installed.
Mac OS
Open your Terminal and type the following at the command prompt:
which anaconda
Step Two – Setting the Working Directory
We want to create a folder in Anaconda where TensorFlow, Jupyter and IPython will be installed and the quickest way to do this is to create a .yml file. First, the working directory must be specified where you want the file created.
Mac OS
The terminal will automatically set the default working directory as Users/USERNAME and, typically, the working directory and anaconda3 paths are identical. You will see a $ sign – the folder before this is the latest one, and the terminal installs the libraries here.
If the text editor path isn’t the same as the working directory, change it by going to the terminal and typing
cd anaconda3
Windows
Type in
cd C:\Users\Admin\Anaconda3
or the path that the "where anaconda" command provides.
Please note that, where it says Admin in the above command, you should insert the folder it is in on your system.
Step Three- Creating the .yml File
The .yml file can be created in the working directory and will be responsible for installing the dependencies needed for TensorFlow:
Mac OS
Type this command in the terminal:
touch hello-tf.yml
You should see the new file, called hello-tf.yml, in anaconda3.
Windows
Type this command at the prompt:
echo.>hello-tf.yml
and you will see the new file appear
Step Four – Editing the .yml File
Mac OS
Now we can edit the .yml file, using vim. Type this in the terminal:
vi hello-tf.yml
This will put you into edit mode and once you press the esc key, you can do any of the following:
  • Press the i key to edit
  • Press the w key to save
  • Press the q key to quit.
Write the code below in edit mode and then press the esc key and the w key. Be aware that everything is intend and case sensitive and there must be two spaces are each intend:
name: hello-tfdependencies:
- python=3.6
- jupyter
- ipython
- pandas
- pip:     
- https://storage.googleapis.com/tensorflow/MacOS/cpu/tensorflow-1.5.0-py3-none-any.whl
Don’t forget to press the esc key and then w to save it; then press esc and q to quit.
Windows
As Windows has no vim program, you can use Notepad for this:
notepad hello-tf.yml
Type this into the file:
name: hello-tfdependencies:
- python=3.6
- jupyter
- ipython
- pandas
Step Five – Compiling the .yml File
Type the following at the prompt:
conda env create -f hello-tf.yml
The environment will be created in the current directory.
Step Six – Activating Conda
We are nearly there. You should now have two conda environments – an isolated one for the libraries and a default one. Every machine learning project you do will require different libraries so it is good practice to set up an isolated one. Once your project is done, you can choose whether to keep the isolated environment or delete it.
conda env list
When you run that, the default environment is indicated by the asterisk and, to activate it, you must switch to hello-tf:
Mac OS
source activate hello-tf
Windows
activate hello-tf
When you do this, make sure all the dependencies are listed where they should be in the same environment. This is an important point – Python needs to be able to use TensorFlow and Jupyter from the same environment and if all three are not listed together, you have to start over.
Mac OS
which python
which jupyter
which ipython
You can also check to see if TensorFlow needs updating by inputting the following command:
pip install --upgrade tensorflow
Windows
where python
where jupyter
where ipython
Now we have two Python environments – the primary environment and the one we just created as hello-tf. In the main conda environment, you will only find the heel-tf environment, not the primary one.
To install TensorFlow on Windows, use the following command:
pip install tensorflow
Launching Jupyter Notebook
This is the same for both Windows and Mac OS. Be aware that, when you want TensorFlow opened, the environment must be initialized. This is what we are going to do:
  • Activate hello-tf conda environment
  • Open Jupyter
  • Import tensorflow
  • Delete Notebook
  • Close Jupyter
Step One – Activating Conda
Type the following commands at the respective prompts:
Mac
source activate hello-tf
Windows
conda activate hello-tf
Step Two – Opening Jupyter
Now, Jupyter can be opened. At the prompt, type in:
jupyter notebook
If your browser doesn’t automatically open, copy the URL you see at the prompt and paste it into your browser. It should start with http://localhost.8888
When the Jupyter Notebook opens, you will see the working directory and all its files. Creating a new notebook is as simple as clicking the New button, followed by Python 3. All new notebooks are saved in the working directory.
Step Three – Importing TensorFlow
TensorFlow can now be imported in Jupyter using the tf alias. Click on Run and a new cell gets created, where you type:
import tensorflow as tf
Time to write a line of code using TensorFlow. Type in the following:
hello = tf.constant('Hello, HappyMonday!')
hello
Here, we created a tensor – you have successfully installed TensorFlow through Jupyter on your computer.
Step Four – Deleting the File
If you want, the file called Untitled.ipynb can be deleted from Jupyter. Just check the box next to it and click the delete button.
Step Five – Closing Jupyter
You can close Jupyter in two ways. First, you can do it from the Notebook and, second, by using the Anaconda prompt or the terminal.
Jupyter
From the main screen in the Jupyter click the  Logout button and you will be taken to the logout page.
Terminal/Anaconda Prompt
From the prompt or terminal, run CTRL+C twice.
On the first time, you need to confirm that you want the Notebook shut down. The second CTRL+C confirms it.

TensorFlow Basics
Now you know how to get TensorFlow on your computer, let’s take a look at some of the basics of the library. We’ll give you a recap on what TensorFlow is and then go into detail about how it works and what it can do.
What is a Tensor?
TensorFlow gets its name from the tensor, which is the core framework of the library. Tensors are involved in every TensorFlow computation but what is a tensor? As we explained earlier, it is a matrix or vector of n-dimensions representing all data types. Every tensor value has the same data type as a known or partly known shape and the data shape is the array or matrix’s dimensionality.
Tensors can come from two places – a computation’s result or the input data. All TensorFlow operations take place in a graph which is a series of successive computations. Each TensorFlow operation is an op node and these are all connected. The graph shows the ops and their connections but it won’t show you their values. The node edge is the tensor and is a way of populating the operation with the data.
In machine learning, we feed models with feature vectors, which are lists of objects. These vectors may be of any data type but they are the primary method of input for populating tensors. The object values flow through the tensor to the op node and a new tensor is created as a result of the computation. The new tensor will then get used in a new operation and all of these can be visualized in the graph.
How Tensors are Represented
We already know that a tensor is an array or matrix of feature vectors of n-dimensions. So, if our matrix was 2x3, and it contained the values 1 through 6, we would write it as:
It would be represented in TensorFlow as:
[[1, 2, 3],
[4, 5, 6]]
If we had a 3-dimensional matrix containing the values 1 through 8, we would have:
And TensorFlow would represent it as:
[ [[1, 2],
[[3, 4],
[[5, 6],
[[7,8] ]
It’s worth bearing in mind that tensors may use scalars to represent them or their shape may be four or more dimensions but it is far harder for higher-dimensions levels to be visualized.
Tensor Types
Every TensorFlow computation flows through at least one tensor and each tensor has three properties:
  • Name – a unique label
  • Shape – its dimension
  • dtype – its data type
Each TensorFlow operation requires a tensor to be manipulated and you can create four primary tensors:
  • tf.Variable
  • tf.constant
  • tf.placeholder
  • tf.SparseTensor
We’re going to look at creating two of those – the tf.Variable and tf.constant. Before we start, you need to ensure that the conda environment has been activated with TensorFlow. Don’t forget; we named it hello-tf. Here’s how to activate it:
Mac
source activate hello-tf
Windows
activate hello-tf
Now we can import TensorFlow:
# Import tf
import tensorflow as tf
Step One – Creating a Tensor of n-dimension
We start by creating one tensor with a single dimension, otherwise known as a scalar. We do this using tf.constant():
tf.constant(value, dtype, name = "")
arguments
- `value`: Value of n dimension to define the tensor. Optional
- `dtype`: Define data type:   
- `tf.string`: String variable   
- `tf.float32`: Flot variable   
- `tf.int16`: Integer variable
- "name": Name of the tensor. Optional. By default, `Const_1:0`
The following code will help you create a tensor with a dimension of 0:
## rank 0
# Default name
r1 = tf.constant(1, tf.int16)
print(r1)
Output
Tensor("Const:0", shape=(), dtype=int16)
# Named my_scalar
r2 = tf.constant(1, tf.int16, name = "my_scalar")
print(r2)
Output
Tensor("my_scalar:0", shape=(), dtype=int16)
The tensor name is used to display each one and we define the tensor objects with the name, shape and dtype.
Tensors can be defined with a string or decimal values by changing the data type.
# Decimal
r1_decimal = tf.constant(1.12345, tf.float32)
print(r1_decimal)
# String
r1_string = tf.constant("HappyMonday", tf.string)
print(r1_string)
Output
Tensor("Const_1:0", shape=(), dtype=float32)
Tensor("Const_2:0", shape=(), dtype=string)
A tensor of dimension 1 can be created as follows:
## Rank 1r1_vector = tf.constant([1,3,5], tf.int16)
print(r1_vector)
r2_boolean = tf.constant([True, True, False], tf.bool)
print(r2_boolean)
Output
Tensor("Const_3:0", shape=(3,), dtype=int16)
Tensor("Const_4:0", shape=(3,), dtype=bool)
You can notice the shape is only composed of 1 column.
If you want an array with 2 dimensions, the brackets at the end of each row must be closed. Here are some examples:
## Rank 2
r2_matrix = tf.constant([ [1, 2],
[3, 4] ],tf.int16)
print(r2_matrix)
Output
Tensor("Const_5:0", shape=(2, 2), dtype=int16)
Our matrix now has 2 columns and 2 rows and the values of 1, 2, 3, 4. TO make a 3-dimensional matrix, we add another level, not forgetting to use the brackets:
## Rank 3
r3_matrix = tf.constant([ [[1, 2],
[3, 4],
[5, 6]] ], tf.int16)
print(r3_matrix)
Output

Tensor("Const_6:0", shape=(1, 3, 2), dtype=int16)
Tensor Shapes
When the tensor is printed, TensorFlow will guess what the shape is but you can use the shape property to get the actual shape. Here, we are going to build a matrix with the values 10 through 15 and then check the m_shape shape:
# Shape of tensor
m_shape = tf.constant([ [10, 11],
[12, 13],
[14, 15] ]                     
)
m_shape.shape
Output
TensorShape([Dimension(3), Dimension(2)])
Our matrix has 2 columns and 3 rows.
TensorFlow contains so commands that help you create a matrix or an array filled with 1 or 0. For example, if you wanted a 1-d tensor with a shape of 10 and filled with 0, you would use this code:
# Create a vector of 0
print(tf.zeros(10))
Output
Tensor("zeros:0", shape=(10,), dtype=float32)
The shape property will also work with matrices too, so here’s how to create a matrix of 10x10, filled with 1:
# Create a vector of 1
print(tf.ones([10, 10]))
Output
Tensor("ones:0", shape=(10, 10), dtype=float32)
The shape of any specified matrix can be used to create a vector of one. The m_shape of the matrix is 3x2 and the tensor will have 3 rows filled with 1 – this is the code:
# Create a vector of ones with the same number of rows as m_shape
print(tf.ones(m_shape.shape[0]))
Output
Tensor("ones_1:0", shape=(3,), dtype=float32)
If the value 1 is passed to the bracket, we can create a vector of ones that equals how many columns are in m_shape for the matrix:
# Create vector of ones with the same number of column as m_shape
print(tf.ones(m_shape.shape[1]))
Output
Tensor("ones_2:0", shape=(2,), dtype=float32)
Lastly, you can also create a 3x2 matrix filled with one’s:
print(tf.ones(m_shape.shape))
Output

Tensor("ones_3:0", shape=(3, 2), dtype=float32)
Data Types
Another property of a tensor is the data type and each tensor can only hold one data type at any time. We use the dtype property to return the data type:
print(m_shape.dtype)
Output
<dtype: 'int32'>
Sometimes, you may need to change the data type and we can do that in TensorFlow using the tf.cast method:
Here’s an example:
In the code below, we convert a float tensor into an integer using tf.cast
# Change type of data
type_float = tf.constant(3.123456789, tf.float32)
type_int = tf.cast(type_float, dtype=tf.int32)
print(type_float.dtype)
print(type_int.dtype)
Output
<dtype: 'float32'>
<dtype: 'int32'>
TensorFlow automatically picks the data type if we don’t specify the argument when the tensor is created. It will guess at the likely data type, for example, if text is passed, it guesses it is a string and converts it as such.
Useful TensorFlow Operators
Now we know how to create tensors, we should turn our attention to mathematical operations. You can find all the basic ones in TensorFlow ad we’ll start with an easy one, using TensorFlow to work out the square of a given number. This is easy because the tensor is constructed with just one argument. We use tf.sqrt(x) and x represents a floating number.
x = tf.constant([2.0], dtype = tf.float32)
print(tf.sqrt(x))
Output
Tensor("Sqrt:0", shape=(1,), dtype=float32)
Note that the output is not what you would expect – you think it would the square of 2 result but it is a tensor object. What the example has printed is the tensor definition and not the operation evaluation. Shortly, we will look at how TensorFlow executes the operations but, first, here’s a list of the common operations, each requiring at least one argument:
  • tf.add(a, b)
  • tf.div(a, b)
  • tf.exp(a)
  • tf.multiply(a, b)
  • tf.pow(a, b)
  • tf.sqrt(a)
  • tf.substract(a, b)
Here’s an example:
# Add
tensor_a = tf.constant([[1,2]], dtype = tf.int32)
tensor_b = tf.constant([[3, 4]], dtype = tf.int32)
tensor_add = tf.add(tensor_a, tensor_b)print(tensor_add)
Output
Tensor("Add:0", shape=(1, 2), dtype=int32)
What we did here was created two tensors, one containing 1 and 2 and one containing 3 and 4. Then we added the two tensors together. Keep in mind that, when you do this, all the tensors you are adding must be the same shape.
We can also do multiplication operations on two tensors:
# Multiply
tensor_multiply = tf.multiply(tensor_a, tensor_b)
print(tensor_multiply)
Output

Tensor("Mul:0", shape=(1, 2), dtype=int32)             
Variables
Up to now, we have concentrated on constant tensors but, to be fair, these are not always of the best use. That is because data comes in all kinds of types and values and capturing that requires us to use the Variable class. This represents nodes where values are always changing.
We need to use the tf.get_variable method to do this:
tf.get_variable(name = "", values, dtype, initializer)
argument
- `name = ""`: Variable name
- `values`: Tensor dimension
- `dtype`: Optional – data type
- `initializer`: Optional – initializing the tensor
If we specify the initializer, we don’t need to add the values because the initializer’s shape is used instead. For example, below we create a 2-d variable that contains a pair of random values. TensorFlow will return a random value by default. The variable is named as var:
# Create Variable
## Create 2 Randomized values
var = tf.get_variable("var", [1, 2])
print(var.shape)
Output
(1, 2)
Next, we will create a variable that has two columns and one row and, to create the variable’s dimension, we use [1,2]. This tensor has an initial value of zero. When a model is trained, you must provide the initial values so the weight of the features can be computed. In this case, we set them to zero:
var_init_1 = tf.get_variable("var_init_1", [1, 2], dtype=tf.int32,  initializer=tf.zeros_initializer)
print(var_init_1.shape)
Output
(1, 2)
A constant tensor’s values can be passed in a variable and a constant tensor is created using the tf.constant() method. This tensor can be used for the variable initialization. The first of the variable’s values are 10, 20, 30, 40 and the shape of the new tensor is 2x2:
# Create 2x2 matrixtensor_const = tf.constant([[10, 20],
[30, 40]])
# Initialize the first value of the tensor equals to tensor_const
var_init_2 = tf.get_variable("var_init_2", dtype=tf.int32,  initializer=tensor_const)
print(var_init_2.shape)
Output
(2, 2)
Placeholders
Placeholders have one simple job – feeding the tensor. We use them to initialize the data we want flowing in the tensors. Supplying a placeholder requires that you use the feed_dict method, and each tensor is only fed within the session they are created in. The syntax you need to use is:
tf.placeholder(dtype,shape=None,name=None )
arguments:
- `dtype`: Data type
- `shape`: Optional – placeholder dimension. By default, it is the data shape.
- `name`: Optional – placeholder name​ ​              
data_placeholder_a = tf.placeholder(tf.float32, name = "data_placeholder_a")
print(data_placeholder_a)​
Output
Tensor("data_placeholder_a:0", dtype=float32)​              
Session
TensorFlow has three primary components that it works around:
  • Graph
  • Session
  • Tensor
The following table describes these three components:
Components
Description
Graph
A fundamental part of TensorFlow, every mathematical operation is done in a graph. We can see the graph as the project containing all the operations, each represented by a node that can absorb new tensors or create them.
Tensor
Tensors represent the data that goes between the operations. We already looked at initializing tensors and the difference between variables and constants is that, with a variable, the initial values will change; with a constant, they stay the same.
Session
Sessions execute the graph operations. A session must be opened to feed the tensor values to the graph and, in the session, an output can only be created with the use of an operator.
Graphs and sessions remain independent of one another; a session can be run, and the values obtained can be used in later computations. In our next example, we will do the following:
  • Create a pair of tensors
  • Create one operation
  • Open a session
  • Print result
Step One – Creating the Tensors
First, we want to create two tensors called x and y:
## Create, run  and evaluate a session
x = tf.constant([2])
y = tf.constant([4])
Step Two – Creating the Operator
The operator is created by multiplying the tensors, x and y:
## Create operator
multiply = tf.multiply(x, y)
Step Three – Opening a Session
Now we need to open a session so all the computation can take place. Then you must remember to close that session:
## Create session to run code
sess = tf.Session()result_1 = sess.run(multiply)
print(result_1)
sess.close()​
Output
[8]​
Let’s break that code down:
  • sess = tf.session opens the session so that all the operations can flow
  • run(multiply) executes the operation we created earlier in step two
  • print(result_1) prints the result
  • close() closes the session
  • The output is 8, a result of x and y being multiplied
We can also create sessions inside blocks and the advantage of this is that it doesn’t matter if you forget to close the session as it will be automatically done for you:
with tf.Session() as sess:   
result_2 = multiply.eval()
print(result_2) ​
Output
[8]​
The eval() method can be used to execute operations and is much the same as using the run() method. It just makes the code easier to read.
A session can also be created to see the values of tensors you already created:
## Check tensors already created
sess = tf.Session()
print(sess.run(r1))
print(sess.run(r2_matrix))
print(sess.run(r3_matrix))​
Output
1
[[1 2]
[3 4]]
[[[1 2]
[3 4]
[5 6]]]
By default, variables are empty, even when a tensor has been created. The variable must be initialized if you want to use it and initializing a variable’s values is done using the tf.global_variables_initialzer() object. Using this explicitly initializes every variable, a helpful thing to do before training a model.
Like the tensors, you can also create a session to check on the variables you already created and run() is required for the tensor evaluation:
sess.run(tf.global_variables_initializer())
print(sess.run(var))
print(sess.run(var_init_1))
print(sess.run(var_init_2))​
Output
[[-0.05356491  0.75867283]]
[[0 0]]
[[10 20]
[30 40]]​
The placeholder you created earlier can also be used and fed with the value. The data must be passed in the feed_dict method. For example, we have a placeholder called data_placeholder_a and we want the power of 2 of the placeholder:
import numpy as np
power_a = tf.pow(data_placeholder_a, 2)
with tf.Session() as sess: 
data = np.random.rand(1, 10) 
print(sess.run(power_a, feed_dict={data_placeholder_a: data}))  # Will succeed.             
Breaking this code down:
  • import numpy as np: imports the numpy library so we can create the data.
  • tf.pow(data_placeholder_a, 2): creates the operations (ops)
  • np.random.rand(1, 10): creates a random data array
  • feed_dict={data_placeholder_a: data}: feeds the data to the placeholder.
Output
[[0.05478134 0.27213147 0.8803037  0.0398424  0.21172127 0.01444725  0.02584014 0.3763949  0.66022706 0.7565559 ]]             
Graphs
Rendering operations in TensorFlow requires that a dataflow scheme is used to represent the computations. To help with that, the dataflow graph was developed so we could see each operation’s data dependencies. Mathematical algorithms consist of a series of operations in succession and a graph conveniently shows us the coordination between the computations.
On a TensorFlow graph, you will see an edge and a node. The edge represents the tensor and can produce new ones or take the input data, while the node represents the operations. Whether a tensor produces new tensors or takes input data is entirely dependent on the individual operation dependencies.
A graph is structured to connect the nodes and show how they are feed. What you won’t see on a graph is the operation’s outputs. Let’s see this better with an example.
Let’s say that we want the following function evaluated:
f(x, z) = xz + x 2 + z + 5
TensorFlow executes this function by creating a graph which looks like this:
This clearly shows which path is taken by the tensors to get to the end. The graph is telling you that the order of operations is:
  • add 1) together
  • add to 2)
  • add 3) to
x = tf.get_variable("x", dtype=tf.int32,  initializer=tf.constant([5]))
z = tf.get_variable("z", dtype=tf.int32,  initializer=tf.constant([6]))
c = tf.constant([5], name =​ "constant")square = tf.constant([2], name =              "square")
f = tf.multiply(x, z) + tf.pow(x, square) + z + c​ ​              
Breaking this code down:
  • x: Initializes the variable called x with 5, which is a constant value
  • z: Initializes the variable called z with 6, which is a constant value
  • c: Initializes the constant tensor called c with 5, which is a constant value
  • square: Initializes the constant tensor called square with 2, which is a constant value
  • f: Constructs the operator
We have chosen to keep out variable values fixed in this example and we also created c, a constant tensor which is also the f function’s constant parameter taking a fixed, or constant, value of 5. The graph shows this tensor parameter as constant.   We also did something else that wasn’t really necessary – built a constant tensor for the power in the tf.pow() operator. We only did this so you could see it visually in the graph as the circle named square.
The next code will evaluate the function inside a session:
init = tf.global_variables_initializer() # prepare to initialize all variables
with tf.Session() as sess:   
​ init.run() # Initialize x and y   
function_result = f.eval()
print(function_result)
Output
[66]
To summarize what you learned here, TensorFlow works three components:
  • Graphs - where all the operations and tensors are computed
  • Tensors – representing the data that flows through the graphs. These are the graph edges
  • Sessions – where all the operations are executed

TensorBoard Graph Visualization Example
First off, what is TensorBoard? It is an interface used for visualizing the graph, and also provides other tools that help us understand the model, debug it and optimize it. The picture below is from the graph that you will be generating and it shows the main panel:
The image below shows the TensorBoard panel where you can see different tabs linked to the information added when the model is run:
Those tabs are:
  • Scalars – this tab shows lots of different information that is useful in the training
  • Graphs – this shows the model
  • Histogram – this helps us display the weights
  • Distribution – this helps us display the weight distribution
  • Projector – this shows the PCS (principal component analysis) and the T-SNE algorithm which is used for dimensionality reduction
What we are going to do is take a simple deep learning model and train it.
Take a look at the graph below; it shows you how the model works:
  1. The data is enqueued to the model which means we push an equal amount of data to the batch size to our model, indicating the data foods that follow each iteration.
  2. The data is fed to the tensors
  3. The model is trained
  4. The number of baches used during training is displayed.
  5. The model is saved to disk.
So, what is the idea behind TensorBoard? It is simple; it helps us inspect the neural network. It helps us understand dependencies between each operation, how weights are computed and it also shows us the loss function, among other information. Bringing all of this together gives us the best tool to debug the model and figure out how to make it better.
The image below will give you an idea of the usefulness of the graph:
Neural networks determine the best way to connect the neurons and the number of layers needed to predict the outcome.  Once the architecture has been defined, the model needs to be trained but we also need the metrics to help us compute how accurate the prediction is. This is the loss function and our objective is to minimize it, i.e. ensure fewer errors from the model. Every algorithm, regardless of what it is, will repeat the computations over and again until the loss is reduced to a flatter line. A learning rate must be defined to help minimize the loss, indicating the speed you want the model learning at. If the rate is set too high,  the model will not have enough time to do any learning, which is what you can see on the left of the picture above. The line moves up and down, which means the predictions are pure guesswork. The right side of the image indicates a decrease in the loss over each iteration until the line flattens. At that point, a solution has been found.
TensorBoard helps us see these metrics and shows where they may be any issues. Neural networks can take weeks in some cases to get to a solution and TensorBoard frequently updates the metrics. By using TensorBoard, you no longer have to wait to see if the model is training properly. Instead, just open the tool and you can see what’s going on and where changes need to be made.
Using TensorBoard
Now we are going to look at how to use TensorBoard. The first thing you need to do is import the required libraries for the training:
## Import library
import tensorflow as tf
import numpy as np
Next, the data must be created; ours is an array containing five columns and 10,000 rows:
X_train = (np.random.sample((10000,5)))
y_train =  (np.random.sample((10000,1)))
X_train.shape
Output
(10000, 5)
The codes you see below are going to transform our data and create our model. Note that we have set the learning rates as equal to 0.1. If this were higher, the models would fail to find a solution, which is what we saw in the earlier image.
Most of our TensorFlow tutorials use a TensorFlow API called the TensorFlow estimator to hold the mathematical computations.
Creating log files requires that the path is specified and we use the argument called model_dir for this. In our example,  the model will be stored in the working directory where your Python file or notebook are stored. TensorFlow creates a folder in this path and names it train, giving it a child folder called linreg:
feature_columns = [
tf.feature_column.numeric_column('x', shape=X_train.shape[1:])]
DNN_reg = tf.estimator.DNNRegressor(feature_columns=feature_columns,
# Indicate where the log file should be stored
model_dir='train/linreg',   
hidden_units=[500, 300],   
optimizer=tf.train.ProximalAdagradOptimizer(     
learning_rate=0.1,     
l1_regularization_strength=0.001   
)
)
