Part Three
An Introduction to Keras

What is Keras?
Keras is an incredibly popular open-source Python library for neural networks. It runs on TensorFlow or Theano and was designed as a modular library by Francis Chollet, an engineer for Google. It is one of the fastest and most user-friendly of all the Python libraries.
We don’t use Keras for low-level computation. Instead, Keras uses a different library called the Backend to do it. In short, Keras is a high-level API wrapper for low-level APIs that can run on Theano, TensorFlow and even CNTK.
It handles how we make models.  It handles how we define layers and how we set multiple input models up. It also helps us compile the model with loss functions and optimizer functions and the training process with a fit function. As we said, forget using it to handle low-level API stuff like making graphs, tensors and variables; it all that gets done by the backend engine.
So, what will you learn here? You will learn:
  • What Keras is
  • What the backend is, including the TensorFlow, Theano and CNTK backends
  • Keras vs. TensorFlow
  • The advantages of using Keras
  • How to install it
  • The fundamentals of Keras for deep learning
  • How to fine-tune and use pre-trained Keras models
  • Facial recognition
You will find plenty of practical examples along the way to help you understand how Keras works.
What is a Backend?
It is a Keras term, the part of Keras the performs the lower-level computations like creating the tensors, convolutions, and lots of other stuff, using libraries like TensorFlow to help. The backend engine is responsible for computing and developing the models. The default backend is TensorFlow, which suits our purposes, but there are other backends that we can change to within the configuration.
Theano, TensorFlow, and CNTK Backends
Theano was developed at the University of Montreal in Quebec, Canada by the MILA group. An open-source project, it was the very first framework to be widely used. Theano is one of the popular Python libraries that helps us with mathematical operations in multi-dimensional arrays using SciPy or NumPy. Theano makes use of GPUs for much faster computation and it can produce symbolic graphs for gradient computations automatically. The official Theano website claims that the library recognizes expressions that are numerically unstable and computes them using algorithms that are stable.
TensorFlow is fast rising in deep learning and was developed by Google Brain. It is one of the more popular tools for deep learning with plenty of features, which we already discussed in the previous section.
The final backend engine is CNTK (Microsoft Cognitive Toolkit,) an open-source framework for deep learning developed by Microsoft. CNTK is capable of running on multiple GPUs to train models on huge scales. In some cases, CNTK beat Theano and TensorFlow in terms of the reporting speed.
Keras vs. TensorFlow
The best way to show you how Keras and TensorFlow compare is this:
Parameters
Keras
TensorFlow
Type
High-level API wrapper
Low-level API
Complexity
Easy if you use Python
Some TensorFlow syntaxes must be learned
Purpose
Fast to deploy and used to make standard-layered models
Used for making computational graphs or model layers
Tools
Makes use of other API debugging tools like TDBG
Provides you with visualization tools in TensorBoard
Community
Backed by a large and active community
Backed by a large and active community and lots of shared resources.
Keras Advantages
Keras offers several advantages:
  • Quick to deploy and user-friendly – Keras is one of the quickest ways of creating network models and you can create a simple one with just a few lines of code. Have a look at this example:
from keras.models import Sequential
from keras.layers import Dense, Activation
model = Sequential()
model.add(Dense(64, activation='relu', input_dim=50)) #input shape of 50
model.add(Dense(28, activation='relu')) #input shape of 50
model.add(Dense(10, activation='softmax'))
And because the API is user-friendly, the process is easily understood. The code can be written using a simple function and we don’t need to think about setting multiple parameters.
  • Backed by a large community – Multiple AI communities use Keras for deep learning and many publish codes and tutorials that are freely available to the public.
  • Multiple Backends – you can choose between TensorFlow, Theano or CNTK to use as your Keras backend. Different backends suit different projects so which one you choose will depend entirely on your needs and each has its own advantages. 
  • Works cross-platform and makes model deployment easy – Keras can be deployed on multiple platforms, including iOS devices with CoreML, a web browser using .js support, Android devices using TensorFlow \android, Raspberry Pi and cloud engines, to name a few.
Support for multiple GPUs – Keras can be trained on one GPU or you can use it on several at once. It has support built-in for data parallelism which means large amounts of data can be processed at the same time and the data can be trained much quicker.

How to Install Keras on
Windows and Mac OS
Before we go any further, we should look at how to install Keras on your Windows or Mac OS computer. First, we need to look at the different ways it can be installed.
Direct Install or Virtual Environment
So, which is best? Installing it directly to your current Python distribution or using a virtual environment? A virtual environment is probably your best bet if you are running lots of projects because each project may require a different version of any one of the libraries Python offers. Let’s say that your project requires Python 3.5 with OpenCV 3.3 and uses an old Keras-Theano backend. But you have another project that you need to use Keras for and it needs the latest version of Keras, a backend of TensorFlow and support for Python 3.6.6. You can see how difficult it would be if you used a direct install method. Because we don’t want conflicts between the libraries, a virtual environment is the best bet. We can also use other platforms to do the computation, such as Amazon Web Services.
Amazon Web Services (AWS)
The AWS platform offers a cloud computing service alongside lots of other products for use by researchers. They ensure that we can use their products straight from the internet by renting networking, hardware, databases, and more. One of the most popular of their deep learning services is the Amazon Machine Image Deep Learning Service, better known as DL.
AWS also offers a virtual environment in the AWS EC2 service. It’s called AWS Deep Learning AMI and is useful for helping researchers with deep learning. DLAMI provides services to suit both small SPU engines right up to the most powerful multiple GPU systems with cuDNN and CUDA preconfigures and offering several deep learning frameworks.
Amazon SageMaker
SageMaker is another Amazon dee learning platform that helps with training deep learning models with the best algorithm and deploying it. This is the easiest method for using Keras:
  1. Set up an Amazon SageMaker account
  2. Open the console and click the option to Create Notebook Instance
  3. Type in your Notebook name
  4. Now you need to create an IAM role which makes an AMI role in the AmazonSageMaker-Executionrole-YYYYMMDD|HHmmSS format
  5. Click on Create Notebook Instance again and SageMaker will launch the new instance – this may take a few moments
If you want to use your VPC to access resources, you need to enable the Direct Internet Access option. If you don’t, the notebook will not have access to the internet and, as such, will not be able to host or train any models.
  1. Launch the notebook instance by clicking on Open
  2. Click New>conda_tensorflow_p36 in Jupyter and you can begin coding.
Install Keras
Before TensorFlow and Keras can be installed, you need to install pip, Python and virtualenv, if you haven’t already. These instructions work for both Mac and Windows systems:
  1. Install Python from https://python.org/downloads
  2. pip should already be installed by default with Python so open the command or terminal prompt and type int eh following commands to check it is there:
Mac OS
$ python -m pip –version
Windows
C:\> py -m pip –version
  1. Next use the following command to install virtualenv
pip3 install –U pip virtualenv
Install Microsoft Visual C++ 2015 Redistributable Update 3
  1. Download Visual Studio onto your computer
  2. Open it and click on Redistributables>Build Tools
  3. Download Microsoft Visual C++ Redistributable Update 3 and install it
Now you can run the following script
pip3 install virtualenv
Virtual Environment
The virtual environment helps to isolate the working system from the main one so type in this command
virtualenv –-system-site-packages –p python3 ./venv
And this code activates the environment
.\venv\Scripts\activate
Once the environment is prepared, we need to enable TENSORFLOW AS THE Keras backend. We do that with this command:
pip install --upgrade tensorflow
If TensorFlow needs GPU support, use the following command:
pip install --upgrade tensorflow-gpu
Now we need to make sure the installation worked so type in the following:
user@user:~$ python
Python 3.6.4 (default, Mar 20 2018, 11:10:20)
[GCC 5.4.0 20160609] on linux
Type "help", "copyright", "credits" or "license" for more information.
>>> import tensorflow
>>>
If you don’t see any error messages, it all worked okay.
Install Keras
Now we can install Keras by typing in the following command:
pip install keras
Keras will be installed with all necessary dependencies.
Before we can begin to use it, we need to check that Keras uses Tensorflow as the backend so type the following, which will open the configuration file:
gedit ~/.keras/keras.json
You should see something similar to this on your screen:
{
"floatx": "float32",
"epsilon": 1e-07,
"backend": "tensorflow",
"image_data_format": "channels_last"
}
If you see this, TensorFlow is in use as the backend, exactly as we wanted.
Now you can type in the following to run it:
user@user:~$ python3
Python 3.6.4 (default, Mar 20 2018, 11:10:20)
[GCC 5.4.0 20160609] on linux
Type "help", "copyright", "credits" or "license" for more information.
>>> import keras
Using TensorFlow backend.
>>>

Keras Fundamentals for Deep Learning
The primary Keras structure is the model, and this is responsible for defining a network’s graph. Layers can be added to models already created to make a custom model that fits your specific project.
Let’s look at a sequential model and the common deep learning layers:
Sequential Model
from keras.models import Sequential
from keras.layers import Dense, Activation,Conv2D,MaxPooling2D,Flatten,Dropout
model = Sequential()
Convolutional Layer
This shows an input layer as a convolutional layer. Its shape is 320x320x3, and it has 48 3x3 filters. The activation function used is ReLU.
input_shape=(320,320,3) #this is the input shape of an image 320x320x3
model.add(Conv2D(48, (3, 3), activation='relu', input_shape= input_shape))
another type is
model.add(Conv2D(48, (3, 3), activation='relu'))
MaxPooling Layer
MaxPool2d is used for downsampling the input representation and you must specify the size of the kernel.
model.add(MaxPooling2D(pool_size=(2, 2)))
Dense Layer
Here, you can add a Dully Connected layer and just specify what size output you want.
model.add(Dense(256, activation='relu'))
Dropout Layer
Here, you can a dropout layer with a probability of 50%.
model.add(Dropout(0.5))
Compilation, Training and Evaluation
Once the model has been defined, we can begin training it. First, the network must be compiled with the optimizer and loss functions so that the network can change the weights and minimize the loss.
model.compile(loss='mean_squared_error', optimizer='adam')
Now, we can begin training. The training and validation data are fed to the model using the fit function. This lets the network train the data in batches and we can also set the epochs.
model.fit(X_train, X_train, batch_size=32, epochs=10, validation_data=(x_val, y_val))
The last step is to use the test data to evaluate our model.
score = model.evaluate(x_test, y_test, batch_size=32)
Simple Linear Regression
This is a simple linear regression model:
import keras
from keras.models import Sequential
from keras.layers import Dense, Activation
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
x = data = np.linspace(1,2,200)
y = x*4 + np.random.randn(*x.shape) * 0.3
model = Sequential()
model.add(Dense(1, input_dim=1, activation='linear'))
model.compile(optimizer='sgd', loss='mse', metrics=['mse'])
weights = model.layers[0].get_weights()
w_init = weights[0][0][0]
b_init = weights[1][0]
print('Linear regression model initialized with weights w: %.2f, b: %.2f' % (w_init, b_init))
model.fit(x,y, batch_size=1, epochs=30, shuffle=False)
weights = model.layers[0].get_weights()
w_final = weights[0][0][0]
b_final = weights[1][0]
print('Linear regression model trained with weight w: %.2f, b: %.2f' % (w_final, b_final))
predict = model.predict(data)
plt.plot(data, predict, 'b', data , y, 'k.')
plt.show()
Once the data is trained, you should see an output similar to this:
The initial weight is:
Linear regression model is initialized with weights w: 0.37, b: 0.00
And the final weight is:
Linear regression model is trained to have weight w: 3.70, b: 0.61
Fine-Tuning and Using Pre-Trained Keras Models
When we fine-tune, we are tweaking models that have already been trained in a way that the parameters work for the new model. Training from scratch on new models requires vast amounts of data to ensure the network can find all the parameters. In our case, we are using a pre-trained model – the parameters have already been learned and we have the weights.
Let’s say, for example, that we want a model trained to deal with a classification problem but we don’t have much data. Solving it is much easier if we use the Transfer Learning and Fine-Tuning method.  By using a network that has already been trained and has weights, there is no need to go down the route of training the entire network. All we need to do is train the final layer as that is what will solve the problem.
Note – the following tutorial is for information only. Later, there will be a practical example you can follow along.
Preparing the Network Model
Keras comes pre-loaded with lots of different pre-trained models, such as:
  • InceptionResNetV2
  • InceptionV3
  • MobileNet
  • ResNet
  • VGG16
  • Xception
We will use the VGG16 model and, for the model weight, we will use imageNet. The network will be fine-tuned to classify no less than eight class types using some images taken from the Kaggle Natural Images Dataset.
Upload Data to AWS S3 Bucket
We are going to use natural images from eight classes to train the model but first, the data needs to be uploaded to Amazon S3 Bucket.
  1. Set up and log in to SW3
  2. Click on Create Bucket, choose a Bucket name and set your region; click on Create
  3. Your bucket is now ready to use but you can see that the access is set to Private. That’s fine if you want to keep it that way but if the Bucket needs public access, you can change it in Bucket Properties.
  4. Now you are ready to upload the training data into the bucket. Click the Upload button and click on the file name; copy the link so it can be downloaded.
Preparing the Data
Next, we use the Keras ImageDataGenerator to get our training data.
  1. First, the data needs to be downloaded so, using the link you copied earlier, use the following code – change the image file to match your own:
!wget https://s3.us-east-2. amazonaws.com/naturalimages02/images.tar.gz
!tar -xzf images.tar.gz
Once the data is downloaded, the training process can begin:
from keras.preprocessing.image import ImageDataGenerator
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
train_path = 'images/train/'
test_path = 'images/test/'
batch_size = 16
image_size = 224
num_class = 8
train_datagen = ImageDataGenerator(validation_split=0.3,
shear_range=0.2,
zoom_range=0.2,
horizontal_flip=True)
train_generator = train_datagen.flow_from_directory(
directory=train_path,
target_size=(image_size,image_size) ,
batch_size=batch_size,
class_mode='categorical',
color_mode='rgb',
shuffle=True)
ImageDataGenerator creates X_training data from a directory and the subdirectory is used as each object’s class. The image is loaded with the color mode RGB, the Y_training data is given the categorical class mode and we have a batch of 16. The data can now be shuffled.
We can use matplotlib to plot the images and show them to us randomly:
x_batch, y_batch = train_generator.next()
fig=plt.figure()
columns = 4
rows = 4
for i in range(1, columns*rows):
num = np.random.randint(batch_size)
image = x_batch[num].astype(np.int)
fig.add_subplot(rows, columns, i)
plt.imshow(image)
plt.show()
Output
After that, VGG16 can be used to create the model with pre-trained imageNet weights. The layers are frozen so they cannot be trained, which helps to reduce the time take for the computation.
Creating VGG16 Model
This is how the model is created:
import keras
from keras.models import Model, load_model
from keras.layers import Activation, Dropout, Flatten, Dense
from keras.preprocessing.image import ImageDataGenerator
from keras.applications.vgg16 import VGG16
#Load the VGG model
base_model = VGG16(weights='imagenet', include_top=False, input_shape=(image_size, image_size, 3))
print(base_model.summary() )
# Freeze the layers
for layer in base_model.layers:
layer.trainable = False
# # Create the model
model = keras.models.Sequential()
# # Add the vgg convolutional base model
model.add(base_model)
# # Add new layers
model.add(Flatten())
model.add(Dense(1024, activation='relu'))
model.add(Dense(1024, activation='relu'))
model.add(Dense(num_class, activation='softmax'))
# # Show a summary of the model. Check the number of trainable parameters   
print(model.summary())
The model summary looks like this:
Layer (type)                 Output Shape              Param #  
=================================================================
vgg16 (Model)                (None, 7, 7, 512)         14714688
_________________________________________________________________
flatten_1 (Flatten)          (None, 25088)             0        
_________________________________________________________________
dense_1 (Dense)              (None, 1024)              25691136
_________________________________________________________________
dense_2 (Dense)              (None, 1024)              1049600  
_________________________________________________________________
dense_3 (Dense)              (None, 8)                 8200     
=================================================================
Total params: 41,463,624
Trainable params: 26,748,936
Non-trainable params: 14,714,688
From the VGG16 Layers, we have the input, then 2 Fully Connected layers are added. These extract 1024 features along with an output layer. The output layer uses the SoftMax activation function to compute the classes.
Training
This is how the model is trained:
# # Compile the model
from keras.optimizers import SGD
model.compile(loss='categorical_crossentropy',
optimizer=SGD(lr=1e-3),
metrics=['accuracy'])
# # Start the training process
# model.fit(x_train, y_train, validation_split=0.30, batch_size=32, epochs=50, verbose=2)
# # #save the model
# model.save('catdog.h5')
history = model.fit_generator(
train_generator,
steps_per_epoch=train_generator.n/batch_size,
epochs=10)
model.save('fine_tune.h5')
# summarize history for accuracy
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
plt.plot(history.history['loss'])
plt.title('loss')
plt.ylabel('loss')
plt.xlabel('epoch')
plt.legend(['loss'], loc='upper left')
plt.show()
Results

You can see from the results that the losses are significantly reduced and the model accuracy is nearly 100%.
As you can see, our losses are dropped significantly and the accuracy is almost 100%. For testing our model, we randomly picked images over the internet and put them in the test folder with a different class to test
Testing Our Model
To test the model, random images were chosen from the internet and placed into the test folder with another class.
model = load_model('fine_tune.h5')
test_datagen = ImageDataGenerator()
train_generator = train_datagen.flow_from_directory(
directory=train_path,
target_size=(image_size,image_size),
batch_size=batch_size,
class_mode='categorical',
color_mode='rgb',
shuffle=True)
test_generator = test_datagen.flow_from_directory(
directory=test_path,
target_size=(image_size, image_size),
color_mode='rgb',
shuffle=False,
class_mode='categorical',
batch_size=1)
filenames = test_generator.filenames
nb_samples = len(filenames)
fig=plt.figure()
columns = 4
rows = 4
for i in range(1, columns*rows -1):
x_batch, y_batch = test_generator.next()
name = model.predict(x_batch)
name = np.argmax(name, axis=-1)
true_name = y_batch
true_name = np.argmax(true_name, axis=-1 )
label_map = (test_generator.class_indices)
label_map = dict((v,k) for k,v in label_map.items()) #flip k,v
predictions = [label_map[k] for k in name]
true_value = [label_map[k] for k in true_name]
image = x_batch[0].astype(np.int)
fig.add_subplot(rows, columns, i)
plt.title(str(predictions[0]) + ':' + str(true_value[0]))
plt.imshow(image)
plt.show()
And from the results, out of 14 images, only one was incorrectly classified.

Face Recognition Neural
Network with Keras
Facial recognition is growing in popularity and is widely used by systems that need to identify specific people, as well as estimating their age and learning facial expressions. Whenever you tag a friend in a Facebook photo, for example, facial recognition has been used to do it. So, how does it all work?
Using Facebook as an example, whenever a person is tagged in an image, Facebook learns it using artificial intelligence. From then on, it will try to predict that name and will continue until it gets it right. When it's right, you won’t have to tag your friends yourself anymore, as Facebook will do it for you.
We’re going to build our own facial recognition system using Keras and deep learning.
Network Model
For this, we will use the VGG16 model and add a VGGFace weight. VGGFace is Keras’s Deep Face Recognition and the network architecture is VGG16.
VGGFace can be freely downloaded from GitHub so go ahead and do that now.
Here’s the code you need:
from keras.applications.vgg16 import VGG16
from keras_vggface.vggface import VGGFace
face_model = VGGFace(model='vgg16',
weights='vggface',
input_shape=(224,224,3))
face_model.summary()
The network summary looks like this:

To speed up the training, we’ll do what we discussed earlier –Transfer Learning and Fine-Tuning. First, we need to freeze the model’s base layers so we don’t train them:
for layer in face_model.layers:
layer.trainable = False
Then, so the model recognizes the test faces, we need to add layers. We’ll add in two Fully Connected layers and one output layer with five faces for detection.
from keras.models import Model, Sequential
from keras.layers import Input, Convolution2D, ZeroPadding2D, MaxPooling2D, Flatten, Dense, Dropout, Activation
person_count = 5
last_layer = face_model.get_layer('pool5').output
x = Flatten(name='flatten')(last_layer)
x = Dense(1024, activation='relu', name='fc6')(x)
x = Dense(1024, activation='relu', name='fc7')(x)
out = Dense(person_count, activation='softmax', name='fc8')(x)
custom_face = Model(face_model.input, out)
The network summary looks like this:

So, following the pool5 layer, you can see that it is flattened down to one feature vector and the dense layer uses this for the final recognition.
Prepare the Faces
The next step is to prepare the faces and we’ll use a directory that consists of images of five well-known people:
  • Pink
  • Colin Firth
  • Tom Hiddleston
  • Hugh Grant
  • Stephen Fry
Each folder has ten images in it for the training and the evaluation. Sure, it’s not much data but isn’t that half the challenge?
Keras is going to help us prepare our data, iterating over the dataset folder and then preparing the data for use in the training process.
from keras.preprocessing.image import ImageDataGenerator
batch_size = 5
train_path = 'data/'
eval_path = 'eval/'
train_datagen = ImageDataGenerator(rescale=1./255,
shear_range=0.2,
zoom_range=0.2,
horizontal_flip=True)
valid_datagen = ImageDataGenerator(rescale=1./255,
shear_range=0.2,
zoom_range=0.2,
horizontal_flip=True)
train_generator = train_datagen.flow_from_directory(
train_path,
target_size=(image_size,image_size),
batch_size=batch_size,
class_mode='sparse',
color_mode='rgb')
valid_generator = valid_datagen.flow_from_directory(
directory=eval_path,
target_size=(224, 224),
color_mode='rgb',
batch_size=batch_size ,
class_mode='sparse',
shuffle=True,
)
Training the Model
We’ll begin training by compiling the network with the optimizer function and the loss function. The loss function we are using is sparse_categorical_crossentropy and the learning optimizer is SGD.
from keras.optimizers import SGD
custom_face.compile(loss='sparse_categorical_crossentropy',
optimizer=SGD(lr=1e-4, momentum=0.9),
metrics=['accuracy'])
history = custom_face.fit_generator(
train_generator,
validation_data=valid_generator,
steps_per_epoch=49/batch_size,
validation_steps=valid_generator.n,
epochs=50)
custom_face.evaluate_generator(generator=valid_generator)
custom_face.save('vgg_face.h5')

Right now, the validation accuracy is standing at around 64% which, for such a small training dataset, is pretty good. However, it can be improved and we do that adding layers or adding extra training images.
We can now use a test picture to test the model with:
from keras.models import load_model
from keras.preprocessing.image import load_img, save_img, img_to_array
from keras_vggface.utils import preprocess_input
test_img = image.load_img('test.jpg', target_size=(224, 224))
img_test = image.img_to_array(test_img)
img_test = np.expand_dims(img_test, axis=0)
img_test = utils.preprocess_input(img_test)
predictions = model.predict(img_test)
predicted_class=np.argmax(predictions,axis=1)
labels = (train_generator.class_indices)
labels = dict((v,k) for k,v in labels.items())
predictions = [labels[k] for k in predicted_class]
print(predictions)
[‘HughGrant’]
When we used an image of Hugh Grant as the test picture, the result shows that it was predicted correctly.
Using a Live Cam for the Prediction
What about using a webcam input for the implementation? If we used OpenCV and Haar Face cascade to locate the face, our network model would be able to recognize the image. Again, this section is purely for information, not as a practical follow-on.
First, prepare your training images – the more you have, the better the result will be.
Prepare your network and train it like you did earlier and then add the following code to get an input image from a webcam:
#Load trained model
from keras.models import load_model
from keras_vggface import utils
import cv2
image_size = 224
device_id = 0 #camera_device id
model = load_model('my faces.h5')
#make labels according to your dataset folder
labels = dict(firstname=0,secondname=1) #and so on
print(labels)
cascade_classifier = cv2.CascadeClassifier('haarcascade_frontalface_default.xml')
camera = cv2.VideoCapture(device_id)
while camera.isOpened():
ok, cam_frame = camera.read()
if not ok:
break
gray_img=cv2.cvtColor(cam_frame, cv2.COLOR_BGR2GRAY)
faces= cascade_classifier.detectMultiScale(gray_img, minNeighbors=5)
for (x,y,w,h) in faces:
cv2.rectangle(cam_frame,(x,y),(x+w,y+h),(255,255,0),2)
roi_color = cam_frame [y:y+h, x:x+w]
roi color = cv2.cvtColor(roi_color, cv2.COLOR_BGR2RGB)
roi_color = cv2.resize(roi_color, (image_size, image_size))
image = roi_color.astype(np.float32, copy=False)
image = np.expand_dims(image, axis=0)
image = preprocess_input(image, version=1) # or version=2
preds = model.predict(image)
predicted_class=np.argmax(preds,axis=1 )
labels = dict((v,k) for k,v in labels.items())
name = [labels[k] for k in predicted_class]
cv2.putText(cam_frame,str(name),
(x + 10, y + 10), cv2.FONT_HERSHEY_SIMPLEX, 1, (255,0,255), 2)
cv2.imshow('video image', cam_frame)
key = cv2.waitKey(30)
if key == 27: # press 'ESC' to quit
break
camera.release()
cv2.destroyAllWindows()
Keras or TensorFlow? Which One is Best?
When you are writing a script, Keras provides the simplicity, allowing us to understand everything straight away. It is a user-friendly library and dead simple to implement, with no need to create multiple variables for the model to run. That means there is no real need for us to understand all the backend details fully.
TensorFlow is a low-level program that offers advanced operations and flexibility to help us create models or computational graphs. It also offers us TensorFlow which helps us visualize everything, and a debugger tool that helps us find and fix errors.
Summary
To summarize this part:
  • Keras is one of the high-level APIs used for making deep learning networks easier to create and use with the help of a backend engine.
  • It is user-friendly and easy to understand, with full Python support which makes it feel natural to use. It is one of the best libraries for beginners who want to learn deep learning and for researchers that are looking for an easy API to use.
  • Keras is simple to install and can be used with a virtual environment or AWS or other external platforms.
Keras also offers multiple network models built-in so you can choose to fine-tune a pre-trained model rather than building one from scratch.

Keras Tutorial
We don’t need too much code here but I will walk you through this final Keras model one step at a time so you know how to do it in the future. Here’s what this tutorial will teach you:
  • Loading data
  • Defining the Keras model
  • Compiling the model
  • Fitting the model
  • Evaluating the model
  • Bringing it all together
  • Making the predictions
There are a few things that must be installed and configured on your system before you start the tutorial:
  • Python 2 or 3+
  • SciPy and NumPy
  • Keras and a backend, TensorFlow or Theano
Before you begin, create a file and call it keras_first_network.py – this is where the code will go.
Step One – Loading the Data
First, we need to define the classes and the functions we want to use. We’re going to load the dataset using NumPy and then define the models using a couple of Keras classes. We require the following imports:
# first neural network with keras tutorial
from numpy import loadtxt
from keras.models import Sequential
from keras.layers import Dense
...
Now the dataset can be loaded.
We will use a popular dataset called Pima Indians, containing data regarding diabetes onset. It is a standard library available in the UCI Machine Learning repository, describing the Pima Indians patient data and whether diabetes onset in a five-year period.
That makes it a binary classification problem as the values are simple – 1 for diabetes onset, 0 for none. The input variables used for describing the patients are numerical, making it simple to use on neural networks that require numerical input and a numerical output. It is perfect for this tutorial.  You can download the CSV file from:
  • Dataset CSV File (pima-indians-diabetes.csv)
  • Dataset Details
Go ahead and download it, putting it in your local working directory – where your Python file is. Name it pima-indians-diabetes.csv and save it.
When you look in the file, you should see several data rows, looking something like this:
6,148,72,35,0,33.6,0.627,50,1
1,85,66,29,0,26.6,0.351,31,0
8,183,64,0,0,23.3,0.672,32,1
1,89,66,23,94,28.1,0.167,21,0
0,137,40,35,168,43.1,2.288,33,1
The file can now be loaded as a matrix of numbers and we use a function called loadtxt() from NumPy. The data has eight input variables and the last column is the single output variable. The model we are learning will map input variables (X) in rows to one output variable (y) and this is typically written as y = f(X).
We can summarize the variables like this:
Input Variables (X):
  1. How many times the patient has been pregnant
  2. Concentration of plasma glucose after a 2-hour test for oral glucose tolerance
  3. Diastolic blood pressure (mm Hg)
  4. Triceps skin fold thickness (mm)
  5. 2-Hour serum insulin (mu U/ml)
  6. Body mass index (weight in kg/(height in m)^2)
  7. Diabetes pedigree function
  8. Age (in years)
Output Variables (y):
  1. Class variable (0 or 1)
When the CSV file has been loaded to memory, the data columns can be split into input variables and output variables. We store the data in a 2D array; the rows are the first dimension and the columns are the second dimension – written as [rows, columns].
Splitting the data into two arrays requires that subsets of columns are chosen using the slice operator in NumPy. The first eight columns, index 0 to index 7, are selected using the 0:8 slice. Then the output column, which is the 9th variable, is selected using index 8.
# load the dataset
dataset = loadtxt('pima-indians-diabetes.csv', delimiter=',')
# split into input (X) and output (y) variables
X = dataset[:,0:8]
y = dataset[:,8]
...
Now the neural network model is ready for defining
Note that we nine columns in our dataset and the 0:8 range selects the columns indexed 0 to 7. If you have not come across this before, the simplest way to explain this is that Python indexing begins at 0 – index 0 indicates the first column, index 1 indicates the second column, and so on.
Step Two – Defining Our Model
Keras models are defined as sequences of layers.  We are going to create a sequential model and the layers will be added one by one until the architecture is how we want it. The first thing that must be correct is making sure the input layer has the number of input features needed. When we create the first layer, we can specify the number of layers using the input_dim argument. We set it to 8 as we have eight variables.
I can hear you asking how we know how many layers we need and what their types are. That is not an easy question to answer and there are a few heuristics that could help us. More often than not, though, the best network tends to be found through experimentation. In general, your network needs to be big enough to capture the problem’s structure.
We are using a Fully Connected structure that has three layers. We use the Dense class to define the Fully Connected layers and the number of nodes or neurons in the layer is specified as the first argument. The activation argument is used to specify which activation function we are using.  We will use ReLU or Rectified Linear Unit for our activation function on layers one and two and the output layer will have the Sigmoid function.
At one time, the Tanh and Sigmoid functions were the preferred activation functions for every layer but now the ReLU function gives us much better performance. We use the Sigmoid function on our output layer so that we can make sure the network output is somewhere between 0 and 1. We also want to ensure it can easily be mapped to a probability of class 1 or snapped to a classification of one or the other class with 0.5 as the default threshold.
We can pull it together when each layer is added:
  • Our model will expect data rows containing eight variables – input_dim=8 argument
  • Hidden layer one contains 12 nodes and the ReLU activation function is used
  • Hidden layer two contains eight nodes and the ReLU activation function is used
  • The output layer contains a single node and the Sigmoid activation function is used
...
# define the keras model
model = Sequential()
model.add(Dense(12, input_dim=8, activation='relu'))
model.add(Dense(8, activation='relu'))
model.add(Dense(1, activation='sigmoid'))
...
One of the most confusing things is that shape of the model input has been defined in the initial hidden layer as an argument. This means that the code adding the first Dense layer does two things – it defines the visible layer, which is the input layer and defines the first of the hidden layers.
Step Three – Compiling the Model
We’ve defined the model, now it’s time to compile it.
Model compilation requires good numerical libraries to be used under the hood. This is the backend and is where we use TensorFlow or Theano. The backend makes an automatic selection on how the network should be represented for training and to make predictions and chooses the best way to run it on your GPU, CPU, or distributed.
When we compile the model, we need to specify some properties that we need to train the network. Remember, when we train a network, we are trying to find the optimum set of weights for mapping the inputs to the output.
The loss function we want to use for evaluating the set of weights must be specified, along with the optimizer we want to use to look through the network weights and any other metrics we might want to collect and report on throughout training.
The loss argument we are using is cross-entropy, a binary classification loss function defined as binary_crossentropy in Keras. The optimize will be defined as adam, the stochastic gradient descent algorithm which is surprisingly efficient. This is the most popular and commonly used gradient descent version. It tunes itself and provides the best results in lots of different problems. Lastly, because we are trying to solve a classification problem, we also want to collect the accuracy of the classification and report it; we use the metrics argument to define this.
...
# compile the keras model
model.compile(loss='binary_crossentropy', optimizer='adam', metrics=['accuracy'])
...
Step Four – Fitting the Model
Now that the model has been defined and compiled, it is now time to execute it on a set of data. We call the fit() function to train (fit) our model on the data. All the training will take place over a specified number of epochs and each of those is split down into batches.  One epoch is a pass through the training dataset rows while a batch is a number of samples the model looks at in each epoch before updating the weights.
The training will take place over a specified number of iterations or epochs and we use the epochs argument to specify how many there will be. We also need to specify how many dataset rows the training will consider before updating the weights at the end of each epoch. This is the batch size and we use the batch_size argument to set this.
We are going to run quite a small number of epochs, 150, with a batch size of just 10. We can choose these just by using trial and error; the model needs to be trained enough to learn the input row data mappings to the output classifications. The model will have a certain amount of error but that levels out after a point depending on the model configuration and this is known as model convergence.
...
# fit the keras model on the dataset
model.fit(X, y, epochs=150, batch_size=10)
...
At this point, the work is happening on your GPU or CPU. We are only using a CPU for this model.
Step Five – Evaluating the Model
The neural network has now been trained on our dataset and we can use that dataset to evaluate how the network performs. But this only provides an idea of how the algorithm might work on unseen data. We did it this way for the sake of simplicity but, if you wanted to, the data could be split into two sets – training and testing.
The model is evaluated on the training set by using the function called evaluate() – the same inputs and outputs that were used to train the model are passed to this function. This results in new predictions for each pair of inputs and outputs being generated and the scores are collected. This includes the metrics you might have included, like accuracy, along with the average loss.
A list is returned by the evaluate() function containing two values. The first is the model’s loss on the dataset, while the second is the model’s accuracy on the dataset. We can ignore the loss because we only want to know the model accuracy:
...
# evaluate the keras model
_, accuracy = model.evaluate(X, y)
print('Accuracy: %.2f' % (accuracy*100))
Step Six – Bringing it Together
That wasn’t too bad, was it? You’ve now built a neural network using Keras so let’s put it all together in a single code:
# first neural network with keras tutorial
from numpy import loadtxt
from keras.models import Sequential
from keras.layers import Dense
# load the dataset
dataset = loadtxt('pima-indians-diabetes.csv', delimiter=',')
# split into input (X) and output (y) variables
X = dataset[:,0:8]
y = dataset[:,8]
# define the keras model
model = Sequential()
model.add(Dense(12, input_dim=8, activation='relu'))
model.add(Dense(8, activation='relu'))
model.add(Dense(1, activation='sigmoid'))
# compile the keras model
model.compile(loss='binary_crossentropy', optimizer='adam', metrics=['accuracy'])
# fit the keras model on the dataset
model.fit(X, y, epochs=150, batch_size=10)
# evaluate the keras model
_, accuracy = model.evaluate(X, y)
print('Accuracy: %.2f' % (accuracy*100))
If you want to do things the easy way, simply copy and paste the code into the file you created and save it where your CSV file is saved. Then it can be run from the command-line using the following command:
python keras_first_network.py
When you do this, there should be one message for each epoch, showing the accuracy and loss for the model, and you should also see an evaluation of the model after training on the dataset.
In an ideal world, we would want to see a loss of zero and accuracy of 1.0, indicating 100% but we don’t live in an ideal world and that never happens unless you are working on a trivial problem. You will always see a certain level of error and the idea is to choose the configuration and training that will give us the least loss and the most accuracy on the dataset.
...
768/768 [==============================] - 0s 63us/step - loss: 0.4817 - acc: 0.7708
Epoch 147/150
768/768 [==============================] - 0s 63us/step - loss: 0.4764 - acc: 0.7747
Epoch 148/150
768/768 [==============================] - 0s 63us/step - loss: 0.4737 - acc: 0.7682
Epoch 149/150
768/768 [==============================] - 0s 64us/step - loss: 0.4730 - acc: 0.7747
Epoch 150/150
768/768 [==============================] - 0s 63us/step - loss: 0.4754 - acc: 0.7799
768/768 [==============================] - 0s 38us/step
Accuracy: 76.56
If you attempt to run this in a Jupyter or IPython notebook, chances are you will see an error. This is because of the output progress bars in the training but these can be turned off; just set verbose=0 in the evaluate() and fit() function calls. For example:
...
# fit model on dataset without progress bars
model.fit(X, y, epochs=150, batch_size=10, verbose=0)
# evaluate the keras model
_, accuracy = model.evaluate(X, y, verbose=0)
...
You may see different results, given that the algorithm is stochastic, or because of the evaluation or even numerical precision differences. The best thing to do is run it a few times and find the average outcome.
Neural networks are stochastic which means you can use the same algorithm to train the same data on different models with different skills every time you run the code. This is not a bug, it is a feature of the algorithm.
Because the model performance varies, you may need to fit it multiple times and average the results to get an idea of your model’s performance. For example, running the network five times gives us these accuracy scores:
Accuracy: 75.00
Accuracy: 77.73
Accuracy: 77.60
Accuracy: 78.12
Accuracy: 76.17
All the scores are about 77% and the average works out at 76.924%.
Step Seven - Making Predictions
Your model is trained but how do you use it on new data to make predictions? The model can be adapted and used for generating predictions on your training data, as if it were a brand-new set of data not seen before.
Making a prediction is simple – just call predict() on the model. Because the activation function in our output layer is Sigmoid, we can expect to see predictions of a probability from 0 to 1. Our task is one of classification so we can convert the predictions to binary by rounding them.
For example:
...
# make probability predictions with model
predictions = model.predict(X)
# round predictions
rounded = [round(x[0]) for x in predictions]
We could also call a function named predict_classes(), which would give us crisp classes, like this:
...
# make class predictions with model
predictions = model.predict_classes(X)
Below, you can see an entire example with predictions for each dataset example, followed by the input data being printed along with the predicted and expected classes for the first five dataset examples:
# first neural network with keras make predictions
from numpy import loadtxt
from keras.models import Sequential
from keras.layers import Dense
# load the dataset
dataset = loadtxt('pima-indians-diabetes.csv', delimiter=',')
# split into input (X) and output (y) variables
X = dataset[:,0:8]
y = dataset[:,8]
# define the keras model
model = Sequential()
model.add(Dense(12, input_dim=8, activation='relu'))
model.add(Dense(8, activation='relu'))
model.add(Dense(1, activation='sigmoid'))
# compile the keras model
model.compile(loss='binary_crossentropy', optimizer='adam', metrics=['accuracy'])
# fit the keras model on the dataset
model.fit(X, y, epochs=150, batch_size=10, verbose=0)
# make class predictions with the model
predictions = model.predict_classes(X)
# summarize the first 5 cases
for i in range(5):
​ print('%s => %d (expected %d)' % (X[i].tolist(), predictions[i], y[i]))
When you run the example, the progress bar has disappeared because we set verbose=0.
Once the model has been fit, we get predictions for every dataset example with the predicted class value for five examples printed; these are compared with the expected class values.
Most rows were predicted correctly and, based on the estimated model performance, we would expect to see 76.9% accuracy
[6.0, 148.0, 72.0, 35.0, 0.0, 33.6, 0.627, 50.0] => 0 (expected 1)
[1.0, 85.0, 66.0, 29.0, 0.0, 26.6, 0.351, 31.0] => 0 (expected 0)
[8.0, 183.0, 64.0, 0.0, 0.0, 23.3, 0.672, 32.0] => 1 (expected 1)
[1.0, 89.0, 66.0, 23.0, 94.0, 28.1, 0.167, 21.0] => 0 (expected 0)
[0.0, 137.0, 40.0, 35.0, 168.0, 43.1, 2.288, 33.0] => 1 (expected 1)
Summary
Disadvantages of Keras
Earlier we discussed the advantages of Keras so it seems only fair to tell you about the disadvantages too – in fact, there is only one:
  • Can’t handle low-level APIs – Keras can only handle the high-level APIs, those that run on TensorFlow, CNTK, Theano, and other backend engines or frameworks. In that sense, if you want to make abstract layers, it isn’t very useful because Keras contains pre-configured layers.
That completes our look at machine learning with Keras; time for the final section, Scikit-Learn.

Part Four
An Introduction to Scikit-Learn

What is Scikit-Learn?
Originally developed in 2007 by David Cournapeau, Scikit-Learn started life as a Google Summer of Code project. Matthieu Brucher then joined and began using Scikit-Learn for his thesis work. INRIA became involved in 2010 and Scikit-Learn was released to the public, in beta format, late in January the same year.
Scikit-Learn provides users with both unsupervised and supervised learning algorithms through Python.  You must install SciPy before you using Scikit-Learn and the entire stack gives you:
  • NumPy
  • SciPy
  • Matplotlib
  • IPython
  • Sympy
  • Pandas
Scikit-Learn is made of SciPy modules or extensions and provides users with a ton of useful algorithms. It is simple to use, contains high-quality, readable code, plenty of documentation, and is high performance.
The Scikit-Learn library focuses primarily on data modeling, not on loading or manipulating the data and definitely not summarizing it; Pandas and NumPy provide those features.
Some of the more popular models Scikit-Learn provides are:
  • Clustering : used to group unlabeled data
  • Cross Validation : used to estimate how supervised models perform on unseen data.
  • Datasets : used for test datasets and to generate datasets containing properties that help investigate how a model behaves.
  • Dimensionality Reduction : used to reduce how many attributes are in the data for the purposes of summarization, feature selection and visualization.
  • Ensemble Methods : used to combine predictions from two or more supervised learning models.
  • Feature Extraction : used to define the attributes found in text and image data.
  • Feature Selection : used to identify the meaningful attributes used for creating supervised learning models.
  • Parameter Tuning : used to ensure supervised models perform at their best.
  • Manifold Learning : used to summarize and visualize multi-dimensional data.
  • Supervised Models : includes linear models, Naïve Bayes, discriminate analysis, lazy learning, support vector machines, neural networks and decision trees, to name a few.
Classification and Regression Trees
I want you to see just how easy Scikit-learn is to use so here is an example of a CART (classification and regression trees) algorithm modeling the popular Iris flower dataset. The Iris dataset is included with Scikit-Learn; we fit the classifier on the data and make predictions using the training data.  Lastly, we print the classification accuracy and confusion matrix:
# Sample Decision Tree Classifier
from sklearn import datasets
from sklearn import metrics
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier
# load the iris datasets
dataset = datasets.load_iris()
# fit a CART model to the data
model = DecisionTreeClassifier()
model.fit(dataset.data, dataset.target)
print(model)
# make predictions
expected = dataset.target
predicted = model.predict(dataset.data)
# summarize the fit of the model
print(metrics.classification_report(expected, predicted))
print(metrics.confusion_matrix(expected, predicted))
When you run this, you should see an output like the following. It shows you the trained model details, the model skills based on common metrics and also a confusion matrix:
DecisionTreeClassifier(class_weight=None, criterion='gini', max_depth=None,
max_features=None, max_leaf_nodes=None, min_samples_leaf=1,
min_samples_split=2, min_weight_fraction_leaf=0.0,
presort=False, random_state=None, splitter='best')

That shows you how easy using Scikit-Learn is.

  • What Scikit-Learn is

  • How to create a DNN using MLPClassifier
  • How to use LIME to trust the model
And more.
Download and Install Scikit-Learn
At some point during the previous tutorials, you will have downloaded Scikit-Learn using a conda environment. There is now a much better version, the developer's version and this is the one we are going to use.  First, you need to remove the original version:
Activate the TensorFlow environment using the following command:
source activate hello-tf
Then remove Scikit-Learn using this command:
conda remove scikit-learn
Now install the correct version and the libraries you need:
conda install -c anaconda git
pip install Cython
pip install h5py
pip install git+git://github.com/scikit-learn/scikit-learn.git

Machine Learning With Scikit-Learn
I have divided this tutorial into two – first the machine learning and second, using LIME to trust your model.
Step One – Importing the Data
We are going to use the Adult dataset for this tutorial. It shows details of adult income and the problem revolves around predicting income levels for individuals as below or above $50,000, using features such as education and relationship.
First, the dataset needs to be imported and we do that with Pandas. Be aware that the continuous variable type needs to be converted to float format.
In the dataset, you will find a file named CATE_FEATURES, which includes eight categorical variables:                               
workclass
education
marital
occupation
relationship
race
sex
native_country
There are also six continuous variables listed in CONTI_FEATURES
age
fnlwgt
education_num
capital_gain
capital_loss
hours_week
The list is going to be populated manually so you understand the columns we are using. One of the fastest ways of constructing a list of continuous or categorical features is:
## List Categorical
CATE_FEATURES = df_train.iloc[:,:-1].select_dtypes('object').columns
print(CATE_FEATURES)
## List continuous
CONTI_FEATURES =  df_train._get_numeric_data()
print(CONTI_FEATURES)
Importing the data is done like this:
# Import dataset
import pandas as pd
## Define path data
COLUMNS = ['age','workclass', 'fnlwgt', 'education', 'education_num', 'marital',
'occupation', 'relationship', 'race', 'sex', 'capital_gain', 'capital_loss',
'hours_week', 'native_country', 'label']
### Define continuous list
CONTI_FEATURES  = ['age', 'fnlwgt','capital_gain', 'education_num', 'capital_loss', 'hours_week']
### Define categorical list
CATE_FEATURES = ['workclass', 'education', 'marital', 'occupation', 'relationship', 'race', 'sex', 'native_country']
## Prepare the data
features = ['age','workclass', 'fnlwgt', 'education', 'education_num', 'marital',
'occupation', 'relationship', 'race', 'sex', 'capital_gain', 'capital_loss',
'hours_week', 'native_country']
PATH = "https://archive.ics.uci.edu/ml/machine-learning-databases/adult/adult.data"
df_train = pd.read_csv(PATH, skipinitialspace=True, names = COLUMNS, index_col=False)
df_train[CONTI_FEATURES] =df_train[CONTI_FEATURES].astype('float64')
df_train.describe()

That row can be removed from the dataset because it won’t give us anything except trouble:
## Remove Netherland, only one row
df_train = df_train[df_train.native_country != "Holland-Netherlands"]
Next, the continuous feature positions can be stored in a list that you will require when you create the pipeline. The next code loops over the names of the columns in CONTI_FEATURES and retrieves the locations (numbers), which are then appended to a list named conti_features:
## Get column index of categorical features
conti_features = []
for i in CONTI_FEATURES:
position = df_train.columns.get_loc(i)
conti_features.append(position)
print(conti_features)
[0, 2, 10, 4, 11, 12]​
The next code does the same for the categorical features in CATE_FEATURES:
## Get column index of categorical features
categorical_features = []
for i in CATE_FEATURES:
position = df_train.columns.get_loc(i)
categorical_features.append(position)
print(categorical_features)
[1, 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 13]
Now you can look at the dataset and you will see that the categorical features are all strings. Models cannot be fed with string values so the dataset needs to be transformed; we do this with a dummy variable:
df_train.head(5)​
You need to create an individual column for each of the feature’s groups – first, the code below computes how many columns you need:
print(df_train[CATE_FEATURES].nunique(),
'There are',sum(df_train[CATE_FEATURES].nunique()), 'groups in the whole dataset')
workclass         ​ 9
education         ​ 16
marital           ​ ​ 7
occupation        ​ 15

As you can see, there are 101 groups in this dataset. The workclass feature has 9 groups, the occupation one has 15 groups, and so on. The next code helps you visualize the group names:
unique() returns the unique values of the categorical features.
for i in CATE_FEATURES:
print(df_train[i].unique())

The training dataset will have 101 + 7 columns, the 7 being the continuous features.
Scikit-Learn can do the conversion for you and it will take two steps:
  1. The string must be converted to ID. For example, State-gov gets ID1, Self-emp-not-inc gets ID2 and so on. This is all done by the LabelEncoder function.
  2. Then each ID has to be transposed into a new column. As you know, there are 101 IDs so that equates to 101 columns for the categorical feature groups. The OneHotEncoder function in Scikit-Learn does this.
Step Two – Creating the Training and Test Sets
The dataset is now ready to split into the training and test sets. We’re going to split it into 80% for training and 20% for testing. We do this with train_test_split with two arguments. The first is a data frame containing all the features and the second is the data frame with the labels. We use test_size to specify the test set size.
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(df_train[features],
df_train.label,
test_size = 0.2,
random_state=0)
X_train.head(5)
print(X_train.shape, X_test.shape)
(26048, 14) (6512, 14)
Step Three – Building the Pipeline
We use a pipeline to ensure we can easily feed consistent data to the model. The idea is to feed raw data into the pipeline so it can perform the operations. For example, with our dataset, the continuous variables need to be standardized and the categorical data needs to be converted. Any operation can be done in a pipeline so, for example, if your dataset contains NA’s, you can replace them with the median or the mean and new variables can be created too.
Obviously, you can choose to hardcode both these processes but, over time, that will lead to inconsistencies and data leakage. The best way is to create a pipeline:
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler, OneHotEncoder, LabelEncoder
from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer, make_column_transformer
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
The pipeline will perform two operations before feeding the logistic classifier:
Standardize the variable: `StandardScaler()``
Convert the categorical features: OneHotEncoder(sparse=False)
The two steps can be done using the make_column_transformer function. It is worth telling you now that you cannot get this function to perform onehotencoder or labelencoder in the pipeline with the older version of Scikit-Learn you removed; this is why we prefer using the developer version.
You will find make_column_transfer dead simple to use. All you need to do is define the columns that need transforming and what transformation to apply. For example, if you want the continuous standardized, you would do this:
conti_features, StandardScaler() inside make_column_transformer.
conti_features: list with the continuous variable
StandardScaler: standardize the variable
The object OneHotEncoder inside make_column_transformer automatically encodes the label.
preprocess = make_column_transformer(
(conti_features, StandardScaler()),
### Must be numeric not string to specify column name
(categorical_features, OneHotEncoder(sparse=False))
)
And to make sure the pipeline works, we use fit_transform. You should get an output of 26048, 107 – this is the shape of the data:
preprocess.fit_transform(X_train).shape
(26048, 107)​
You can now use the data transformer. The pipeline is created using make_pipeline and, once you have transformed the data, you can feed it to the logistic regression:
model = make_pipeline(
preprocess,
LogisticRegression())
Using Scikit-Learn to train models is trivial; all you need is the pipeline or model, followed by the fit object. The accuracy can be printed using the score object, found in Scikit-Learn:
model.fit(X_train, y_train)
print("logistic regression score: %f" % model.score(X_test, y_test))
logistic regression score: 0.850891​
Lastly, we can use predict_proba to predict the classes and return each class’s probability. Be aware that it will sum to one:
model.predict_proba(X_test)​
array([[0.83576663, 0.16423337],
[0.94582765, 0.05417235],
[0.64760587, 0.35239413],
...,
[0.99639252, 0.00360748],
[0.02072181, 0.97927819],
[0.56781353, 0.43218647]])
Step Four – Using the Pipeline in Grid Searches
Hyperparameters are variables used for determining the network structure, such as the hidden units and these need to be tuned but doing so can take a lot of time and effort.  We could evaluate our model by changing the training dataset size and evaluating how it performs and you could do that ten times and average the score metrics. That, too, takes a lot of time. With Scikit-Learn, you get an easy way, a function that helps you tune the parameters and do cross-validation.
  • Cross-Validation – with cross-validation, the training dataset is split into folds and the model evaluated. You have to specify the number of folds and how many times the model gets evaluated. For example, if you set cross-validation to 10, the training set will be evaluated ten times and, after each time, a random nine-fold is chosen to train the model – the 10th fold is for the evaluation.
  • Grid Search – every classifier has hyperparameters that need tuning and you can try with different values but the easiest way is setting a parameter grid. Simply choose which parameter you want to be tuned; for example, there are multiple parameters in the Scikit-Learn logistic classifier. Choosing the C parameter would speed up the training as it controls the regularization parameter. The value should be a positive one and the smaller the value, the more weight the regularizer has.
To create a grid search, we use the GridSearchCD object and create a dictionary that has all the hyperparameters that need tuning. The hyperparameters are listed with the values we want to try. The C parameter, for example, would be tuned with this:
'logisticregression__C': [0.1, 1.0, 1.0]: The classifier name in lower case and two underscores followed by the parameter.
The model will now try the values of 0.001, 0.01, 0.1 and 1
The model is trained with 10 folds
from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV
# Construct parameter grid
param_grid = {
'logisticregression__C': [0.001, 0.01,0.1, 1.0],
}
The model is trained with GridSearchCV, using two parameters – cv and gri.
# Train model
grid_clf = GridSearchCV(model,
param_grid,
cv=10,
iid=False)
grid_clf.fit(X_train, y_train)
OUTPUT
GridSearchCV(cv=10, error_score='raise-deprecating',
estimator=Pipeline(memory=None,
steps=[('columntransformer', ColumnTransformer(n_jobs=1, remainder='drop', transformer_weights=None,
transformers=[('standardscaler', StandardScaler(copy=True, with_mean=True, with_std=True), [0, 2, 10, 4, 11, 12]), ('onehotencoder', OneHotEncoder(categorical_features=None, categories=None,...ty='l2', random_state=None, solver='liblinear', tol=0.0001,
verbose=0, warm_start=False))]),
fit_params=None, iid=False, n_jobs=1,
param_grid={'logisticregression__C': [0.001, 0.01, 0.1, 1.0]},
pre_dispatch='2*n_jobs', refit=True, return_train_score='warn',
scoring=None, verbose=0)
To access the best parameters, you use best_params_
grid_clf.best_params_
OUTPUT
{'logisticregression__C': 1.0}
Once the model is trained on four regularization values, we get the optimal value:
print("best logistic regression from grid search: %f" % grid_clf.best_estimator_.score(X_test, y_test))
best logistic regression from grid search: 0.850891
And to get the predicted probabilities:
grid_clf.best_estimator_.predict_proba(X_test)​              
array([[0.83576677, 0.16423323],
[0.9458291 , 0.0541709 ],
[0.64760416, 0.35239584],
...,
[0.99639224, 0.00360776],
[0.02072033, 0.97927967],
[0.56782222, 0.43217778]])

XGBoost Model
Now we’re going to have a go at training the XGBoost classifier, one of the very best and a definite improvement over the random forest classifier. I won’t go into detail about the classifier because that is outside the scope of this book. I will ask you to keep in mind that XGBoost has won lots of competitions, particularly the Kaggle competitions. On average-sized datasets, it is just as good, if not better, than many of the deep learning algorithms.
However, I will say that XGBoost is somewhat challenging to tune because of the sheer number of parameters that need tuning. Of course, GridSearchCV can always pick the parameters for you.
We want to use another better way of finding the best parameters. While GridSearchCV works, it is time-consuming, especially where multiple values have been passed. The more parameters there are, the bigger the search space and the better solution lies in using RandomizedSearchCV. This method involves choosing each hyperparameter’s value randomly after an iteration. For example,  if we trained the classifier over, say, 1000 iterations, 1000 combinations would be evaluated. It works like GridSearchCV but is better for our purposes.
First things first, let’s import xgboost. If you don’t already have it, you can use pip to install it or do this:
use import sys
!{sys.executable} -m pip install xgboost
Next, in the Jupyter environment, we do this:
import xgboost
from sklearn.model_selection import RandomizedSearchCV
from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold
Now we must specify which parameters we want tuning. The official documentation tells you all of the parameters that can be tunes, but we are only going to choose two that have two values for this tutorial. XGBoost is time-consuming to train so the more hyperparameters you put in the grid, the longer it is going to take:
params = {
'xgbclassifier__gamma': [0.5, 1],
'xgbclassifier__max_depth': [3, 4]
}
Next, the XGBoost classifier is used to construct a pipeline, defining 600 estimators. It’s worth knowing that n_estimators is a tunable hyperparameter. Too high a value and you run the risk of over-fitting the models. Do try different values but don’t forget that it can take a long time. The default values are used for the rest of the parameters:
model_xgb = make_pipeline(
preprocess,
xgboost.XGBClassifier(
n_estimators=600,
objective='binary:logistic',
silent=True,
nthread=1)
)
The cross-validation can be significantly improved if we use the Stratified K-Folds cross validator. With this one, we speed up the computation by only constructing three folds but the trade-off is lower quality. If you want better quality results, the value could be increased to 5 or 10.
We’re going to train our model over four iterations:
skf = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=3,
shuffle = True,
random_state = 1001)
random_search = RandomizedSearchCV(model_xgb,
param_distributions=params,
n_iter=4,
scoring='accuracy',
n_jobs=4,
cv=skf.split(X_train, y_train),
verbose=3,
random_state=1001)
Now the randomized search is ready, the model can be trained:
#grid_xgb = GridSearchCV(model_xgb, params, cv=10, iid=False)
random_search.fit(X_train, y_train)

Using the MLPClassifier to Create a DNN
Lastly, we can use Scikit-Learn for training deep learning algorithms. This method works the same as the other classifiers and can be found at MLPClassifier:
from sklearn.neural_network import MLPClassifier
The deep learning algorithm below is now defined:
  • Adam solver
  • Relu activation function
  • Alpha = 0.0001
  • batch size of 150
  • Two hidden layers containing 100 and 50 neurons respectively
model_dnn = make_pipeline(
preprocess,
MLPClassifier(solver='adam',
alpha=0.0001,
activation='relu',
batch_size=150,
hidden_layer_sizes=(200, 100) ,
random_state=1))
The number of layers can be changed to improve how the model works”
model_dnn.fit(X_train, y_train)
print("DNN regression score: %f" % model_dnn.score(X_test, y_test))
DNN regression score: 0.821253
Using LIME to Trust Your Model
You have a good performing model now so you need to be able to trust it and for that, you need the right tool. Thanks to three intrepid researchers, we have an algorithm  called Local Interpretable Model-Agnostic Explanations, or LIME for short.
For example, you don’t always know if you can trust the predictions your machine learning model provides. In the same way, a doctor won’t necessarily trust a specific diagnosis just because a computer told him that was what it was. You need to know that your model is trusted before you can put it to use.
Just think, if we could understand exactly why a classifier made a specific prediction, even the most complex models like the neural networks, SVMs, Random Forests, any that have a kernel, will become much easier to trust. Going back to our doctor and his computer making the diagnoses, if he had a computer model that told him the essential symptoms for a disease, he would be more likely to place some trust in it.
That’s where LIME steps in as it can tell you which features affect the classifier’s decisions.
Preparing the Data
Running LIME with Python requires a couple of changes, not least, installing it on the terminal:
pip install lime
LIME needs to approximate the model locally and it does this using an object called LimeTabularExplainer. The object needs the following:
  • A dataset formatted to NumPy
  • The feature names (feature_name)
  • The class names (class_names)
  • The column index for the categorical features (categorical_features)
  • The group name for each categorical feature (categorical_names)
Create the NumPy Training Set
Copying and converting df_train to NumPy from Panda is simple:
df_train.head(5)
# Create numpy data
df_lime = df_train
df_lime.head(3)
Getting the Class Name
We use the unique() object for this and you should see:
  • '<=50K'
  • '>50K'
# Get the class name
class_names = df_lime.label.unique()
class_names
array(['<=50K', '>50K'], dtype=object)
Getting the Categorical Feature Column Index
The method we used for getting the group names can be used here. LabelEncoder is used to encode the label and the operation is repeated on each categorical feature:
##
import sklearn.preprocessing as preprocessing
categorical_names = {}
for feature in CATE_FEATURES:
le = preprocessing.LabelEncoder()
le.fit(df_lime[feature])
df_lime[feature] = le.transform(df_lime[feature])
categorical_names[feature] = le.classes_
print(categorical_names)

import numpy as np

The explainer can be used by calling explain_instance – this will tell you why the model made the prediction:
exp = explainer.explain_instance(X_test_lime[i], predict_fn, num_features=6)
exp.show_in_notebook(show_all=False)
The output shows you the correct prediction so, what can we say about this?
First, we can say that the classifier wasn’t overly certain about the probabilities being predicted. The predictions state a probability of 64% that the household has an income of more than 50k. That 64% comes from two features – marital and capital gain. You will see two colors on the output – blue and orange. Blue is a negative contribution to the positive class while orange is a positive contribution.
Why is the classifier so confused? This is because we have null as a value for the household’s capital gain and this is one of the best wealth predictor features. Add to that the fact that the household working hours are less than 40 and it explains some of the confusion. The features that affect the classifier positively are occupation, age and sex.
If the householder were single we would have got a prediction of less than 50k – 0.64-0.18=0.46

The overall pattern dictates that the income would be less than 50k, as the classifier explains.

Building a Random Forest Model
To Predict Wine Quality
This is our final Scikit-Learn tutorial. We’ll be using the library to build the random forest supervised learning model and tune it to predict wine quality, based on features such as residual sugar, acidity, and alcohol concentration, and so on.
Here’s what we will cover:
  1. Setting up your environment
  2. Importing the required libraries and modules
  3. Loading the red wine data
  4. Splitting the dataset into train and test sets
  5. The data pre-processing steps
  6. Declaring the hyperparameters, we want to tune        
  7. Using a cross-validation pipeline to tune the model
  8. Refitting the data on the whole training dataset
  9. Using the test data to evaluate the model pipeline
Lastly, we save the model so we can use it later and, at the end, I will give you the entire code, from start to finish. Let’s get on.
Step One – Setting Up Your Environment
The first step is to ensure that you have these on your computer:
  • Python 2.7+ or Python 3+
  • Pandas
  • NumPy
  • Scikit-Learn (otherwise called sklearn)
If you haven’t already got all of this, install Anaconda as it comes with all those required packaged pre-installed.
If any of your packages require an update simply type the following, with the relevant package name, at the command prompt:
$ conda update <package name>
To confirm that you scikit-learn properly installed on your system, type the following:
$ python -c "import sklearn; print sklearn.__version__"
0.18.1
Your version number may be different.
Now create a new file and call it sklearn_ml_example.py
Step Two – Importing the Required Libraries and Modules
First, we should import NumPy as this gives us great support for numerical computations:
import numpy as np
Next, Pandas, one of the best libraries with support for data frames. Technically, this is an optional install because Scikit-Learn has the ability to deal with numerical matrices, but it does make things easier for us:
import pandas as pd
Now, we can import the functions we need. First, we want the function called train_test_split(), which is found in the module called model_selection. The name says it all – the module has loads of utilities that help us choose the right model:
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
Next, we want the pre-processing module, all of it, not just a couple of functions. This module has everything we need to scale the data, transform it, and wrangle it.
from sklearn import preprocessing
Now we want some families of models and these differ from actual models. Families are broad model types, like the SVMs, random forests, linear regression, etc. Once the parameters have been fit and tuned to the data, each family gives us an actual model. Don’t worry about this at the moment; it will all become clear later in the tutorial.
We want the random forest family:
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestRegressor
The next step involves importing the tools we need to do the cross-validation:
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV
Now some metrics for evaluating how the model performs:
from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error, r2_score
And lastly, we want to be able to persist the model so we can use it in the future:
from sklearn.externals import joblib
If you are wondering what joblib is, it is an efficient way of storing the bigger NumPy arrays and is an alternative to pickle in Python.

dataset_url = 'http://mlr.cs.umass.edu/ml/machine-learning-databases/wine-quality/winequality-red.csv'
data = pd.read_csv(dataset_url)
